# Sources

One entry per source: what it is, where to obtain it, and **what fixes one item to one year**. That
last clause is the whole of the evidence argument, so it is stated per source rather than in a
preamble, and a source with no per-item date is not admitted whatever else it offers.

Paths are relative to the repository root. Every ingest command assumes the file has been placed at
the path shown.

Sources evaluated and closed are here too, each with the single measurement that closed it, so the
same ground is not broken twice. [discovery.md](discovery.md) is the method used to price one before
building a collector.

**Where a figure appears below it is the measurement a source was admitted or rejected on, at the
date given, not a current count.** A source re-measured later against a larger store reads lower, so
the two are different quantities and both are kept. The authoritative current counts are in
`audit/source_contribution.csv`, which `ark export` rewrites from the store on every run and whose
`netnew_pairs` column sums to the report's headline increment. A hand-copied snapshot once lived here
claiming to be generated, and by the time anyone checked it had omitted the round's largest
contributor entirely.

---

## `prior_task`: the supplied baseline

The six annual files supplied with the task, 8,224,963 hostname lines. In the delivery archive at
`baseline/original/`; the baseline additions are scored against sits at `baseline/<release>/`, named
by `CURRENT_BASELINE_MARKER` in `src/ark/baseline.py`.

```bash
cp -R <archive>/baseline/original legacy-data
uv run ark ingest-legacy --legacy-dir legacy-data --marker-prefix original
```

Both flags are required, or the six files are skipped as already ingested.

**Dating: the file a line appears in is its year. No inference.**

`prior_reused`. Excluded from the scored metric, being baseline rather than addition.

---

## `isc_survey`: Internet Domain Survey host lists

The Network Wizards / ISC twice-yearly DNS walk: five intact `.domains` lists for 1996-1997 plus 583
per-TLD host files. ISC's own copies fail their gzip integrity check, so these come from a 1996
Wayback crawl of `nw.com` and the survey author's live site.

```bash
mkdir -p data/raw/isc_survey && cd data/raw/isc_survey
curl -O http://web.archive.org/web/19961112163532id_/http://nw.com:80/zone/9507.domains.gz
curl -O http://web.archive.org/web/19961112163635id_/http://nw.com:80/zone/9601.domains.gz
curl -O http://web.archive.org/web/19961112163826id_/http://nw.com:80/zone/9607.domains.gz
curl -O http://3waylabs.com/zone/9707.domains.gz
cd - && uv run ark ingest isc_survey data/raw/isc_survey/*.gz
```

```bash
uv run python scripts/fetch_nw_host_files.py   # resumable, three connections, ~2h for 116 MB
uv run ark ingest isc_survey data/raw/isc_survey/*.gz
```

**Dating: the survey date is the `YYMM` code in the filename (`9607` = July 1996), and every host in
that file was observed in DNS on that date, so the file's provenance fixes the year for all of its
lines.**

`artifact_listing`. 42,299 net-new pairs, **14,956.3877 equivalent-English**, in the two years the
Internet Archive cannot supply in bulk: **the best 1996-1997 source in the project.** Hard caps: no
`com.gz` and no `edu.gz` in any edition, and the `.domains` lists stop at 9707.

---

## `afnic_fr`: `.fr` registry open data

The monthly `.fr` open-data file, one row per domain. Open licence, attribution only.

```bash
mkdir -p data/raw/afnic && cd data/raw/afnic
# from https://opendata.afnic.fr/ download the current "A" file (Noms de domaine en .fr)
unzip '*_OPENDATA_A.zip'
cd - && uv run ark ingest afnic_fr data/raw/afnic/*NomsDeDomaineEnPointFr.csv
```

**Dating: the row's registry-written creation date and permanent-deletion date (blank while
registered). AFNIC's Technical Integration Guide v3.0 (27 February 2015) states `<domain:crDate>` is
"the last creation date of the domain name" or the date of the last transmission, and both events
fall after any prior deletion, so `[crDate, deletion-or-now]` contains no deletion event: it is
continuous by construction.**
<https://www.afnic.fr/medias/documents/technique/integration-guide-en-2015-02-27.pdf>

`whois_creation`, master for every in-window year the interval covers; each row stores its interval
verbatim (`registered 16-03-1999..active`).

`crDate` can only be later than the true first registration, so the tranche undercounts and cannot
overcount. Omits names deleted before 28 January 2014. Republished monthly, so unpinnable; this
delivery used the June 2026 edition.

---

## `ukwa_link_source` and `ukwa_link_target`: UK Web Archive host link graph

The JISC UK Web Domain Dataset host link graph 1996-2010, rows of
`year|source_host|target_host<TAB>count`. Wayback only: the original address answers HTTP 200 with a
159-byte stub and the DOI is dead. The archived stream drops partway, giving 2 GiB of the advertised
20.9 GB.

**The file is not year-sorted.** The year column decreases 14 times, so it is 15 concatenated shards
each sorted internally, with **2,468,674** in-window rows spread across all of them; a parser must
not stop at the first post-2001 row.

```bash
mkdir -p data/raw/ukwa && cd data/raw/ukwa
curl -L -o host-linkage.tsv.gz \
  "https://web.archive.org/web/2019id_/https://www.webarchive.org.uk/datasets/ukwa.ds.2/linkage/host-linkage.tsv.gz"
cd -
uv run ark ingest ukwa_link_source data/raw/ukwa/host-linkage.tsv.gz
uv run ark ingest ukwa_link_target data/raw/ukwa/host-linkage.tsv.gz
```

**Dating: the year column of each row, the crawl year that observed the link.**

The source host was crawled successfully that year to produce the row, so `link_source` is
master-eligible; the target was merely linked to, so `link_target` is candidate-only. One file,
ingested twice. `.uk`-weighted.

---

## `arquivo_ia` and `arquivo_roteiro`: Arquivo.pt capture indexes

Two CDXJ indexes from the Portuguese web archive: `IA.cdxj`, 47 GB donated by the Internet Archive,
covering 1996-2007, and the smaller early-Portuguese-web `Roteiro.cdxj`.

```bash
mkdir -p data/raw/arquivo && cd data/raw/arquivo
curl -C - -O https://arquivo.pt/datasets/cdxj/IA.cdxj
curl -C - -O https://arquivo.pt/datasets/cdxj/Roteiro.cdxj
cd -
uv run ark ingest arquivo_ia data/raw/arquivo/IA.cdxj
uv run ark ingest arquivo_roteiro data/raw/arquivo/Roteiro.cdxj
```

**Dating: the 14-digit crawler-written capture timestamp on each line.**

`cdx_timestamp`, a capture with an in-year timestamp and HTTP 200. Portuguese-web weighted; skipping
both costs 17,696 pairs over 7,001 domains.

---

## `odp`: Open Directory Project (DMOZ) RDF content dumps

Three surviving dumps: a truncated prefix of the August 2000 full dump and two complete 2001
Kids-and-Teens dumps. The live URLs serve a stub, so these come from Wayback.

```bash
curl -s "https://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=dmoz.org/rdf/*&from=2000&to=2001&filter=statuscode:200&fl=timestamp,original"
mkdir -p data/raw/odp
# then, for each capture of interest:
curl -o data/raw/odp/c2000.gz "https://web.archive.org/web/<timestamp>id_/http://dmoz.org/rdf/content.rdf.u8.gz"
uv run ark ingest odp data/raw/odp/*.gz
```

**Dating: the dump's own generation stamp, corroborated by the Wayback capture timestamp and the
filename (`c2000` = 2000, `kt200106` = June 2001).**

`artifact_listing`: a dated data file, not an undated directory page, so every catalogued external
URL inside it is a line in that file and the file's date fixes the year.

The August 2000 full dump is unrecoverable, Wayback holding only that year's `structure.rdf`, which
carries no external links. The 2001 full content dumps are not retrievable.

---

## `early_web_cdx`: Internet Archive Early Web CDX dataset

A published CDX dataset of early-web captures, 224 gzipped index files.

```bash
uvx --from internetarchive ia download early-web_cdx-lang-cdxa \
    --glob='*.cdx.gz' --destdir=data/raw/early_web --no-directories
uv run ark ingest early_web data/raw/early_web/*.cdx.gz
```

Item: <https://archive.org/details/early-web_cdx-lang-cdxa>

**Dating: the 14-digit capture timestamp on each line.**

`cdx_timestamp`. It overlaps the supplied baseline almost completely, itself derived from the same
archive, so its 2.28M evidence rows serve as corroboration.

---


## `ia_cdx_bulk`: Wayback CDX verification engine

A query engine, not a file: one collapsed CDX query per domain covering all six years, run against domains missing a year they are bracketed by. Endpoint <https://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx>; the response journals ship under `journals/`, so ingest replays offline.

```bash
uv run ark gaps                                             # choose targets
uv run ark cdx data/raw/cdx/gap_candidates.txt --workers 8  # query, writes a journal
uv run ark ingest cdx_snapshot data/raw/cdx/cdx_*.jsonl.gz  # journal -> evidence
```

**Dating: the 14-digit capture timestamps the archive returns for that domain, written by the crawler at capture time, collapsed to distinct years client-side.**

Evidence type: `cdx_timestamp`.

## `dartmouth_nber_captures`: the archive's own capture census

A 2017 research release publishing, for every host the Wayback Machine then held, captures per calendar year; 228 MB of `host<TAB>year<TAB>count`. From archive.org item `DARTMOUTH-NBER-RESEARCH-2017-metadata`, which no longer serves. The input does not ship:

```bash
uv run ark ingest dartmouth_nber_captures data/raw/dartmouth_nber/domain-year-captures.txt
```

**Dating: a row states the archive holds N captures of that host inside that calendar year, the same machine-counted fact a CDX query returns, so it dates that year and no other.**

**Evidence type: `cdx_timestamp`.** Self-dating, no corroboration split. Agrees with our own CDX querying on **138,760 (domain, year) pairs**. Yield **227,273 net-new pairs, 142,084.0 equivalent-English**. Approved `master` by Ivo 2026-08-17.

## `domain_creation_bulk`: published registry creation dates in bulk

A published WHOIS/DNS compilation of 171 million domains, each carrying the registry's own creation date parsed from a port-43 answer. 25.9 GB semicolon-separated CSV, fetched with the Kaggle CLI: `kaggle datasets download -d wotschofsky/171-million-domain-names-whois-dns-dnssec`.

```bash
uv run ark ingest domain_creation_bulk data/raw/domain_creation/domains.csv
```

**Dating: the registry-written Creation Date field in the record. A creation date in 1998 writes 1998 and no other year, because the parser emits one evidence row for one year.** Falsification: of 21,698 in-window rows in the six TLDs delegated in 2001, **zero** predate 2001.

**Evidence type: `whois_creation`.** Filed under the `registry` lineage, so it cannot corroborate our own `rdap` sweeps. Yield **2,165,523 net-new pairs, 1,241,812.0 equivalent-English**. Approved `master` by Ivo 2026-08-17.


## `rdap` and `rdap_snapshot`: registry creation dates

Registry RDAP lookups, direct to the authoritative registry resolved per TLD from the IANA bootstrap,
`rdap.org` fallback only. `rdap_snapshot` is the journalled path, `rdap` an earlier tranche.

```bash
uv run ark gaps --creation --out data/raw/rdap/creation_candidates.txt
uv run ark rdap data/raw/rdap/creation_candidates.txt
uv run ark ingest rdap_snapshot data/raw/rdap/rdap_*.jsonl.gz
```

```bash
uv run python scripts/build_rdap_pool_list.py --tlds com,net --limit 1400000 \
    --out data/raw/rdap/pool_targets_verisign.txt
bash scripts/rdap_pool_sweep.sh 6 100000 32
uv run ark ingest rdap_snapshot data/raw/rdap/rdap_pool_*.jsonl.gz
```

Bootstrap <https://data.iana.org/rdap/dns.json>, fallback <https://rdap.org/>

**Dating: the `registration` event date, a registry-written timestamp in the response. RDAP gives the
current state plus that one historical timestamp, no registration history.**

`whois_creation`, creation year only: it does not establish registration in a later year. Dated
outside 1996-2001, the name stays a candidate.

| closed here | what closed it | reopen |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PIR `.org` | 403 for 9,253 consecutive requests after ~850 answers | slow probe, stop on first refusal |
| Nominet `.uk` | three refusals in the first fourteen queries at 0.5 q/s | |
| auDA `.au` | namespace re-registered 2002, dates stamped with the migration | |
| `.gov` pool | pool-to-dated ratio 182.0 against `.com` 0.3, names invented | |

---

## `page_directory` and `page_expansion`: archived curated directory pages

Wayback captures of curated catalogue pages, read for the sites they list. Seeds under
`seeds/expansion/`; primary catalogue the WWW Virtual Library, <http://vlib.org/>

```bash
uv run ark download seeds/expansion/seeds_round2.txt --out data/raw/expand/round2/expand_round2.jsonl.gz
uv run python scripts/split_expansion_journal.py data/raw/expand/round2/expand_round2.jsonl.gz --write
uv run ark ingest expansion_directory data/raw/expand/round2/*_corroborated.jsonl.gz --round 2
uv run ark ingest expansion_links     data/raw/expand/round2/*_unverified.jsonl.gz --round 2
```

**Dating: the crawler-stamped Wayback capture timestamp of the page. A listing dated 1998 evidences
its entries for 1998 only.**

`dated_directory` for a page recorded as a curated catalogue. Every other name, and any name no other
source attests, carries `link_target` under `page_expansion` and is candidate-only, a listing being a
claim by the linking page.

Rejected: the 1996-1997 Yahoo tree under `www.yahoo.com/<Category>/`, 11 pairs and 7.7295 EE from 55
archive requests.

---

## `internet_scout`: Internet Scout Report archive

Weekly curated review of scholarly, government and educational sites, harvested by OAI-PMH from
<https://archives.internetscout.org/OAI>.

```bash
mkdir -p data/raw/scout
curl -A "Mozilla/5.0" \
  "https://archives.internetscout.org/OAI?verb=ListRecords&metadataPrefix=oai_dc" \
  >> data/raw/scout/scout_oai.xml
# then repeat with &resumptionToken=<token from the previous page> until none is returned
uv run ark ingest internet_scout data/raw/scout/scout_oai.xml
```

**Dating: `dc:date` on each record gives the issue year and `dc:identifier` the reviewed URL, archive
metadata fields, not prose.**

`dated_directory`. Scholarly and US-weighted; the live feed cannot be hash-pinned.

---

## `ncsa_whats_new`: NCSA "What's New" announcement pages

Dated issues of the era's list of newly launched sites, from Wayback captures of the NCSA Mosaic site.
The only surviving 1996 editorial directory artifact here.

```bash
curl -s "https://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=ncsa.uiuc.edu/SDG/Software/Mosaic/Docs/whats-new*&from=1996&to=1996&filter=statuscode:200&fl=timestamp,original"
# fetch each monthly issue with the id_ modifier, then extract the announced entries
uv run ark ingest ncsa_whats_new data/raw/ncsa-whats-new/ncsa_1996_domain_date_pairs.tsv
```

**Dating: the issue date carried by the page holding the entry. Every row is 1996.**

`dated_directory`. Navigation and masthead links are excluded. One of the 4,916 names is attested by
no other source.

---

## `ia_cdx`: per-year CDX verification (superseded)

Kept only so its 11 rows stay attributable. Superseded by the collapsed six-year query in
`ia_cdx_bulk`.

---

## NYPW first-capture index: rejected

IA's "Not Your Parents' Web" first-capture index (`https://archive.org/details/nypw_urls_CDXfirstentry`)
yields **60 net-new pairs over 53 net-new domains** from 2,413,003 in-window pairs, 99.998% overlap
with the same IA CDX the baseline drains. The 19.35 GB `nypw_timemaps` sibling samples that URL
universe too.

## Australian Web Archive: rejected, endpoint correction kept

`https://webarchive.nla.gov.au/awa/cdx` returns an Anubis challenge, but
**`https://web.archive.org.au/awa/cdx` answers normally**, a pywb server supporting `url`,
`matchType=domain`, `from`/`to`, `limit`, `collapse`, `output=json`. It is a lookup API, not a bulk
dump. Paired with the PANDORA titles list
(`https://github.com/GLAM-Workbench/trove-web-archives-titles`, CC0) it gives 35,391 registered
domains, 29,595 in no annual file, but a random 60-domain sample returned **60 answers, 0 transport
failures and 0 with any in-window capture**.

## Source names that are not separate sources

`cdx_snapshot` writes under `ia_cdx_bulk`, `rdap_snapshot` under `rdap_snapshot`, `early_web` under
`early_web_cdx`, `expansion_directory` and `expansion_links` under `page_directory` and
`page_expansion`. `deduplicated_urls_2001-2002` and `mid_slice` are candidate-only names with zero
evidence rows, retained so earlier seeding runs stay attributable.

---


## `trade_press` and `trade_press_mention`: scanned computer magazines

archive.org's scanned, OCR'd computer press, via `archive.org/advancedsearch.php` then
`archive.org/download/<id>/<id>_djvu.txt`. Hobbyist corpus `collection:computermagazines OR
collection:byte-magazine OR boardwatch`, 4,030 in-window items; American `collection:computerworld
OR collection:pub_computerworld OR collection:applemagazines OR (identifier:bub_gb* AND
(title:infoworld OR title:"network world" OR title:computerworld OR title:"pc mag"))`, 1,288.

**Dating: the item's `year` metadata field, the publication date of the issue. No inference.**

`dated_directory` after the corroboration split, because OCR fabricates hostnames: names seen only
here go to the candidate pool. Lineage `trade_press`. Yield 1,590 pairs, 1,004.33
equivalent-English.

```bash
uv run python scripts/collect_trade_press.py --discover     # collection sizes, run this first
uv run python scripts/collect_trade_press.py --limit 5000 --query "$HOBBYIST"   # first corpus
uv run python scripts/split_trade_press.py --write
uv run ark ingest tradepress_dated      data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_dated.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest tradepress_candidates data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_candidates.jsonl.gz

uv run python scripts/collect_trade_press.py --limit 1400   # second corpus, now the default
uv run python scripts/split_trade_press.py \
    --journal data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_20260808T172417Z.jsonl.gz --tag american --write
uv run ark ingest tradepress_dated      data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_dated_american.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest tradepress_candidates data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_candidates_american.jsonl.gz
```

The extractor also reads bare `foo.com`, `http://foo.com/` and `bob@foo.com`; re-reading cached OCR
under it sends no request:

```bash
uv run python scripts/reextract_trade_press.py --write
uv run python scripts/split_trade_press.py \
    --journal data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_reextract_<stamp>.jsonl.gz --tag reextract --write
uv run ark ingest tradepress_dated      data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_dated_reextract.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest tradepress_candidates data/raw/tradepress/tradepress_candidates_reextract.jsonl.gz
```

| closed | the fact that closed it |
| --- | --- |
| widening the query | `--discover` finds no `pub_infoworld`, `pub_network-world`, `pub_pc-week`, `pub_internet-world`, `pub_cio`, `pub_web-techniques`, `drdobbs`, `linuxjournal`, `maccompendium`, `boardwatch`, `pcmag`, `wired-magazine` or `internet-magazines` collection, and no `sim_*` microfilm run of a computing title except Computerworld |
| `magazine_rack` | 34,288 items, 0.4 net-new pairs each |
| `folkscanomy_computer` | 519 items, 36 of 40 unreachable |
| `sim_microfilm` at large | 57,245 in-window items, but a 1,500-item sample is journals, gazettes and "Table of Contents" stubs |

---

## `usenet_address` and `usenet_address_mention`: the addresses the extractor never read

The 19,083 already-ingested Usenet archives at `data/raw/usenet/*.mbox.zip`, re-read for `ftp://`
hosts, `mailto:` links and typed body addresses. **Dating: the posting date of the message carrying
the address, from the message header, identical to `usenet_announce`.** `dated_directory` after the
corroboration split, `link_target` otherwise. Lineage `usenet`: a post and an address inside it are
one observation. Yield 102,577 pairs, 62,820.7 equivalent-English.

```bash
uv run python scripts/collect_usenet_addresses.py --workers 10
uv run python scripts/split_usenet_addresses.py --write
uv run ark ingest usenet_addr_dated      data/raw/usenet_addr/usenet_addr_dated.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest usenet_addr_candidates data/raw/usenet_addr/usenet_addr_candidates.jsonl.gz
```

Closed: a generic dot-rule token scan of the same text gave 354 net-new tokens worth at most 193
equivalent-English, contaminated (`ads.my`, `lol.ie`). Closed: mining `.edu` from the pool, since
these extractors supply 213,703 of its 216,185 `.edu` names and they are anti-harvester munged
addresses, CDX hit rate 0.003 over 1,709 answers.

---

## `usenet_bare` and `usenet_bare_mention`: the bare `foo.com` in the message bodies

The same 19,231 archives read a third time for a plain `foo.com` in prose, no scheme, no `www.`, no
`@`. No network. **Dating: the posting date of the message, identical to `usenet_announce`.**
`dated_directory` after the corroboration split, `link_target` otherwise; 36.3% of rows were
uncorroborated and went to the pool, so a company or file name asserts nothing. Lineage `usenet`.
Guards in `ark.usenet` are unit-pinned: TLD allowlist, lookbehind, lookahead, all-digits rule, and
body text only, never `Path:`, `Xref:` or `Newsgroups:`. Yield 42,139 pairs, 28,460.3
equivalent-English.

```bash
uv run python scripts/collect_usenet_bare.py --sample 400 --workers 8    # project first
uv run python scripts/project_usenet_bare.py --journal data/raw/usenet_bare/<file> --archives 400
just usenet-bare                                                        # or the whole corpus
```

Limitation: 1,200 of the 42,139 pairs come from `comp.mail.maps` or `can.uucp.maps`, which
`ark.uucp` already parses under the `registry` lineage, so one posting can be read twice; every
evidence row names its group.

Coverage gap, measured 2026-08-30 by matching every `group` field in the journals against the
archives on disk: **9,050 of the 16,846 archives, 29.96 GB, are named in no `usenet_bare` journal at
all**, the largest being `alt.sex.erotica` 1.53 GB, `free.ucp` 674 MB, `us.politics` 646 MB and
`linux.kernel` 449 MB. That is unread bytes in an existing pipeline, not a new source, and it is the
cheapest unclaimed work in the register: no request, no terms, no new evidence class.

---


## `uucp_map_registry`, `uucp_map_creation`, `uucp_map_mention`: the UUCP maps

UUCP maps posted to `comp.mail.maps`, the `.CA` portion machine-generated from the Canadian domain
registry. On disk at `data/raw/usenet/comp.mail.maps.mbox.zip` (205,143,394 bytes), identical to
`https://archive.org/download/usenet-comp/comp.mail.maps.mbox.zip`.

```bash
uv run python scripts/split_uucp_maps.py --write
uv run ark ingest uucp_listing  data/raw/uucp/uucp_listing.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest uucp_creation data/raw/uucp/uucp_creation.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest uucp_mentions data/raw/uucp/uucp_mentions.jsonl.gz
```

**Dating.** A registry-generated file declares its own provenance (`#R Automatically generated from a
.CA domain registration form`) and is regenerated from the live registration database at posting time,
so it takes the posting date: all 8,309 in-window registry postings carry an internal generation stamp
in the same year as their `Date:` header.

`artifact_listing` (posting date), `whois_creation` (registrar's `approved:`/`received:` date), lineage
`registry`. Hand-maintained maps are `link_target`, candidate-only.

---

## `rtfm_faq` and `rtfm_faq_mention`: the Usenet FAQ mirror

The `rtfm.mit.edu` FTP mirror, 19,478 FAQ documents under `pub/usenet-by-group`, at
`https://archive.org/download/ftp_rtfm.mit.edu_2014.07/2014.07.rtfm.mit.edu.tar` (1,691,248,640
bytes); the live host refuses connections.

```bash
tar -xf 2014.07.rtfm.mit.edu.tar -C data/raw/rtfm rtfm.mit.edu/pub/usenet-by-group
uv run python scripts/split_rtfm_faqs.py --write
uv run ark ingest rtfm_dated      data/raw/rtfm/rtfm_dated.jsonl.gz
uv run ark ingest rtfm_candidates data/raw/rtfm/rtfm_candidates.jsonl.gz
```

**Dating.** The document's own `Last-modified:` / `X-Last-Updated:` / `Version:` revision header, not
`Date:`, which is the auto-reposter's stamp on the single copy rtfm keeps: of 12,318 documents carrying
both, 6,610 disagree, 3,296 with the repost later against 4 earlier.

`dated_directory`, after the corroboration split, because these URLs are prose typed by a human.
Lineage `usenet`.

---

## `ukwa_geoindex`: the UKWA Geoindex, found served after being closed as unreachable

Geographic index of the JISC UK Web Domain Dataset: every `.uk` resource the Internet Archive held for
1996-2013, each row a 14-digit timestamp, the URL, a tab, a postcode. 11,217,295,098 bytes over 12
members, CC Public Domain Mark 1.0:

```bash
curl -L -r 0-0 https://bl.iro.bl.uk/downloads/090bbffa-d82c-4641-ba72-0089e8ef885f
```

Extracted whole by `scripts/ukwa_geoindex_pull.sh`.

**Dating.** The row's own 14-digit IA capture timestamp prefix, a machine record of the capture, so it
dates that year and no other, and nothing in the file was typed by a human.

`cdx_timestamp`, master-eligible, no corroboration split. **Admitted master 2026-08-24** at 4,591
net-new pairs, 4,493.0 EE. `bl.iro.bl.uk` robots names ClaudeBot `Disallow: /`; `/downloads/` is allowed.

| closed | closing fact |
|---|---|
| **A live web search engine used as an existence instrument, pointed at the file signatures of the families closed on ARTIFACT EXISTENCE (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 0.0 net-new post-split EE, and the instrument itself is validated and works.** It surfaced an artifact six independent existence layers had missed: `https://rscott.org/OldInternetFiles/domain-info.19960614.txt`, 9,918,513 B, the complete InterNIC gTLD register at 14 June 1996 (COM 374,462 / NET 15,452 / ORG 26,331 / EDU 2,661 / GOV 451, 417,109 distinct names), held independently a second time at `https://www.statdns.com/files/`, against a register entry that had closed the series with "Every surviving copy is 1993". What dates one item is the machine-written stamp `DDN NIC DOMAIN SUMMARY   14-Jun-96` on line 3 of the file, class `artifact_listing`. Neither host serves a robots.txt (404 on both), so both are permitted; `emaillab.jp` disallows `/pub/` and every candidate file there sits under it, so it was not fetched. PRICE, `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: 417,109 items, all in window, 417,103 distinct pairs, 417,103 already held, **0 net-new pairs, 0.0 EE**, 0 names new to the candidate pool. An independent duckdb screen sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS reads 417,101 of 417,109 held at 1996; the 8 residuals are fixed-width column-wrap junk (`concept-.com`, `comicnet..com`) or third-level names, not registrations. The store's 1996 `com` baseline, 559,053 names, is already denser than the entire mid-1996 `.com` namespace of 374,462. Other in-window files on the host are zero too, and the negatives are proved against the files' own headers, not asserted: `hosts.19960614.txt` and `hosts.19970826.txt` give 103,945 and 143,493 lines, every one `NET : addr : NETNAME :`, matching the header "contains data about IP networks only"; `edu.19991120.zone` gives 5,850 distinct `.edu` delegations, 5,850 of 5,850 already held at 1999, and is terms-blocked independently of value because lines 2 to 5 paste Network Solutions' zone-file access agreement. A first parser returned 0 on the zone through a layout bug and was re-derived before being believed. **The number that matters is the edition that does NOT survive**: the same 417,109 names on the adjacent-year screen (held Y-1, missing Y), so nothing takes the split, are worth 47,982.6 EE at 1997, 49,729.0 at 1998, 8,874.1 at 1999, 8,710.0 at 2000 and **61,033.9 at 2001** (com 56,512.3, org 2,778.6, net 1,525.7, edu 161.3, gov 56.0). The series terminates at 1996-06-14 on BOTH holders, which is the strongest available evidence that no later edition survives. METHOD, and it is the transferable part. (1) **Search the machine HEADER, not the host**: `"DDN NIC DOMAIN SUMMARY"` returned both holders as results #1 and #2, while `"domain-info.txt"` returned neither and so did six existence layers. (2) **Always run the "engine quoted it back" control before recording a zero**: the three named validation positives all returned nothing, which read as a dead instrument and was not; searching a phrase the engine had itself just quoted returned its source page as #1, proving literal retrieval works. Without that control a semantic engine's topical fallback is indistinguishable from a proved negative. (3) The 0 of 3 has a structural cause: all three positives live inside a `.tar.gz` member or in Wayback replay, and **a live engine indexes SERVED pages only**, so it is blind to archive members and dead hosts by construction. Never validate it on an archive-only artifact. (4) A frozen personal "old internet files" collection skews pre-1996, because enthusiasts collect the hosts.txt era, so expect this class to keep landing just outside the window. (5) Price the edition that does not exist before hunting it: one adjacent-year query over a surviving edition's name list priced every missing edition. This is the sharpest confirmation yet of "aim at 2001, not 1996": the most complete registry artifact of the era, 417,109 names, machine-dated, master-eligible and freely served, adds exactly zero. The untried application is method 1 run systematically over the machine headers of every artifact already banked, filtered to 2001 headers only. Do not re-test rscott.org, statdns.com, `emaillab.jp` (robots-blocked payload), the InterNIC `domain-info` series, or DNS-OARC (members only). |
| **OpenPGP keyserver user-ID binding self-signatures, at open-keyserver scale rather than on the Debian keyrings (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 0 EE on ARTIFACT EXISTENCE, and no host was contacted, because the family is already closed twice over.** `sources.md:775` closed the SKS and Hockeypuck bulk dumps on 2026-08-18 across nine hosts (dead, NXDOMAIN or 404, with `pgp.key-server.io/sks-dump/` serving a squatted 1,095-byte stub under HTTP 200 and `keys.openpgp.org` publishing no dump by design), and `sources.md:776` closed the only retrievable substitute, the curated distribution keyrings, on size at 69 net-new pairs and 44.4 EE over 4,096 items. The evidence class was never the problem: what would date one item is the binding self-signature's creation time, machine-written by the signing implementation, which `discovery.md:56-60` already separated from the key creation time over 4,225 signatures. What is new is a **zero-network pricing of what such a dump WOULD have paid**, measured read-only against the store's own era email-address population, `usenet_address`, 899,998 distinct domains sampled AS DOMAINS. Bracketed between two independently derived rates: FLOOR 0.01084 EE per binding (the Debian empirical), CEILING 0.0585 EE per binding (0.346 pairs per item x 0.898 held x 0.2979 lacks-own-year x 0.6321), so 5,000 EE needs between 85,460 and 461,261 in-window bindings. **So the family was not arithmetically dead; it dies entirely on the artifact not existing.** THE DECISIVE NUMBER, and it corrects a project-wide screen: P(store lacks 2001 | `com` held) is **0.286** on an email-address population against the store-wide 0.611 that CLAUDE.md's 2001-threshold rule assumes, a 2.1x shortfall, so an already-held `com` name dated 2001 is worth 0.181 EE here and "1,000 EE needs ~2,600 held com names" becomes ~5,500. This joins Backflip at 0.041 and the CACM/IEEE citation set at 0.044 as a third independent confirmation, with a different selector again, that **self-selected populations concentrate in the years the store already covers**. The mechanism is visible before fetching: era address populations peak 1999-2001 (355,046 / 340,409 / 336,075 in this one) and the store's `com` gap is narrowest exactly there (.209 / .093 / .286), so the years an address corpus supplies most are the years it can least add. METHOD: **price an unobtainable artifact against a proxy population already in the store**, at zero network cost, which converts "no dump exists" from a dead end into a stated size threshold the next proposer must beat, instead of a re-run of the host census. Do not re-test the nine dump hosts, `keys.openpgp.org`, archive.org or Zenodo, and do not re-price the Debian, GNU or Apache keyrings; only a previously unknown artifact holding ~100,000 in-window bindings reopens it, and it is worth one grep if one ever surfaces inside another lens's whole-host index. |
| rest of `bl.iro.bl.uk` | all 20,871 file_sets enumerated, the geoindex is the only web dataset; `woa1.zip` at 16.7 GB is 583 War Office photographs |
| DOI `10.5259/ukwa.ds.2/cdx/1` | the record has no file attached, one of 389 works in a 2021-10-20 bulk metadata import; cheaply re-testable |

## `iedr_register`: the archived IE Domain Registry register listing

The IE Domain Registry regenerated the whole `.ie` register as static pages at
`/statistics/<letter>-doms.html`, one per initial letter, captured by Wayback; 26 in-window pages hold
24,805 distinct `.ie` names.

```bash
uv run python scripts/collect_iedr_register.py
uv run ark ingest iedr_register data/raw/iedr/*-doms.html
```

**Dating.** Each page's own machine-written line, `updated automatically at 14:51 GMT on Friday, 21
December 2001`, stamped by the cron that rebuilt the register. A page whose own line falls outside the
window is dropped whole: `l-doms.html` resolves to a 28 March 2002 edition, and taking the capture
stamp instead would have imported 931 names into 2001.

`artifact_listing`, lineage `registry`: the registry stating which names were registered at a stated
instant, the same instrument as an InterNIC zone file. Nobody typed the list, so no corroboration
split, and it says nothing about any other year. **Admitted master by Ivo, 2026-08-24**, at 19,263
net-new pairs worth 18,769.9 EE. **829 of those come from the earlier `/lists/` tree, 812 at 1999 and
17 at 2000, and they carry a different footer**: a plain `Last updated 27 Nov 1999` rather than the
`updated automatically at 14:51 GMT on Friday, 21 December 2001` cron stamp the 2001 editions carry.
They rest on the page being a generated register listing, not on the word "automatically", which is a
weaker ground and is stated so it can be discarded separately.

`stalled.html` is excluded by filename before its date is read: it lists pending applications, names
nobody had registered.

---


## Evaluated and rejected

| Source | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Letters to the ccTLD registries that still hold their own 1996-2001 registers: Nominet, auDA and CIRA (2026-08-30)** | **BLOCKED at 0 EE, and two of the three namespaces are closed permanently while the third is now sized.** No data artifact was fetched; this is a screen of the three registries' published data-access policies plus a repricing against the live store (merged260827, 15,624,381 domains, snapshot 14:42), every count taken as DISTINCT DOMAINS off a per-domain pivot. **The migration screen decides where a letter can go, and it runs before any policy is read.** Ask when the namespace last changed registry operator and whether the changeover re-created the licences, because a re-registration resets every creation date to the migration instant. `.au` dies outright: Kevin Robert Elz administered it from 1986, `com.au` was licensed to Melbourne IT in October 1996, and auDA's competition model plus the AusRegistry registry began 1 July 2002 with 282,632 names under management, so auDA never held the window and its published-policy archive begins in 2002 with no earlier document. `.ca` is halved: from 18 September 2000 every registrant had to transition to CIRA by RE-REGISTERING, CIRA became the registry on 1 December 2000, so only the 2000 and 2001 slices are CIRA's to give and the 1996-1999 headroom is not. `.uk` survives whole, and the **inverse test** proves it in one record: our own Nominet RDAP observation has `demon.co.uk` at `1996-05-05T21:08:48Z`, nineteen days before Nominet was incorporated on 24 May 1996 (company number 03203859, printed in its own policy PDF), so the UK Naming Committee register was imported with its dates intact rather than restamped. **Bidirectional headroom (held Y-1 or Y+1, missing Y), the correct screen for a register since a register attests membership at Y:** `.uk` 702,229 EE total (1996 58,978 / 1997 27,390 / 1998 121,088 / 1999 202,226 / 2000 173,429 / 2001 119,118), `.au` 167,587, `.ca` 126,063; adjacent-only `.uk` 194,173, `.au` 53,401, `.ca` 49,486. Held distinct in-window: `.uk` 538,057 of 589,786, `.au` 123,408 of 154,019, `.ca` 105,045 of 133,560. The naive held-any-year-missing-Y figures, which must not be quoted, are `.uk` 2,190,672, `.au` 440,660, `.ca` 313,748. **The hypothesis over-priced its own prize by about 1.9x** by quoting those naive per-name numbers as register value: it claimed `.uk` 1999 at 0.7546 EE per already-held name, `.au` 0.6309, `.ca` 0.5270; measured today the naive figures are 0.7333, 0.5418, 0.4433 and the defensible register figures are **0.3758, 0.3402, 0.2930**. Cross-check: `.uk` 1999 bidirectional measures 202,226 EE here against the 200,574.8 EE recorded on 2026-08-27, agreeing to 0.8% with the store having grown between; and the CIRA-reachable 2000+2001 slice at 50,656 EE, spread roughly evenly over 2000-2002 transitions, gives ~24,900 EE, independently reproducing the 25,377 EE this file derived for `.ca` by a different route. **The policy screen answers no in all three, so the RIPE precedent does not transfer as a channel.** RIPE worked because its own README named `hostcount@ripe.net` for exactly this ask; no ccTLD registry here names an equivalent address. Nominet's only published door is the Data Release Policy (`cdn.prod.website-files.com/.../DataReleasePolicy.pdf`, 170,892 B, 4 pages, read in full), exclusively about personal data on a NAMED domain for legal enforcement, its three worked examples a trade mark holder, solicitors enforcing IP rights and a law enforcement agency, with no bulk, research or historical clause; the registrar-facing `data.nominet.uk` is credentialed one login per accredited registrar and serves the current zone file and drop lists, so killer law 4 applies to it. auDA's complete 2002-to-date policy archive (521,566 B) holds a WHOIS Policy, a Privacy Policy and a Registrant Contact Information Policy and no data-access, research-access or bulk-release policy of any kind. CIRA's complete document index (read at `web.archive.org/web/20241214125556id_/https://www.cira.ca/en/resources/documents/`, 526,815 B, live host 403s behind Cloudflare) holds a DNSSEC Practice Statement, Dispute Resolution Rules, the Registrant Agreement, Professional Services terms and privacy documents, its only data publications being the aggregate Internet Factbook and `.CA` Insights Reports. Freedom of information reaches none of them: Nominet is a private company limited by guarantee, auDA a not-for-profit under a Commonwealth endorsement rather than a Commonwealth entity, CIRA a not-for-profit corporation. **Operational: a WAF is not a refusal and is never a reason to file a policy as unread**, since when a registry's live site 403s an honest User-Agent, its policy documents are usually on a CDN host that does not, or in Wayback replay at `/<ts>id_/`. Robots read whole before any request: `nominet.uk` and `www.nominet.uk` return a 103 B 403 to an honest User-Agent and to Claude-User alike including on `/robots.txt`, so no Nominet robots file could be read at all; `registrars.nominet.uk/robots.txt` 179 B allow-all; `www.cira.ca/robots.txt` 188 B blocks only `/wp-admin/`, `/?s=`, `/page/*/?s=`, `/search/`; `www.auda.org.au` and `files.auda.org.au` serve no robots.txt; `rbscarchives.library.ubc.ca/robots.txt` 26 B, both lines commented out. **Do not write to auDA and do not re-open `.au` on this shape.** Exactly one letter is worth Ivo's time and it is on the open decision sheet: Nominet, for a two-column extract of domain name and registration date for `.uk` names registered before 1 January 2002, no registrant data at all, which puts the ask outside the Data Release Policy's scope entirely, addressed through `registrars.nominet.uk` and NOT to `data-release@nominet.uk`, which is the personal-data door and will bounce. **The one genuinely untried thread is the third question the screen ends on: who held the window is routinely not who holds the namespace now.** The pre-December-2000 `.ca` register was maintained by hand at UBC Computer Science by John Demco and a team of volunteers on UBC facilities, more than 100,000 names before the handover, so the 75,407 EE of 1996-1999 `.ca` headroom that is not CIRA's to give belongs to a UNIVERSITY, which has an archive and a deposit policy where a registry has terms of use. UBC's archival catalogue at `rbscarchives.library.ubc.ca` has no robots rules but is a JavaScript-only AtoM instance returning a 292 B "Please enable JavaScript" shell to a plain client, so it could not be searched here; it needs a JS-capable fetch or one email to UBC Archives asking whether a CA Domain Registry fonds exists. |
| **Machine-written Usenet posting families: a whole-spool census of the on-disk 1996-2001 archives for host-dense and recurring robot postings, the class both five-figure Usenet wins belong to (2026-08-30)** | CLOSED at **780.1 net-new post-split EE**. No network used: the 16,849 `*.mbox.zip` already under `data/raw/usenet_*`, 456.82 GB expanded and 178,700,605 messages, read end to end on two gates; what dates one item is the message's own `Date:` header written by the posting agent, over host tokens a human typed or a spammer forged, so the split applies. 109,006 items, 357,859 pairs over 261,996 domains, 240,776 already held, **2,207 net-new pairs and 780.1 EE** (117,083 pairs and 71,311.2 EE pre-split, do not quote, 91.4x), mean weight 0.3535, typo bound 62.7%. The kill is one number: of 45,147 domains named at 2001 that the store already dates, 682 lack 2001, P = 0.0151 against store-wide `com` 0.611, a 40x shortfall holding in every year of the window, because four collectors have already read this spool whole and a new SELECTION over it recovers only what they missed. Census, not a sample, so it bounds the class: 0 of 65,101 families reach 10,000 distinct in-window domains. Positive control on `can.domain`, which the same screen ranks the known CA-Registrar family first from its signature alone. Method in `discovery.md`, the re-selection law. |
| **Software Heritage as the one un-robots-barred archive of 1996-2001 source trees, hunting the period DATA FILE inside a revision (`domains`, `blacklist`, `urls`, `hosts`, `sites`) (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 0 EE on the archive itself, because nothing in it dates one item, and at 13.4 net-new post-split EE on the control corpus that does carry the stamp.** `softwareheritage.org` stores content addressed by hash and its public export factors the filename and the date into separate tables, so the blob is the only thing a file family can be asked for and the blob carries no stamp: 0 of 692 sampled from the exact name family carry a machine-written 1996-2001 generation stamp. That fails condition 2 of the standing rule at the CLASS level, so no artifact in the archive can be admitted whatever its contents, and the revision's committer timestamp dates the commit and not the list inside it. **Control arm, run so the zero could be told from pointed-wrong**: `https://archive.debian.org/debian/dists/{bo,hamm,slink,potato,woody}/`, the same file family in a container where the stamp does exist as the `.deb` `data.tar` member mtime. 2,124 in-window items give 1,758 pairs over 1,108 domains, 1,067 domains held (96.3%), **21 net-new pairs and 13.4 EE post-split** (68 pairs and 47.9 EE pre-split, do not quote, 3.6x), mean weight 0.6380, typo bound 66.1%, and 97 packages carry the family against 48 read, so the ceiling is ~27 EE. **The kill is the 2001 screen: of 552 domains named at 2001 and held, 14 lack 2001, P = 0.025 against store-wide `com` 0.611, 24x worse.** A hostname printed in a config file that a Unix distribution shipped is a public server the crawlers reached, so a package payload belongs with the operator and authority corpora and not with the blocklists. **Method worth keeping: `Contents-<release>.gz` is the `ls-lR` trick for a package archive.** Five requests indexed 1,695,996 paths and reduced the whole archive to 270 candidate paths in 97 packages before a single payload was fetched, and the whole measurement cost 18 MB. **Do not re-test** Software Heritage, `archive.debian.org` package payloads, or the period-data-file-inside-a-package shape anywhere. |
| **The other four regional registries, on the reading that just paid 90,770 EE at RIPE: the RPSL `changed:` attribute in the APNIC, ARIN, LACNIC and AFRINIC database dumps (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 99.7 net-new post-split EE, and refuted on SCHEMA, not on availability: every dump in the family was reachable and read.** `changed:` no longer exists outside RIPE's frozen mirror. APNIC carries 0 `changed:` lines, bulk-replaced by `last-modified:` stamped `2008-09-04T06:40:26Z`; LACNIC has 533,380 objects all carrying `changed:` and 0 in window, over a schema with no hostname attribute at all; ARIN's IRR has 25 in-window `changed:` lines out of 25,663 (0.097%), the same shape the register already priced at 4.44 EE. The `*dn:` reading that gave RIPE 1,232,554 names has no counterpart either: APNIC's 304,694 `domain:` objects are 28 forward names and the rest in-addr.arpa, because RIPE NCC was a domain registry for European ccTLDs in 1999 and the other three are reverse-delegation registries that never held forward names. The three NIR mirrors APNIC carries (`jpnic`, `krnic`, `twnic`) are stripped identically: 0 `changed:`, 0 `nserver:`, 0 `domain:`. No registry publishes a history of dumps, proved rather than assumed: ArchiveTeam's `archive.org/download/ftp.arin.net/2014.05.ftp.arin.net.tar.txt`, 19,483,705 B, lists all 186,105 entries of the 43.4 GB tree and only 123 carry a 1996-2001 mtime, every one rwhois source, an RFC PostScript or a slide deck, while `web.archive.org/web/20000817130836id_/http://ftp.apnic.net:80/apnic/` shows no `whois/` directory in August 2000, so the bulk dumps postdate the window. AFRINIC not fetched: `ftp.afrinic.net/robots.txt` is 58 B and 4 lines, read whole, and `Disallow: /db` is a prefix covering both its database paths. **The one artifact that survives, priced in full against the live store: 99.7 EE.** `https://ftp.apnic.net/apnic/arin/arin.zones.tar.gz`, 405,696 B, `Last-Modified: Wed, 03 Feb 1999 08:50:39 GMT`, is APNIC's tar of its own BIND slave directory holding ARIN's twelve in-addr.arpa /8 zones. What dates one item is BIND 8's own transfer comment at the head of each zone file, `; from 192.149.252.21   at Thu Jan  7 12:22:21 1999`, written by `named` when the AXFR completed, backed by the SOA serial `1998111700` and the tar member mtimes. 194,400 NS records give 8,805 hostnames and 5,591 registrable domains at 1999, of which 5,406 already carry 1999 (96.69%): **157 net-new pairs and 99.7 EE post-split** (185 pairs and 116.2 EE pre-split, do not quote), mean weight 0.6352, read twice by independent code agreeing on every figure. Not banked: the tarball is ARIN's data on APNIC's host and carries no notice of its own, so its terms are the one condition of the standing rule left open, and 99.7 EE does not justify chasing them. The directory has been frozen since 1999, so there is no second edition to recover. **The letter route the hypothesis proposed was priced before writing and is not worth sending: 299.3 EE.** APNIC's 717,655 live `nserver:` lines give 10,423 registrable domains of which only 557 are held-and-missing-2001; the other 6,878 are modern nameservers, so the pre-split 4,305.4 EE overstates the source 14.4x. The ARIN region is bounded worse, since 99.607% of the 5,591 operators its own reverse tree names are already held, and the counterfactual caps even a perfect 2001 snapshot of the identical artifact at 372.2 EE. **Method, two reusable pieces.** (1) The index mtime is the screen: on a host with no whole-tree index, walk the HTTP autoindex and keep only rows whose own date column falls in 1996-2001, which is how the paying artifact was found, in a directory one registry keeps for another registry's operational files and stopped tidying. Where an ArchiveTeam grab exists its `.tar.txt` does the same job for the whole host in ONE request and also recovers what has since been deleted: 19 MB answered 43.4 GB. (2) Price the family's ceiling from the MODERN file before asking anyone for the old one: today's `nserver:` set re-dated to 2001 bounds what a 2001 snapshot could ever be worth, and it cost one download to learn the letter was not worth Ivo's time. Terms read whole first on all eight hosts; APNIC's bulk-access AUP does permit "Internet research and analysis", so the whois tree was usable and is simply worthless. The counterfactual re-dating the same 5,591 names to 2001 pays 372.2 EE, 3.7x the 1999 reading, so the aim-at-2001 law holds on this population too. |
| **An exhaustive sweep of the NAME space: a machine walking a wordlist or every three- and four-character string and recording taken or available at a stamped instant, hunted in the speculator Usenet groups, on archive.org, in discmaster and at the "N-letter domains" hosts (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 22.1 net-new post-split EE on the one arm that exists, and the artifact the hypothesis describes does not survive at all: five independent existence layers returned zero against a working positive control in each.** The only arm with data is `https://archive.org/download/usenet-comp/comp.protocols.tcp-ip.domains.mbox.zip`, 24,402,629 B, 36,737 messages, 23,655 in window; what dates one item is the message's own `Date:` header in the Usenet spool, machine-written by the posting agent, over a name list a human pasted, so the names take the corroboration split. At 60+ distinct names only 121 in-window posts exist and at 200+ only 6: 3,835 pairs over 3,264 distinct domains, 2,986 already held, 849 net-new pre-split (601.8 EE, **do not quote, it overstates by 27.2x**), **49 net-new pairs and 22.1 EE post-split**, mean weight 0.4510, typo upper bound 43.7%. Reading them kills the shape independently: 100 of the 121 are automated AlterNIC and International Root Server Confederation "Root Sync Reports" listing 130 to 180 TLD **nameservers**, and the largest single post, 1,212 names, is a 1996 list of DNS servers with UDP checksums disabled, so the densest posts in the densest group are killer 3, authorities and not hosts. **The four other layers, each with its control.** (1) The three groups the hypothesis names are out of window: the four `alt.domain-names.*` mboxes are 9,023,811 B and 9,046 messages with **earliest post 2003 in every one of the four**, while the same screen finds 56 posts carrying 100+ host tokens, all 2003 or later. **Corrected 2026-08-30, verified twice: the reason is wrong and the closure still stands.** The two copies on disk are `alt.domain-names.forsale.mbox.zip` 9,642,746 B, 29,342 messages, 18,286 dated 1999-2001, and `alt.domain-names.registries.mbox.zip` 4,291,950 B, 9,680 messages, 5,885 in window, so the in-window population does exist; re-priced over every in-window post of both, 22,163 items and 41,327 distinct domains give **88 net-new pairs and 31.6 EE post-split**, typo bound 68.7%. (2) archive.org `advancedsearch.php`, never `scrape`, control `title:(squidguard)` returning 3 in the same batch: `"domain availability"` in window **0**, `"zone file"` **0**, `title:(domainsurfer OR namedroppers OR "domain name database")` **0**, `"registered domains"` **1**, a 2002 shareware checker. The two availability tools that do exist ship no data (`avdom102_zip` is 402,933 B of `setup.exe` and a readme; `tucows_220806_Check_Domains` is a 2002 installer). (3) discmaster over 1,722,834,293 indexed files, `tsMin=19960101&tsMax=20011231`, control `blacklist` at 1,290: `domains.txt` returns 196 matches and **all 100 shown are two programs**, VisualRoute's ccTLD lookup table and the Up Yours mailbomber's relay list; `fourletter` and `domainlist` return nothing. (4) `archive.org/wayback/available` at `timestamp=20010601` on the hosts whose names promise the artifact, control `namewinner.com/whole_list.php?del=tab` returning its known capture: all seven with 2001 captures are framesets, parking pages, registrar homepages or one person's hand-typed 40-name for-sale page, and three have no captures at all. **The prize is real, which is why the zero matters, and it was priced off the store with no network at all**: 3-letter `.com` is 17,576 of 17,576 held and all 17,576 carry 2000 but only 10,595 carry 2001; 4-letter `.com` is 152,659 held, 114,659 at 2000, 69,836 at 2001. One 2001-dated per-name sweep of six letter spaces is worth **73,321 EE gross over already-held names**, **42,300 EE** on the adjacent-year-clean subset, plus ~13,477 EE from pool names an `artifact_listing` would date without the split. **That is the largest measured ceiling this lens can reach and there is no artifact under it.** **Method, and the first two transfer.** **(a) Ask when a Usenet group was CREATED before pricing what is in it**: one Giganews item per hierarchy with a `/metadata/` file list costs one request, so a whole hierarchy's year histogram is two requests and a `From `-split, and it settles the entire speculator family in under 10 MB. **The domain aftermarket's published record starts in 2002-2003, after the window closes** (alt.domain-names 2003, DNForum 2002, dnjournal 2002), so any hypothesis of the form "the speculator trade published tables" is asking the trade for records from before it had a press and dies on that alone. **(b) Price the enumerable space against the store BEFORE hunting the artifact**: generating `aaa.com`..`zzz.com` and joining `domain_year` costs one query and no requests, and it turns "would a sweep be worth it" into 73,321 EE with a per-space breakdown. Do this for every "somebody enumerated X" hypothesis. (c) A 2001 capture of a promising HOST NAME is worth almost nothing on its own, and `wayback/available` plus one `id_` fetch settles each for two requests with no CDX. (d) Confirmed for a second lens: an availability-checking PROGRAM never ships the answers, so screen software items out unless the file list shows a data file larger than the binary. **Do not re-test** the `alt.domain-names.*` groups, `comp.protocols.tcp-ip.domains`, discmaster by filename for this signature, or the "N-letter domains" host family. The 73,321 EE ceiling stays open with no public route: a self-run 2026 sweep evidences current state and not 2001 (killer 4), the Verisign quota that would pay for it is spent, and the only remaining routes are the two open non-public letters that `era_bulk_whois` already closed on. |
| **The Linux Counter machine census, a row-per-machine registry with its own entry date (`counter.li.org` reports, plus `lugww.counter.li.org`) (2026-08-30)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE over 0 distinct domains, because the registry never collected a host name.** `https://web.archive.org/web/19991117060801id_/http://counter.li.org:80/machine-form.html` and 13 siblings, 189,831 B. What dates one item is the counter's own machine-written registration stamp, printed per row on `reports/recentusers.php3` as `2001-12-24 20:20:16`, on a row that carries no host name at all: 15 machine rows, 15 exact timestamps, 0 host names. The 1999 registration form's complete field list is key, name, country, purpose, cpu-count, cpu-maker, cpu-type, cpu-speed, cpu-comment, memory, disk, network, distribution, distribution-version, source-main, source-detail, users, accounts, with no hostname, FQDN or URL field, and every machine report (`machines.html` 1999, `machines.txt` 2001, `systemstats.php` over 1,856 machines, `uptimestats.php` over 2,315) is an aggregate histogram. The volume half was right and did not save it: 100,306 registrations by 2001. Two further independent kills, either sufficient: the operator's whole 186 B `robots.txt` disallows `/cgi-bin/`, the only per-record view that could have carried a domain, and `legal.html` forbids "bulk usage" under the Berne Convention and the Norwegian Privacy Act and says the database "has been seeded with addresses to detect such usage", so those are terms we do not hold. The sibling `lugww.counter.li.org` (489 groups, homepage URL and a per-record "Last changed on 2001-04-01") was dropped once `sources.md` was found to close it: at the 0.0553 EE per listed group measured on `linux.org/groups`, 489 groups price at ~27 EE. **Method, three reusable parts.** **(a) For a registry hypothesis, read the REGISTRATION FORM before the reports**: one 6,690 B `machine-form.html` settled a 100,306-row corpus in a single request, because a field the form never collected cannot appear in any listing, in any year, at any fill rate. That is strictly cheaper and strictly more decisive than the fill-rate screen, which only measures how often an existing field is populated. **(b) A dead origin host is not a dead robots.txt**: `counter.li.org` no longer resolves, but its archived robots and terms are retrievable at `web/<ts>if_/<host>/robots.txt`, and they are the operator's standing instruction; fetch both before walking an archived site. **(c) A host-name grep is not a family grep**: "counter.li.org" and "linux counter" both return nothing here, which reads as a clean lens, while the LUG register the same host serves is closed three lines inside the entry titled "Federation club registers". Navigation cost no CDX: `archive.org/wayback/available` then `web/<ts>id_/<url>` and the page's own hrefs, with a retry wrapper, charging the politeness gap only to requests that reached the server. **Do not re-test** `counter.li.org`, `lugww.counter.li.org`, or any Linux Counter report. **One residual the negative does not touch**: a census that recorded the machine's NAME, which this one deliberately did not. The era's public NTP stratum-1 and stratum-2 server lists and the public DNS-secondary and Usenet-feed offer lists are named-host censuses with maintenance stamps, and neither is a roster, a directory nor a club register, so neither collides with a closed family. |
| **Registry whois records transcribed into artifacts that stamp themselves: pasted whois blocks in the on-disk Usenet spool, and the registrar creation dates recited in WIPO and NAF decisions (2026-08-30)** | **FIND, BANKED at 30.4 EE on the Usenet arm** (see `usenet_whois_paste` below), the UDRP arm PARKED. The Usenet arm reads the registry's own `Record created on 20-Jul-2000.` line out of 16,849 archives already on disk at zero network cost: 769 in-window pairs, 92.1% already held, 50 net-new pairs. **The UDRP arm fails condition 2 of the standing rule and is not ingested**: the date reaches the artifact as a panellist's sentence, `The Whois record of the domain MUSICWEB was created on January 10, 1995` (WIPO D2000-0001), which is a human recitation of a machine stamp and not the stamp itself, and `adrforum.com/robots.txt` answers with a redirect body rather than the file, so the NAF arm never had its terms read either. Measured anyway at 1,125 WIPO and 550 NAF decisions fetched: WIPO 284 pairs, 73.6% held, 46.2 EE; NAF 70 pairs, 75.7% held, 10.4 EE. **The family ceiling is 21,284 EE only if every one of the 8,892 disputed names earned every missing year, and rule 6 gives a creation date its own year alone, so the real ceiling is a small fraction of that.** Two things worth carrying. **A whois block puts the name twenty to forty lines above its creation line, so the binding is the whole risk**: an earlier pass bound `openssl.org`'s 1998 date to `engelschall.com` because a message quoted the same block three times and the HTML-escaped copy defeated a start-anchored name pattern while the date pattern still matched, the same mis-binding that once overstated the Edelman transcriptions by 47%. Fix is one normalisation both patterns read, plus a 40-line look-back cap carried on every row so it can be re-argued against data. **And `whois_creation` rows are year-checked against the first four-digit run in their evidence value**, so a value naming a group and a Message-ID would be read as the year 2000 on every row out of `microsoft.public.win2000.dns`: put the registry's own date first. |
| **MTA rejection notices quoted into dated messages: `Final-Recipient`, `Diagnostic-Code` and `550` blocks across mailing-list archives, LISTSERV gateways and the Usenet net-abuse groups (2026-08-30)** | CLOSED at **0.8 net-new post-split EE**, priced entirely off disk over 8.5 GB in four corpora (`data/raw/maillists/python` 1,207 archives, `data/raw/maillists/gnome` 1,415, 61 net-abuse and mail groups in `data/raw/usenet_bulk`, and all 582 `bit.*`, `mailing.*` and `fa.*` LISTSERV gateways in `data/raw/usenet_new`), with nothing fetched. What dates one item is the `Date:` header of the message quoting the notice, machine-written by the sending MUA, over a DSN body machine-written by the rejecting MTA. **700 in-window bounce messages gave 337 pairs over 307 domains, 313 pairs already held, 2 pairs and 0.8 EE post-split.** **The reusable law, and it generalises past mail: a corpus that fires on LIVENESS cannot add the year in which the liveness was observed.** A bounce exists because somebody was actively mailing that domain that month, and a domain being actively mailed in year Y is exactly the population the store already dates at Y. The discriminator, sampled as DISTINCT DOMAINS: **285 of 307 domains held (92.8%), and of the 315 pairs whose domain is held the store lacks the artifact's own year for 2, 0.6%, against a 58.3% control drawn at the same TLDs and years.** 97x worse than the population, so this is pointed-wrong, not nothing-found, and the held fraction is the wrong screen for any activity-triggered artifact (bounce, referrer, DNS query log, mail log, ticket queue). Density kills it independently: a census of the 61 net-abuse groups found 436,003 messages, 1,530 (0.35%) carrying any bounce marker and 156 (0.036%) dated in window. The dead arm (`Host unknown`, `Unrouteable`) was priced separately at 0.5 EE and should not be counted at all, since a rejection for an unknown host is evidence of ABSENCE and the address was human-typed besides. Method worth keeping: split the recipients into a live arm (`550 user unknown`, `552`, `554`, `Remote-MTA: dns;`, which prove a mail exchanger answered) and a dead arm, because a naive grep counts the negative arm as existence evidence. Do not re-test DSN material in list archives, LISTSERV gateways or net-abuse groups. The one residual is a different hypothesis: a bounce corpus whose recipients were never subscribers, such as a bulk-mailer's return-path log or an ISP postmaster dump from 2001-2003, and nothing of that shape is on disk. |
| **Pasted `user:pass@host` paysite credential dumps, hunted as a target across the whole Usenet spool plus 51 credential-family groups fetched for it (2026-08-30)** | CLOSED at **6.7 net-new post-split EE** over both arms. `https://archive.org/download/usenet-<hierarchy>/<group>.mbox.zip`, 1,515,813,396 B newly fetched (all HTTP 200; archive.org robots.txt read in full first, 12 lines and 238 B, `Disallow: /control/` and `/report/` only) plus the 16,849 archives already on disk, of which the sweep read 16,500 (97.9%) before the session ended. What dates one item is the `Date:` header of the message carrying the dump, machine-written by the posting agent; the `http://user:pass@host/members/` line itself is pasted by a human, so the hostname takes the corroboration split and only already-held names earn a year. **Union of both arms: 13,234 pairs over 9,686 distinct domains, 96.2% of domains held in some year and 97.0% of pairs already held, 17 pairs and 6.7 EE post-split** (whole-spool arm alone 12,851 pairs, 2.3 EE; targeted arm 792 pairs, 4.4 EE). **The kill is the 2001 screen: of 1,748 domains named at 2001, 1,610 are held and 10 lack 2001, so P(lacks 2001 | held) = 0.006 against the store-wide `com` 0.611, 102x worse.** The premise was that a 2001 paysite is short-lived and thinly crawled; measured, a host that was worth scraping a login for was a host with traffic, and traffic is what the crawlers followed. **A dump is a popularity ranking, not a census**, which puts it beside the magazine archive and the operator corpus on the head-selection list, and it refutes the 7.6 EE per dump page rate the Something Awful closure left behind: that page was one lucky page, not a rate. 362 of the 372 pre-split net-new pairs are novel names that earn nothing. Do not re-test credential or password material anywhere in Usenet, IRC logs or the warez hierarchies. |
| **Resolver and zone-file fragments pasted into dated messages: `dig` and `nslookup` answer sections, `named.conf` and zone excerpts, across ten Usenet DNS-operations groups (2026-08-29)** | CLOSED at **15.6 net-new post-split EE**. `https://archive.org/download/usenet-comp/comp.protocols.dns.bind.mbox.zip` and nine siblings, 275,156,324 B, 199,227 in-window messages; what dates one item is the `Date:` header of the message carrying the paste, machine-written by the posting agent, over a resolver's own ANSWER section. 5,033 pairs over 4,391 distinct domains, **81.7% already held**, 43 pairs and **15.6 EE** post-split. **Two reusable pieces.** (1) For a corpus of a KNOWN OPERATOR population, screen on P(lacks 2001 | held), never on the held fraction: here it is `com` 0.128 against the store-wide 0.611, so a held `com` name is worth 0.081 EE and the hypothesis's own floor of ~2,600 held `com` names understates the requirement by 4.8x. Somebody who ran BIND in 2001 ran a server the crawlers reached. **Operator corpus belongs beside authority corpus on the pre-download list.** (2) Prove the negative against the COUNTERFACTUAL store: a store that had never read a Usenet body would still price this lens at 937 pairs and 543.1 EE pre-split, **26.6 EE post-split over 59 pairs**, far under the bar. That separates "pre-empted by our own earlier ingest" from "capped", and it is the ceiling for every DNS newsgroup that exists. **The first pass put that ceiling at 772.4 EE and was wrong by 29x, from a join defect worth carrying.** It asked which pairs are held only by `usenet_bare`/`usenet_announce`/`usenet_address` by joining `evidence ON evidence_id = domain_year.evidence_id`, which returns exactly ONE row, the one the assignment happens to point at, so its `having sum(...)=0` tested whose row won an arbitrary tie-break rather than whether any other evidence exists for the pair. Joining every evidence row for that `(domain, evidence_year)` instead takes the count from **1,253 pairs to 16**. Re-derived independently 2026-08-30, the live figures reproduce exactly (5,033 pairs, 81.7% held, 15.6 EE, provenance `prior_task` 2,530, `usenet_announce` 680, `usenet_bare` 476, `isc_survey` 189, `usenet_address` 97) and only the counterfactual moved. **A counterfactual store is the set of PAIRS that would disappear, never the set of assignments that would change hands.** Also measured: PTR answers are the LEAST held bucket at 65.8%, not the visitor-log killer the hypothesis predicted, and machine forward answers the MOST at 91.0%. Do not re-test in the abuse groups: those hostnames are forged and the ~5% held law kills them on the other screen. |
| **US patent non-patent-literature citations carrying a printed retrieval date, read off the Google Patents front page (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 0.0 net-new post-split EE, and the ceiling for every US patent ever granted is 325 EE.** `https://patents.google.com/patent/US6167569/en` and 8,963 siblings; robots.txt is 6 lines and allows `/patent/` for `User-agent: *`. What dates one item is the retrieval date printed inside the citation string itself, `[retrieved on Apr. 30, 2001]`, never the patent's issue date, so a year counts only where it stands within 60 characters after a retrieval verb, and URLs are masked to a token first so a digit inside a path cannot pose as the date. **Measurement**, 8,964 utility patents sampled at random inside each grant year 1996-2004 (199/198/199/1025/1027/1023/2175/2123/995, seeded RNG over the first-number-of-year table): 36,815 NPL citations, **695 carry a hostname (1.89%)**, 159 carry a retrieval date inside the window, 87 distinct hosts, **80 distinct (domain, cited-year) pairs over 78 registrable domains**. 75 of 78 held (96.2%), **61 of 78 carry ALL SIX in-window years (78.2%)**, 76 of 80 pairs already held at that very year, **0 held-but-missing**, 4 novel and taking the split. **0 net-new post-split pairs, 0.0 EE.** **P(store lacks the cited year given the domain is held) = 0.0000** against the population 0.611 for `com`: the most extreme head-selection in this register, past gopher's 0.018 and the browser cache's 0.024. **Ceiling**: 0.008925 pairs per patent over the 1,356,390 utility patents granted 1996-2004 gives 12,105 pairs corpus-wide, the rule of three on 0 misses in 76 caps P(missing) at 0.0395, and the cited TLD mix (com 53, edu 8, org 4, gov 3, au 2, uk 2, net 2) means 0.6800 EE per pair, so **325 EE if every patent were read**, 15x under the bar. Both looser dating arms were priced and both also fail: any in-window year anywhere in a URL-bearing citation gives 9 pairs and 5.3 EE at a 72.2% typo bound, and the patent's own grant year gives 2 pairs and 0.8 EE at 66.7%. **Method 1, the OCR-novelty floor, and it is a pre-download screen for any scanned corpus: 3 of 3 novel names (100.0%) are one edit from a name already held** (`nearac.com` for `nerac.com`, `procucts.com` for `products.com`, `www.gamesdomain.comgdreview` where the OCR ate a space). An OCR'd corpus cannot contribute DISCOVERY at all, because every name in it you do not hold is a corruption of one you do; run the typo bound before believing any novelty figure taken off scanned text. **Method 2, and it is the transferable law: an examiner citation is created by an ADVERSARIAL process, so it is head-selected harder than a voluntary one.** To survive a validity challenge the cited page has to be findable and institutionally credible years after the fact, which selects exactly `proquest.umi.com`, `www3.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov`, `www.ietf.org`, `schedule.acs.org`, `www.research.ibm.com`, `www.santafe.edu` and `britannica.com`. That is the same effect measured on a different corpus in the Edelman row, where a blocklist filed as court evidence came out 78x worse than the robot-compiled one: **sort citation corpora by whether the citation had to withstand a challenge, not by how many citations they hold.** The grant years richest in retrieval-dated citations are 2002 and 2003, sampled hardest here at 4,298 patents and 116 in-window retrieval dates between them, and they contributed 0 held-but-missing pairs like every other year. The 2026-08-15 projection lower in this table got the size right (order 10^4 distinct domains, measured 13,000) and the value wrong, because it counted names and never asked which YEARS they were missing. Bulk routes all closed and none is worth retrying: `bulkdata.uspto.gov` does not resolve, `data.uspto.gov` serves a 20,666 B Angular shell and `api.uspto.gov` PTGRXML is 401 without a key, Reed Tech 403s, BigQuery `patents-public-data` is 401 without GCP auth, and the PatCit Zenodo release ships no WEBPAGE table. **`patentsview.org` and `relianceonscience.org` both name ClaudeBot with `Disallow: /`**, two more for the refusing list in `CLAUDE.md`. **Do not re-test** patent citations on a bulk route or at a second patent authority: the ceiling is a property of the citation population, not of the sample size, and the adversarial-selection argument carries to EPO and WIPO unchanged, though only the US corpus was measured. |
| **The `.biz` and `.info` launch registries of 2001: an archived Afilias or NeuLevel zone snapshot, the NeuLevel startup and randomisation output, the Afilias sunrise allocations and the STOP and RDRP dockets (2026-08-29)** | CLOSED at **0 net-new EE**, and the entire family is capped at **57.7 EE** whatever is found in it. What dates one item in the only master-eligible arm is a registry creation date, `whois_creation`, and both registries forbid compiling it: `https://rdap.identitydigital.services/rdap/help` says "(b) enable high volume, automated, electronic processes ... except as reasonably necessary to register domain names" and `https://rdap.nic.biz/` says "the copying, compilation, repackaging, dissemination or other use of the information provided by the Service is explicitly forbidden without prior and explicit permission". No launch-era bulk listing exists either: `advancedsearch.php` holds 310 items matching `title:("zone file") OR description:("zone file")` and every one is 2022 or later, `afilias` returns 6 items (four court dockets, two podcasts), and ten plausible early `.info` and `.biz` directory hosts have **zero 2001 captures** between them, nine first appearing in 2002 or later. Method, three pieces. (1) **RDAP terms can be read with ZERO data queries**, which satisfies rule 3 exactly: the RFC 7482 `/help` endpoint returns the full `notices` array (3,575 B for `.info`) and a malformed path returns the same notices on an HTTP 400 (3,342 B for `.biz`), so no record is ever fetched under unread terms, strictly better than the port-43 habit of reading past the record. (2) **Price the SPLIT CEILING of a whole family before hunting any artifact in it.** One query, sampled as distinct domains: 123 `.info` and 29 `.biz` names hold at least one in-window year and miss 2001, so every human-typed artifact the family could ever yield is worth 123 x 0.3648 + 29 x 0.4425 = 57.7 EE, and a docket of 300,000 names pays the same as one of 300. A family is worth hunting only where a master-eligible artifact is plausible. (3) **Check provenance-by-source of what the store already holds in the namespace before believing a hypothesis about what no compilation can see**: the premise was that landrush names are invisible to `domain_creation_bulk`, and one `source x evidence_type` count inverted it, since that source supplies **93.9% of our `.info` 2001 pairs** (20,435 of 21,766) and 59.0% of `.biz`. Negative worth keeping: a namespace opening in the last quarter of the window has no in-window periodic observation at all, so ISC-style surveys, dispute dockets and directories covering it are all first dated 2002. Do not re-test sunrise or landrush lists, STOP or RDRP dockets, or registry startup reports; reopen only on written permission of the RIPE kind from Identity Digital, and only if a bulk list of the ~279,000 launch-era names surfaces first. |
| **The `.edu` registry's own creation dates at `whois.educause.edu:43`, the only endpoint that carries them (2026-08-29)** | BLOCKED on terms at **0 net-new EE banked**, priced at 400 to 1,400 EE had they allowed it. `whois.educause.edu:43`; what dates one item is `Domain record activated:    23-May-1985`, a registry-written creation date, `whois_creation`, dating its own year only. One query made (`mit.edu`) and no more: the banner PRECEDING every record says "The use of electronic processes to harvest information from this server is generally prohibited except as reasonably necessary to register or modify .edu domain names", and a ~7,700-record sweep is not that. No RDAP, `.edu` is absent from `data.iana.org/rdap/dns.json`; the web front end is the same database behind a Cloudflare `cf-mitigated: challenge` 403. Method: **band the candidate population before pricing any single-year evidence class.** Sampled with a third-party name list (`raw.githubusercontent.com/Hipo/university-domains-list`, 2,448 distinct `.edu` names) so no term was touched: 51.39% of the live register holds all six in-window years at P(missing) 0.0000, so a creation date pays nothing there whatever year it names, and 23,680 of the store's 25,400 `.edu` names are not in the live register at all. **A live registry's creation dates are anti-correlated with the store's gaps**, because the names still queryable today are the ones an archive crawled every year, and the effect is strongest where the TLD is small, institutional and stable. Also: the terms are not always past the record, EDUCAUSE puts them ahead of it, so read the whole response both sides of the data. Do not re-test either host or `.edu` RDAP. |
| **Commercial filter category databases: the CyberNOT, N2H2 and Bess, SurfWatch, WebSENSE, SmartFilter, X-Stop, CYBERsitter and Net Nanny lists, and the censorware publication and litigation channels that were meant to carry them (2026-08-29)** | BLOCKED at **134.1 net-new post-split EE**. `https://cyber.harvard.edu/archived_content/people/edelman/mul-v-us/`, Benjamin Edelman's expert report for the CIPA case; what dates one item is the vendor classifier's own observation column, `Blocked On (dates) (all dates are in 2001)`, e.g. `http://www.0-roulette.com  Blocked by: N2H2 (Pornography - Jul 26, Sep 16), Websense (Sex - Jul 5, Aug 18, Sep 16)`. 7,641 dated records, 4,898 distinct domains, 4,867 held any year (99.4%), 4,639 already carrying 2001 (94.7%), 228 held-and-missing-2001 all adjacent, **134.1 EE**. BLOCKED on rule 3, not on evidence: the host page states "The data contained here is not intended for use for other purposes, and it should not be used for other purposes without first contacting the author", so nothing here may be ingested until Edelman answers. The product side is closed separately: the vendor database never shipped in readable form on four products and three delivery architectures (Bess holds it server-side, Net Nanny ships an empty starter list, SurfWatch for Windows ships it encrypted at 7.7 to 7.99 bits per byte, SurfWatch for Mac fetches it on subscription), so the only retrievable artifact in this family is somebody else's test output. Method: **a blocklist published as litigation EVIDENCE is head-selected and loses the blocklist discount** because proving overblocking requires exhibiting sites a court will accept as legitimate, so the discriminator for an adversary-derived list is not "did a robot compile it" but "was it selected to be recognised": squidGuard's robot-compiled 2001 list is 57.9% held at 2001 and paid 10,736 EE, Edelman's same-vendor 2001 list is 94.7% held at 2001 and pays 134, a 78x collapse at comparable size. |
| **Random-IP web censuses: OCLC's Web Characterization Project, the Internet Operating System Counter, RIPE's DNS Hostcount published tables and the 1998 Internet Auditing Project (2026-08-29)** | CLOSED at **2.1 net-new post-split EE**. `https://web.archive.org/web/20001109203600id_/http://wcp.oclc.org:80/` and 53 sibling artifacts, 237,326 B; every artifact is self-dating (the IOS Counter's own report generator writes `Date : April '99`, RIPE's writes `This Count : Fri Apr 14 2000`) but **not one names a host**, so there is no item to date: of 57 domains recovered, only 16 occur in a census output at all and all 16 are queso fingerprint credits or illustrative labels inside an OS string, never a scanned host. 3 net-new pairs, **2.1 EE**, all from the prose around the censuses. Method in `discovery.md`, the sampling-unit law. |
