| **Wayback-captured registry lookup pages: the `domainsurfer.com` zone-file substring search, plus the per-record whois front ends `betterwhois.com`, `allwhois.com`, `geektools.com`, `checkdomain.com`, `whois.net` and the `domainwatch.com` change reports (2026-08-29)** | CLOSED at **0.0 net-new post-split EE**. `https://web.archive.org/web/20000118094612id_/http://www.domainsurfer.com:80/ssearch.cgi?dom=money`, the only in-window query-bearing capture found across both arms; what dates one item is the machine-written index build stamp the page prints above its hit list, `10,401,344 records / Last updated: 17 Jan. 2000`. 40 distinct domains, **40 of 40 already held and all 40 already dated at 2000**, 0 net-new pre-split, **0.0 EE**. Method in `discovery.md`, the form-endpoint law. |
| **Package-manager fetch endpoints: `MASTER_SITES`, `PATCH_SITES` and `WWW:` across the full CVS history of FreeBSD ports, NetBSD pkgsrc and OpenBSD ports, pulled as dated checkouts from `codeload.github.com` (2026-08-29)** | CLOSED at **40.8 net-new post-split EE**. `https://codeload.github.com/freebsd/freebsd-ports/tar.gz/<sha>` and the two sister trees, 22 annual and intra-year checkouts, 168 MB; what dates one item is the CVS-converted commit stamp of the checkout, `committer.date 2001-12-31T20:39:31Z`, machine-written and attesting the whole tree at that instant. 51,191 port directories, 4,044 distinct domains, **94.91% already held at 2001** and only 106 of 3,822 held at 2000 are missing 2001, 91 net-new post-split pairs, **40.8 EE**. | **Browser cache indexes, cookie files and history databases on preserved media (discmaster `msieCache`, `internetExplorerCache`, `mozillaMorkDatabase`, `netscapeCookies`, plus filename sweeps for `index.dat`, `fat.db` and `netscape.hst`) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 0.0 net-new post-split EE, and it dies twice over: the class is not preserved in a USED state, and where it is used the machine-written half of a cache is the most head-selected corpus yet measured.** What dates one item is sound and is not why this fails: the media mtime discmaster records per file (`artifact_listing`), and inside the file a per-entry machine-written stamp (IE5 index.dat FILETIME, Netscape `netscape.hst` last-visit record). **Measurement**: **0 net-new post-split pairs and 0.0 EE against merged260827; 99 of 99 pairs already held at the artifact's own year, and 85 of 85 distinct registrable domains (100.0%) already known.** The population was ENUMERATED by content hash, not sampled: **90 distinct in-window artifacts** across discmaster's 1,718,970,121 indexed files (31 `netscape.hst`, 27 `cookies.txt`, 10 `index.dat`, 9 `fat.db`, 6 `cookies`, 7 `MM2048/MM256.DAT`), 94 files and 1,547,518 B fetched, of which **45 held zero http hosts**; the 49 that held any gave 113 host strings and **85 registrable domains** sampled AS DOMAINS. The IE arm the hypothesis names is **6 `index.dat` files in a browser-profile path in the entire index**, all 16,384 or 32,768 B, from a Dr Watson diagnostic dump, two never-booted Virtual PC images and one warez release. Counterfactual with the population fixed and only the year moved to 2001: **2 pairs, 1.1 EE**, 0.0134 EE per domain. **The pre-fetch discriminator, and it is the finding: P(store lacks 2001 | domain held) is 0.024 over all 85 and 0.018 for `.com` (1 of 56), against the store-wide `.com` figure of 0.611. A 34x shortfall, worse than Backflip's 15x and the CACM citation set's 14x.** **Why, and both halves are measurable before a download.** (1) **Availability: what survives is a SHIPPED profile, never a used one.** All nine in-window `fat.db` are exactly 65,536 or 16,384 B, the empty Berkeley DB allocation, and every one held zero http hosts; the `netscape.hst` files on magazine cover discs (WPCDEC97 57,344 B, pcwaug97 49,152 B, pcwjul97 45,056 B) parse correctly and hold only `file:///D|/html/...`, the publisher's own QA pass over the CD, not the web. (2) **The hypothesis's own discriminator is refuted in the opposite direction to the one it predicted.** A cache does hold the ad servers, the image hosts and the one-visit tail exactly as claimed, and those turn out to be the MOST universal hosts of the period web: `doubleclick.net`, `ad.doubleclick.net`, `flycast.com`, `imgis.com`, `focalink.com`, `preferences.com`, `burstnet.com`, `mediaplex.com`, `realmedia.com`, `us.yimg.com`, `bfast.com`, beside `netscape.com`, `yahoo.com`, `altavista.com`, `infoseek.com`, `excite.com`, `hotbot.com`, `zdnet.com`, `tucows.com`. **An ad server is shared by every site that carries the ad, so a cache is head-selected HARDER than a bookmark, not more softly.** The Backflip closure's rule holds and tightens: a corpus dated by human ACTIVITY selects the year the store already covers, and the machine-written half of that activity selects the most-shared hosts on the web. **Method, five reusable and all pre-download.** **(a) Price a whole file CLASS's ceiling in one request by reading discmaster's own format facet**: `GET /search` returns a `<select name="format">` of **6,452 formats each carrying its all-time file count** (`msieCache (343)`, `mozillaMorkDatabase (423)`, `netscapeCookies (172)`, `iBrowseCookies (57)`, `konquerorCookie (34)`, `windowsURLShortcut (206,969)`), which is the availability screen for any signature before any query and before any filename guess, the same trick as reading a host's own `ls-lR` first. **(b) The filename query and the format query are each incomplete and must be UNIONED**: `q=index.dat&qfields=name&mode=deep` returned 973 in-window rows and MISSED the `MSHist01.../index.dat` that `format=msieCache` returned, while `format=internetExplorerCache` is a false-positive detector, 868 of its 1,000 in-window rows being static HTML from one item. **(c) Deduplicate by the `b3sum` the search result already carries, BEFORE fetching**: 372 in-window rows collapse to 84 distinct contents, a 4.4x request saving, because cover discs republish the same preinstalled profile every month. **(d) For a preallocated index, exact size IS the emptiness test**: IE5 `index.dat` allocates in 32 KB blocks and Netscape `fat.db` in 64 KB, so a size of exactly 16,384 / 32,768 / 65,536 is an untouched file, and here every such file held zero hosts, so a size histogram of the distinct set closes the class with no download. **(e) `/file/<itemid>/<url-encoded path>` serves the raw member from inside an ISO, sit, rar or zip**, so a member of a 673 MB image costs one small request. robots.txt read in full first (267 B, `Disallow: /` with the operator's written carve-out for limited targeted research automation); about 110 requests spaced 2.5 s. **Do not re-test** browser caches, cookie files, cache indexes or history databases on preserved media on any screen, and do not widen to `mozillaMorkDatabase`, whose 103 in-window rows are one CompuServe installer's `panacea.dat` republished across magazine discs. **The live adjacent lead belongs to a different hypothesis**: the client-side log that is NOT one user's head, a period PROXY log covering a whole office's long tail. discmaster in-window `access.log` at `sizeMin=100000` is 74 rows and the one genuine proxy log is `squid/logs/access.log`, 1,272,715 B, ts 2001-06-22, inside `network-alias.tar.gz` on item 14148; the 131 MB 1998 files at `ftp.parc.xerox.com/pub/uir/WWW-1999-data/logs_cleaned/` are SERVER logs and the directory name flags killer 6, so they are already covered by the visitor-log closure at 98.4% held. |
| **Gopher master server directories, the protocol's own machine-maintained register of every server that registered itself (UMN mother gopher `othergophers`, plus Floodgap, Veronica and the quux gopherspace mirror screened out with it) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 0.2 net-new post-split EE, and it dies on YIELD after passing the trust-selection screen honestly.** What dates one item is legitimate `artifact_listing`: the tar member mtime on the "Other Gophers" menu cache, 1996-04-13, stamps the whole edition at the instant the mother gopher rebuilt it, one date per edition rather than per entry. Cleared the whole host in one request under the ls-lR rule: `archive.org/download/Boombox_201511/Boombox.tar`, 211,814,400 B, is the UK Mirror Service copy of the entire `ftp://boombox.micro.umn.edu/pub/` tree of the site that invented gopher, greppable offline, and its `Boombox.README` dates the upstream disappearance without one request to the origin. The register is `Boombox/gopher/misc/othergophers.tar.Z`, 98,304 B, member `./all/.cache`, tab-separated, host in field 3, truncated upstream at exactly 96 KiB so 122,082 of 156,203 content bytes survive (78.2%, ends mid-entry at "Th"). **Measurement** on distinct registrable domains, not rows: 2,106 host fields, 1,782 distinct hostnames, **1,294 distinct registrable domains** (edu 432, com 162, org 112, ca 98, us 75, net 68, gov 45, au 38). **1,291 of the 1,294 are known to the store at all, 99.77%**, above the 87-99% authority-corpus band. `price_items.py` on the artifact's own year: 1,288 of 1,292 (domain, 1996) pairs already held, 4 net-new gross, **2 pairs and 0.2 EE net-new post-split** at mean weight 0.1002, and half the net-new names are one edit from a name already held. Independent SQL agreed on 1,288. The three names the store has never seen are `savba.cs`, `gopher.cr`, `inred.com.mx`. The number that closes the family is the six-year sweep over the same 1,294 domains, held-but-missing year Y, priced as the gross EE a perfectly dated edition of this population could pay in its best year: 1996 missing 3 (0.2 EE), 1997 missing 1 (0.0), 1998 missing 5 (2.3), 1999 missing 11 (6.9), 2000 missing 12 (7.6), **2001 missing 41 (23.3 EE gross)**. That is 0.018 EE per held name in the best year, against the 0.386 population average for `com` and the 0.041 of a 2001 magazine archive: **twenty-one times worse than population average and the worst head-selection yet measured.** Correcting the 78.2% truncation gives ~1,650 domains and ~30 EE; scaling to gopherspace at its ~5,500-server peak gives ~4,200 domains and ~76 EE. The hypothesis set its own floor at 2,000 names for 1,500 EE and the family misses it by 20x to 70x with every edition summed. Self-registration passed the trust screen honestly, but in 1996 self-registration still selected universities, libraries and government, and those are the names the baseline holds in every year of the window. **Method, and it generalises past gopher: sweep ALL SIX window years on the sampled domains, not just the artifact's own year.** The standing "held AND missing the artifact's year" screen answers only whether this edition pays; the same count across 1996-2001 answers whether ANY edition of that population could, and it costs one extra query on a sample already in hand. A population that is missing no year cannot be paid for at any date, so hunting a later or larger edition is provably wasted. Run it before any hunt for a second edition of anything. **Do not re-test** on a fuller `othergophers` copy, a Floodgap edition, a Veronica index or the 2006/2007 quux gopherspace mirror: all four draw the same saturated population and the 2001 ceiling of 23.3 EE bounds them together. By-product for the refusal list: **`www.floodgap.com` and `gopher.floodgap.com` both refuse ClaudeBot by name with `Disallow: /`**, and www.floodgap.com also names `anthropic-ai`, so the obvious host for any gopher or retro-internet lens is closed to us before it is proposed. |
| **Free-DNS hosted-zone inventories, the nameserver's own list of the zones it was configured to serve (Granite Canyon, secondary.com, zoneedit.com, xname.org, freedns.com) (2026-08-29)** | **FIND at 1,732.9 net-new post-split EE over 3,059 pairs from seven objects and 1,567,653 B, 0.102 EE per listed name, confirmed on one of five hosts and refuted on the other four.** What dates one item: the list stamps its own generation instant (`Rejected Zone List: 7-May-2001 22:11 GMT`; status.shtml's "29 November 1999 ... here is the list of pruned zones") and the IA capture fixes when it existed, so one row is Granite Canyon's nameserver holding that zone in its BIND configuration at that instant, class `artifact_listing`. The zone name was typed by the customer into a submission form, so the corroboration split applies and only already-held domains earn a year. **The artifacts**: `stale_30Nov1999.txt` (205,787 B, 14,522 zone names) plus the six in-window editions of `soa.granitecanyon.com/ZoneRejects/` (1,361,866 B, 2,948 to 4,097 forward zones each). **Measurement**, `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` over 18,797 items: 17,049 distinct pairs over 16,979 domains, 6,777 already held, pre-split 10,272 pairs and 5,813.2 EE (overstates 3.4x, do not quote), **post-split 3,059 pairs and 1,732.9 EE at mean weight 0.5665**, by year {1999: 2,001, 2001: 1,058}, by TLD {com 1,613, net 407, org 300, ch 58, de 51, nu 49}, 7,213 pairs and 6,828 names to the candidate pool, typo upper bound 57.1% (856 of 1,500). An independent duckdb screen sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS agrees to about 1%: stale_30Nov1999 13,199 distinct domains, 7,969 held-any (60.4%), 2,026 held-and-missing-1999 against price_items' 2,001; the 2001 ZoneRejects union 4,092 domains, 1,916 held-any (46.8%), 1,071 held-and-missing-2001 against 1,058. **The held-fraction is the finding**: 60.4% and 46.8% against 87 to 99% for authority corpora, ~50% for blocklists and 98.4 to 99.6% for visitor logs, because a zone is not a page, so no crawler reaches it through a link and the artifact is not head-selected. **And the population does not collapse on the 2001 threshold**: P(lacks 2001 | held) here is com 0.5745, net 0.6174, org 0.5113 against the store-wide law's 0.611 / 0.653 / 0.568, within 6%, and yield per held name at 2001 is 0.317 EE against the 0.386 the law predicts. At 1999 the same population gives com 0.2539, so a held name in a 2001 edition is worth 2.3x one in a 1999 edition; the 1999 list alone is 1,125.5 EE and the 2001 reject union alone 607.3 EE. **Refutations**: secondary.com (2001-05-16), zoneedit.com (2001-06-04) and xname.org (2001-10-27) put the whole zone inventory behind `/auth/`, `login.html` and a per-zone password, and freedns.com served an empty Apache index. **Method, and it is the transferable part.** (1) A free-DNS operator's CUSTOMER population is the tail and its OPERATOR population is not, and the two look identical until you ask who typed the name: the zone-file-RHS closure the same week measured nameserver operators at 99.3% held-at-year and concluded that any population defined by "this host answers DNS" is the ISC survey's own, but the same service read from the other end measures 46.8% held and 0.5745. The discriminator is whether the selection predicate is "runs a nameserver" or "asked somebody else to run one". (2) **When a service hides its inventory behind a login, look for its ERROR LOG.** All four operators refused to publish a customer list; the one that published a nightly list of the zones its BIND could not load gave away 4,369 names it never meant to. Reject lists, prune lists, lame-delegation reports and stale-zone reports are machine-generated, self-stamped and regenerated on a schedule, so every capture is a fresh dated edition and nobody thinks of them as a customer list. (3) **Enumerate a dead site's list files from its own dated changelog, not from an index**: four captures of `status.shtml` at 1999-02-22, 2000-03-11, 2001-06 and 2002-06-06 named every list file the site ever served and dated each, which a CDX prefix query would have cost many more requests to establish. **Exhaustion**: six ZoneRejects editions exist in 2001 and no more, fourteen probes across 2001-01 to 2002-04 collapsing onto those six timestamps; the 2002-05-26 edition is out of window and cannot date a year; and the predecessor `zoneRejects.txt` is 9 names at 2000-03-03 and HTTP 403 at every later capture. Not a by-construction zero: no `granitecanyon` directory exists under `data/raw` and this table records no ingest, and what already dates the held names on a 400-domain sample is `prior_task` 196, `usenet_announce` 48, `domain_creation_bulk` 33, `isc_survey` 33, `rdap_snapshot` 26. Prior art: the 2026-08-24 row below records this family at 1,881.1 EE against a 5,000 bar and kept no URL and no bytes, so it was not reproducible; 1,732.9 is that figure decayed by five days of ingest. Cost 40 archive.org fetches, no other host touched, zero CDX queries. **Density per LISTED name is 0.102 EE combined, 0.148 at 2001 and 0.085 at 1999, 6x to 30x the curated-directory floor of 0.005 to 0.017**, so the ~83,000-name floor that class demands becomes about 7,000 to 12,000 names here. Family CLOSED at those seven objects: `granitecanyon / artifact_listing` is in `approved-sources-list.md` awaiting a human `Decision:` line, the bytes are already fetched and nothing is ingested. |
| **Sweep for the registry FILE SIGNATURE rather than for the host that might hold it (whole-tree indexes from ftp.gwdg.de, ftp.ripe.net, ftp.zx.net.nz, gatekeeper.dec.com, FUNET and discmaster, plus 105 hosts robots-screened and 31 probed at 22 canonical index paths) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 1.0 net-new post-split EE, and the premise dies on availability, not on content: the whole-host index is nearly extinct.** What dates an item is the artifact's own stamp in its bytes (SOA serial, index header date), which the hypothesis assumed correctly and is not why it fails; the sweep found no in-window name-bearing artifact to date. **Measurement**: the only in-window name-bearing artifact the sweep surfaced was the Dec-1997 Walnut Creek Internet Info CD's `netinfo/`, priced with `scripts/price_items.py` against merged260827 at 399 pairs over **399 distinct domains** (single-year, so the per-row sampling trap cannot bite), **390 already held (97.7%)**, pre-split 9 pairs and 7.9 EE (overstates 8.1x, do not quote), **post-split 1 pair and 1.0 EE**, 1997 only, `.edu` only, 8 pairs to the candidate pool. Four orders of magnitude below the ~5,000-pair volume bar. **Availability**: 31 permitted hosts x 22 canonical paths (`ls-lR{,.gz,.Z,.bz2,.xz}`, `locatedb{,.gz}`, `INDEX.gz`, `FILES.gz`, `find.txt.gz`, `filelist.txt.gz`, `ALL`) = 682 requests, and **exactly one host served one** (`ftp.gwdg.de/pub/locatedb`). Three whole-host indexes exist in 32, and only one was findable by probing: `ftp.ripe.net/ls-lR.gz` came from a search result, and `ftp.zx.net.nz` publishes its index as a **27-part sitemap of 1,319,576 URLs** named on line 2 of its own robots.txt. `ls-lR.gz` was a mirror-protocol artifact and rsync killed the protocol, so the index now survives only at the RIR and the very large general archive, both current-state trees: **killer 4 one level up, a current-state index cannot index a past year's file.** **Signature counts, all zero against working controls**: gwdg's 8.8 GB mlocate tree (927,852,695 B compressed, 8,838,050,642 B raw) gave `content.rdf` 0, `ripe.db` 0, `internic` 0, `hostcount` 0, `com.zone` 0, `net.zone` 0, `.zone.gz` 0. `ftp.zx.net.nz`'s 1,319,576 URLs are the strongest single negative, because that host **is** the frozen-mirror population, 67 preserved FTP sites including `gatekeeper.dec.com`, `garbo.uwasa.fi`, `ftp.digital.com`, `simtel.net`, `ftp.netscape.com` and `ftp.microsoft.com`: every signature 0, and the two `netinfo` hits are a Novell utility and a Delphi component. gatekeeper's own 986,543-entry `Index-byname.Z`: zero registry artifacts. discmaster over **1,722,834,293 indexed files**: `content.rdf.u8` 0 rows, `ripe.db` 0 rows, and `com.zone`/`net.zone`/`org.zone`/`root.zone`/`whois.gz` return rows but **0 rows over 200 KB**, where a real gTLD zone is megabytes (the `com.zone` hit is the DOS game `ZONE.COM`), against same-session controls `resolv.conf` 100, `dmoz` 100 and `hostcount` 15, so the tokeniser works and the zeros are real. **Both openings the hypothesis named are individually shut.** `ftp.ripe.net/ls-lR.gz` (3,696,737 B, 870,126 lines) is a one-request decisive proof: `./ripe/hostcount` is one 5,257 B text plus four plots, `./ripe/hostcount/History` is only the 5,901-6,556 B country tables, and `./ripe/dbase` is `nrtmv4/` and `split/` alone, the current dummified edition, with no archived edition in 870,126 entries; FUNET agrees, `/pub/netinfo/RIPE/dbase/archive/` is one file and `split/` is 15 same-1999-edition subsets. **So no 2000 or 2001 `ripe.db` exists on any reachable mirror, and the `changed:`-lines-dated-2000-and-2001 route does not exist.** And the only third-party mirror of an ODP dump is `download.huihoo.com/dmoz/content.rdf.u8.gz`, 285,221,345 B, `Last-Modified: Wed, 13 Feb 2013`, eleven years late, which closes the opening the ODP record left. **The one real find is out of window and closes an open thread**: the signature `domain-contacts`/`domain-info` recovers InterNIC's own register on the Walnut Creek Internet Info CD **(1993)**, `domain-contacts.txt` 2,355,463 B ts 1993-10-30 and `domain-info.txt` 171,293 B ts 1993-06-30, the DDN NIC register entire and also on the March 1994 disc, the files the FUNET closure proved gone. Every surviving copy is 1993, and **the December 1997 edition of the same series dropped the register**: its `netinfo/` is 19 DoD NIC documents, its `00netinfo-index.txt` is stamped `[30-Apr-97]` and lists no register, and its largest file self-stamps `[14 June 1993]`. Rule 6 says the listing evidences the instant it stamps, and that instant is 1993, so it can date 1993 and nothing else. **Nobody should price it.** **Method, five reusable parts.** **(a) archive.org lists the complete filename tree inside an ISO or ZIP without downloading a byte**: `GET /download/<id>/<file>.iso/` returns an HTML index, and one request against a 673 MB ISO gave 29,333 paths; it is the `ls-lR` trick applied to a disc image, and needs no discmaster and no ISO9660 parsing. Match `<id>.iso/`-prefixed hrefs and `unquote` them; a `<td>` parse fails because the listing is not a table. **(b) Check the 8-byte magic before decoding any `locatedb`**: gwdg's is `\0mlocate\0\0`, not LOCATE02, and **a LOCATE02 decoder does not fail on mlocate, it runs to completion and emits zero hits**, which reads exactly like a proved negative. That cost one full pass and would have produced a confident false zero. **(c) discmaster is the largest filename index reachable, and one request settles a signature across CDs, disc images and FTP tarballs at once**: read the count from the `outputAs=json` row array, never from the HTML, whose "N results found" string is absent from the served markup and silently yields zero for everything; every zero needs a dotted-filename control in the same session. **(d) robots.txt's `Sitemap:` line names a whole-host index, and it is in the file you are already required to read in full**: read line 2 as a lead and not just as a permission, and it is the politest form of the trick, since the operator published it to be fetched whole. **(e) Ask for a host's own index once, and when it 404s, stop**, rather than building a sweep that assumes the index exists; a registry's own `ls-lR` is the cheapest existence proof and should precede any access request, since 3.7 MB and one request retired a question that a Wayback sweep, eleven European FTP hosts and a national-contributor hunt had taken a session to answer. **New robots refusals**: **`ftp.acc.umu.se` names ClaudeBot, Claude-User, Claude-Web, Claude-SearchBot and Claude-Code at lines 115-119 of a 6,238 B file whose FIRST group is a permissive `User-agent: *`**, exactly the trap the register warns about; blanket `Disallow: /` at `ftp.ulak.net.tr`, `ftp.tu-clausthal.de`, `ftp.linux.org.tr`, `mirror.ox.ac.uk`, `ftp.iinet.net.au`, `ftp.isc.org`, `ftp.freebsd.org` and `ftp.openbsd.org`; refusals in substance at `ftp.ntua.gr` and `ftp.grnet.gr` (`/pub/`) and `mirror.csclub.uwaterloo.ca` (`/pub` and every mirrored tree). Of 105 hosts screened: 38 open, 21 limited, 9 refusing, 37 unreachable. **Do not re-test**: gwdg locatedb, ripe.net ls-lR, the zx.net.nz sitemap, gatekeeper `Index-byname`, discmaster for any zone or RDF signature, or the Walnut Creek Internet Info series. **One residual**: the zx.net.nz 1,319,576-URL index is on disk and is the best surviving instance of the frozen-mirror population, so it is worth re-grepping for a DIFFERENT lens's signature at zero further request cost. |
| **RIPE Hostcount raw per-host output, the monthly European DNS walk's own AXFR transcript rather than its published aggregates (ftp.ripe.net History tree, FUNET, uni-erlangen, DENIC and eleven further European FTP archives, plus the national contributor layer) (2026-08-29)** | **BLOCKED at 0 EE banked, because no copy is reachable, and the family is worth 10x what it was parked at: 353,308 EE of adjacent-year headroom, of which 312,014 sits in the 2000 and 2001 editions the hypothesis did not ask for.** What dates one item is the walk's own AXFR transcript, a resolver's answer carrying the zone SOA serial at the instant of transfer, plus the edition stamp in the filename, so `artifact_listing` with no human in the loop and no corroboration split; the class was never the problem. **Artifact existence is.** The published series is aggregates only, verified at its finest granularity: `ftp.ripe.net/ripe/hostcount/History/RIPE-Hostcount.98-Dec-09` is 6,907 B of a 101-row country table with no names, and the per-TLD per-month pages `www-static.ripe.net/pub/info/stats/hostcount/2001/06/uk/{all,zones,axfr,hosts,ratio}.cmp.html` are 838-12,813 B of attribute counts. The raw data is not lost but withheld: McGregor, Alcock and Karrenberg, "The RIPE NCC Internet Measurement Data Repository", PAM 2010, section 2.5, "The raw data from between 1990 and 2007 has been archived and is currently in off-line storage. However, the present policy is to discard the raw data after statistics have been extracted. This could be reversed if there was sufficient need amongst the research community", and, on any release, "Some administrators permit the RIPE scan, but this is often under the condition that only statistics, rather than raw data, are published." **Measurement, priced against merged260827 over the 97 RIPE-scanned TLDs carrying non-zero weight and held domains (2,936,405 held domains, TLD list read off the artifact's own Dec-1998 table), sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS: adjacent-year headroom held Y-1 and missing Y is 1997 2,002 EE, 1998 32,997, 1999 6,295, 2000 70,909, 2001 241,105, total 353,308 EE.** The naive held-any-missing-Y ceiling is 2,062,037 EE and must not be quoted. Per TLD across 1997-2001 adjacent: `de` 173,592 EE (980,570 names held 2000 and missing 2001), `dk` 25,322, `nl` 24,400, `uk` 20,209, `ch` 19,649, `it` 13,772, `se` 11,823, `at` 8,571, `no` 7,941, `be` 6,099. The high-weight TLDs want different editions: `uk` at 0.9813 is worth 17,543 EE from a **1998** edition and only 1,591 EE at 2001 because we already hold 2001 for nearly all held `.uk`, while `de`, `nl`, `ch` and `it` want **2001**. Walk size is not the constraint: Dec-2001 zone counts are `uk` 1,652,982, `de` 3,093,019, `nl` 529,457, `it` 418,539, all larger than our held sets. **Existence sweep, all negative against working controls:** ftp.ripe.net `/ripe/hostcount/{output,error-files}/` and `/ripe/hostcount/History/output/` 404 while `/ripe/hostcount/METHOD` is 200; ftp.uni-erlangen.de mirrors the same aggregates; **FUNET is a proved zero**, its stale `/pub/netinfo/ls-lR.test` (351,368 B) still lists a `hostcount` directory but HTTP 404s it and the live FTP listing of `/pub/netinfo/RIPE/` has no `hostcount` entry at all, while `dbase/` in the same session lists `ripe.db.gz`; 11 further European FTP archives had `/pub` fully enumerated and carry no RIPE or netinfo tree; archive.org `advancedsearch.php` returns numFound 0 for `hostcount` and for `RIPE hostcount` against a control of 19 for `ripe.net`; Wayback has no capture of `www.ripe.net/ripe/hostcount/`. **The national-contributor layer is dead**: `ftp.heanet.ie` answers "This site has stopped operations", and `ftp.nikhef.nl`, `ftp.nic.nl`, `ftp.iedr.ie`, `ftp.nominet.org.uk`, `ftp.denic.de`, `ftp.nic.at`, `ftp.nic.dk`, `ftp.nic.ch` and `ftp.nask.pl` are all NXDOMAIN. **New refusals to record, robots read in full before every first request: `ftp.tudelft.nl` names ClaudeBot explicitly alongside GPTBot and DeepSeek, and blanket `Disallow: /` at `ftp.rediris.es`, `ftp.uninett.no`, `ftp.fu-berlin.de`, `ftp.tu-chemnitz.de`, `ftp.uni-stuttgart.de`.** **Method, three reusable pieces. First: the surviving copy of a restricted central corpus is at the national contributor, not at the document mirrors, and it is filed under the contributor's name.** Grepping mirror indexes for `hostcount` found nothing; enumerating `/pub/doc/` on a large national archive found `ftp://ftp.icm.edu.pl/pub/doc/nask-hostcount/`, the RIPE hostcount raw output for `.pl` kept by NASK after RIPE restricted the central copy: `pl.output.gz` 63,809,385 B plus `old/` with 44 dated editions and `old/bad/` with 7 more. A 200 kB range request on `old/bad/pl.output.200212.gz` proves the layout is a raw AXFR transcript ("Asking zone transfer for pl ...", then `pl. 86400 IN SOA bilbo.nask.org.pl. ... 2002122003 ;serial (version)`), so it names hosts and self-dates and is master-eligible; it pays 0 here only because its earliest edition is 2002-12-30, one year outside the window. **Second: read the pricing question off the artifact's own table before hunting the artifact** - the free 6,907 B Dec-1998 aggregate names exactly which TLDs the instrument covered and how many zones it found, so the whole family priced against the store in one query at zero fetch cost. **Third: a range request on one `.gz` settles record layout for a 64 MB file.** Next step, and the only route left because every public copy is proved gone: one letter to `hostcount@ripe.net`, the address RIPE's own README names for this ("If you wish to view/use the raw data, please contact <hostcount@ripe.net>"), citing the PAM 2010 sentence that the discard policy "could be reversed if there was sufficient need". Same channel that granted `ripe_dbase_1999` on 2026-08-26. Ask for **2000 and 2001 first** (312,014 of the 353,308 EE), `de`/`nl`/`ch`/`it`/`dk` at 2001 and `uk` at 1998; expect a partial answer, since the paper says some administrators permitted the scan only on condition that statistics rather than raw data be published. State plainly that the off-line-storage claim is from 2010 and the project is now filed under archived-projects, so the tapes may not exist. **If the reply is negative the family is CLOSED**: the mirror, archive and national-contributor layers are all proved empty. Unfinished and not worth much: `ftp.man.torun.pl` is the last live Polish MAN host and was not checked for an older `nask-hostcount` mirror, but `.pl` at 0.1070 caps that thread near 2,600 EE. |
| **Zone-file NS right-hand sides, the nameserver hostname a delegation we already hold points AT rather than the delegated name itself (InterNIC `org`/`edu`/`gov`/`mil`/`root`/`arpa` at 1997, tomocha `edu`/`gov` at 1999) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 70.4 net-new post-split EE, and it dies on the yield screen after PASSING its own volume screen by 5x, which is the whole lesson.** Zero fetches and zero permission: the bytes were on disk, `data/raw/internic_zones/*.zone.gz`, 1,637,073 B. What dates one item is the zone's own SOA serial (`1997041800`, `1999112000`, `1999111901`) plus the IA capture fixing when the file existed, so an NS record is the registry serving that nameserver for that delegation at a stamped instant: `artifact_listing`, no human in the loop, no split. The class dates the item fine; the item is worth nothing. **Measurement: 160,258 NS records across the eight zones, 22,409 distinct RHS hostnames at 1997 and 7,418 at 1999, collapsing to 14,573 distinct registrable domains at 1997 and 4,452 at 1999, 16,231 over both.** The hypothesis guessed the file dies under about 3,000 distinct domains and pays at 15,000; it measured 14,573, so volume passed. `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` over 19,025 items: 19,024 distinct pairs over 16,231 domains, **18,822 already held**, pre-split 202 pairs and 119.2 EE (overstates 1.7x, do not quote), **post-split 120 pairs and 70.4 EE at mean weight 0.5864**, by year {1997: 63, 1999: 57}, by TLD {com 59, net 43, org 7, us 5, gov 1, in 1}; 82 pairs and 69 names to the candidate pool; typo upper bound 79.1% (159 of 201), itself a symptom, since a novel `ns2.` operator name is usually one edit from the `ns1.` we hold. An independent duckdb screen sampling **DISTINCT DOMAINS** reproduces it exactly: 1997 held-any 14,531 of 14,573 (99.71%), held **at** 1997 14,468 (99.28%), held-and-missing-1997 = 63; 1999 held-any 4,412 of 4,452 (99.10%), held at 1999 4,355 (97.82%), held-and-missing-1999 = 57; 63+57 = the 120 post-split pairs, so the two routes agree to the pair. **Not a by-construction zero**: `parse_internic_zone` (src/ark/sources.py:316) reads only the owner and explicitly discards the target, and the RHS has never been ingested. Per-TLD **P(lacks 1997 given held)**: com **0.0063** (5,697 held, 5,661 at 1997), net 0.0065, org 0.0015, edu 0.0007, uk 0.0051, and exactly 0.0000 for ca, us, gov, de, fr, se, nl, es, ch, au. On a 300-domain sample what already dates them at 1997 is `prior_task` 201 and **`isc_survey` 87**. **Method, and the reusable part is the screen this refutes.** The hypothesis priced itself off the RSS closure's fraction-of-held-missing at 1997, 0.8423, and argued a 1997 artifact over held names beats a 2001 one by 1.4x per name. Measured here that fraction is **0.0043, a 196x miss**, and 0.0063 for `com` against the 0.611 the 2001-threshold law quotes, a **97x** miss. So **fraction-of-held-missing is a population average over the store's names and does not survive conditioning on the population an artifact selects.** The conditioning is nameable in advance and costs no fetch: **the ISC 1997 survey is a reverse-DNS walk, so any 1997 population defined by "this host answers DNS" IS the ISC survey's own population, and nameserver operators are the maximal such set.** Same shape as the visitor-log and ping-directory closures at 98.4%, 99.6% and 9-of-9, and it generalises past them: it is not that the name comes from reverse DNS, it is that the artifact's selection predicate is a subset of a walk we already ran. **Pre-download discriminator to add to the held-fraction list: infrastructure-operator corpora sit at 99.3% held-AT-YEAR, above the 87-99% quoted for authority corpora**, and held-at-the-year is the statistic, not held-any (here they differ by 0.43 points and hide nothing). Second lesson: volume and yield are independent screens and volume is the weaker one - 14,573 domains cleared the gate by 5x and returned **0.0048 EE per domain** against the 0.386 expected for a held `com` name at 2001, an 80x shortfall. **Never let a domain count stand in for a yield estimate when the corpus is infrastructure.** Cost of the whole test: eight `gzip -dc | awk` calls and two `price_items.py` runs, no network at all. Closed: the `A` glue records in the same files are the same hostnames again, and `arpa`/`in-addr` targets are already measured at 336 pairs (src/ark/sources.py:320). A zone file dated **2001-2003** would escape the argument, P(lacks year given held) being 0.611 for com rather than 0.0063, but the reason none has been found is a licence, not an absence, and that is a different lead. |
| **Uptime and availability monitor target lists, the machine's record that a named host answered at a stamped instant (Netcraft uptime tables, MIDS/Matrix, WebSitePulse, InternetSeer, NetWhistle, Red Alert, WatchDog, SiteUptime, NetMechanic, AlertSite, Big Brother and NetSaint status displays) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 4.0 net-new post-split EE, and the evidence class was never the problem: it is master-eligible and clean.** What dates one item is the monitor's own machine-written stamp on the table it generated, `Generated on  5-Apr-2001` on `uptime.netcraft.com/up/today/*.html` and `Big Brother - Network status @ Fri Feb 13 13:16:21 WET 1998`, plus the replay instant, so `artifact_listing` plus `cdx_timestamp`, no human in the loop and no corroboration split. **Measurement: six artifacts all self-dated 2001, 130 distinct registrable domains sampled AS DOMAINS not rows. 123 of 130 (94.6%) already known, 111 of 130 (85.4%) already held AT 2001, and held-2000-missing-2001, the adjacent figure, is 10 of 130 (7.7%). Pre-split 19 pairs and 7.6 EE (do not quote), post-split 12 pairs and 4.0 EE, mean weight 0.3329, all at 2001 (nl 5, com 3, jp 2, org 1, net 1)**, priced with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against the live store. Per listed name that is **0.031 EE**, against 0.386 for a held `com` name at the 2001 threshold and 0.041 for the 2001 magazine archive, so this is the worst head-selected artifact measured to date. The `.com` arm alone: 67 distinct names, 63 held, 60 held at 2001, so **P(store lacks 2001 given held com) = 0.048 against the store-wide 0.611, a 12.8x shortfall.** Volume kills it before quality does: **the largest single published monitor artifact anywhere in the window is 52 names.** Operator arm, nine services probed by replay with no CDX: internetseer.com, which claimed to be the largest such service in the world, has its full 2001 navigation enumerated off `/home/` and it is ten marketing sections with no directory of any kind; netwhistle, redalert, watchdog.mycomputer and uptimemonitor expose login or signup only; siteuptime is an Oingo parking page in window so the service post-dates it; netmechanic puts results behind a control panel CGI. Netcraft's two surfaces with real volume, `/up/graph?site=` and `/up/hosted?netname=`, both redirect in-archive to netcraft's own `error/blocked.html`, so what had volume was robots-blocked to the 2001 crawler and what survives is top-50 by construction. Status-page arm via arquivo.pt `/textsearch` (its robots blocks `/wayback`, `/services`, `/cdxj` and `/datasets` for `*`, so textsearch was the only legal instrument): in-window hits Big Brother 279 and 235, NetSaint 110, control Apache 329,817, so availability is real, but of 29 returned items exactly ONE is a live display and it prints 13 monitored hosts as **bare labels with zero fully-qualified names**. **METHOD, four transferable parts. (1) The screen is published-over-managed and it costs one request: the monitored list IS the customer list, so what gets published is the showcase. WebSitePulse prints both numbers in one table, 27 linked customer sites beside "4134 accounts currently managed", a published fraction of 0.65%. Ask what fraction of the managed population reaches the page, never whether a list was published, because every operator publishes something. (2) A free substitute for CDX path enumeration that never touches the metered endpoint: fetch `/web/YYYYMMDD000000/URL` at a stride and read `%{url_effective}`, since replay redirects to the nearest capture. Twelve strides from 1998-01 to 2001-11 all landed on two timestamps, proving the Netcraft dailies survive as two editions rather than four years of them, at zero CDX cost. (3) The law: a server-generated page can be perfectly self-dating and still yield nothing, because the machine writes the LABEL, not the NAME.** Big Brother prints `ayron`, not `ayron.idt.ipp.pt`. Ask what name FORM a machine-written artifact emits before asking whether it is dated. This is the exact complement of the `mod_info`/`mod_status` closure of 2026-08-28, which had the right name form and no availability; together they close server-status output from both ends. **(4) Public monitoring output is head-selected BY CONSTRUCTION and Netcraft says so on its own `poweredby.html`**: only the most frequently requested sites appear, and the uptime page is a top 50. Do not re-test any of the nine operators or widen the Netcraft stride. Two adjacent things this did NOT close: MRTG and Big Brother per-host trees put the FQDN in the URL path, which is the right form and dated, but each tree is one organisation, so that needs a full-text archive index that is not arquivo.pt to find thousands at once; and the dated-recheck-log request at `docs/sources.md:569` survives untouched, since no operator here published an outage log. |
| **Crawler-indexed host tables, the roster of servers a crawler actually reached with per-host timestamps (FTP Search at NTNU, FAST, ftpsearch.lycos.com, AllTheWeb FTP tab, Filez/Zco, and the news.answers ftp-list) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 0.0 net-new post-split EE, and the family dies on POPULATION, not on era or on evidence.** The premise is false for both named members: a full navigation sweep, 10 replays and 77,621 B with no CDX touched, over the FTP Search home page, its advanced form and its entire `search-info/` subtree, the Lycos and AllTheWeb successors, and Filez plus its about page, found not one link to a server list. The only host list in the whole FTP Search document tree is `search-info/mirrors.html`, which lists 8 MIRROR SEARCH ENGINES rather than indexed servers. **Filez publishes the count and withholds the list**: "75+ million files, 7000+ FTP servers, servers are indexed weekly". `advancedsearch.php`: `ftpsearch` 6 unrelated items, `"ftp site list"` 0, `title:("anonymous ftp" AND list)` 3 items all 1993. The roster was the product and the product was never given away. Priced instead against the one surviving artifact of exactly this shape, already on disk at zero network cost: Perry Rovers, Anonymous FTP Sites Listing v5.2, 23 parts, 1,371,026 B, posted 1997-11-13. **Measurement, sampled as DISTINCT DOMAINS: 2,758 site entries. Reading A, whole list attested at its posting year 1997, gives 1,496 pairs over 1,496 domains, 1,491 already held (99.67%), pre-split 5 pairs and 2.1 EE (do not quote, it overstates by 21,490x), post-split 0 pairs and 0.0 EE. Reading B, each entry at its own `Date` field, gives 282 pairs, all 282 held, 0.0 EE.** **The zero is not an artefact of prior ingest**: the counterfactual dropping the `rtfm_faq` lineage (source_id 29) finds 996 of 997 whitelisted domains already dated 1997 by some other timestamped source, leaving exactly 1 worth 1.0 EE gross. **The number that closes the family is the 2001 threshold on this population: 1,486 of 1,496 are held at 2000 and only 30 are missing 2001, that is 2.0% against the `.com` population average of 61.1%.** So a listed anonymous-FTP host is worth **0.0078 gross EE**, Filez's own claimed 7,000-server roster would have been about 55 gross EE had it survived dated 2001, and 5,000 EE would need roughly 640,000 FTP hosts. Law 3 is visible in the TLD mix: 851 `.edu`, 430 `.com`, 201 `.de`, 122 `.gov`, 120 `.net`, 119 `.nl`, 100 `.au`, 99 `.ca`, 80 `.uk`, 75 `.jp`, 69 `.org`, 17 `.mil`. **METHOD, four reusable parts. (1) Ask whether a crawler service published its roster before hunting for the roster, and answer it in about ten replays: read the complete link set of the home page plus the whole help and about subtree at one capture each. Coverage was a marketing COUNT for every one of these services, so the shape "crawler's own record that a named host answered" reliably has no public artifact. (2) The population screen that kills the whole family is cheap and should run BEFORE the fetch: anonymous-FTP hosts are 97.4% already dated at 2001 and only 2.0% held-2000-missing-2001. Long-lived institutional servers are precisely the names every other source also carries, so a host roster is the worst possible shape under the 2001 threshold no matter how well dated. (3) A "last verified" date COLUMN is not a per-item date.** Rovers' list was posted 1997-11-13 and 88.2% of its rows are stamped 1994 or 1995, because the column records the last EDIT to the entry: only 326 of 2,758 rows carry an in-window date at all. Check the distribution of a date column before pricing any maintained register. **(4) The already-ingested counterfactual costs one SQL query** and should run before blaming an ingest for a zero: here it moved the answer by 1.0 EE, proving the corpus was worthless rather than merely spent. Positive controls held: 10 of 10 requested pages returned parseable era HTML three levels deep, and `archive.org/wayback/available` lied again, returning `{}` for `www.filez.com` at 19990601 while returning a 1999-04-28 capture of the SAME url at 20010601, so its zeros were discarded. Do not re-test any named member, and do not re-open Archie on the wrong-member argument: the era objection was never the binding one. The only untested surface is FTP Search RESULT pages, which do carry per-host `Date` columns, but the server is dead, few query URLs were captured, and screen (2) prices that population at 0.0078 gross EE per host regardless. Any host-roster idea worth an hour must target a NON-institutional population, for instance virtual-host tables from a mass shared-hosting provider, which is a different family. |
| **Online bookmark save streams, the row a user wrote by saving a page rather than by publicising it (Backflip public member folders, HotLinks guide, blink.com, itList, Clip2) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 0.5 net-new post-split EE against merged260827, and the interesting part is that the evidence class was perfect and the population still paid nothing.** What dates one item is master-eligible and exactly what the hypothesis wanted: Backflip's server printed a per-row `<span class="date">(added 2000/08/17)</span>` beside each saved URL and the replay instant fixes when that page existed, so `artifact_listing` plus `cdx_timestamp`, machine-written, no corroboration split. **Measurement: 291 dated rows off 50 member-folder pages (19 of 60 stride-sampled members), 248 distinct pairs over 244 distinct domains sampled AS DOMAINS. 237 of 244 (97.1%) already known; 240 of 248 (96.8%) already held AT THE ROW'S OWN SAVE YEAR. Pre-split 8 pairs and 4.4 EE (do not quote), post-split 1 pair and 0.5 EE, a single `.hk` at 0.4784.** Save years 2000: 188, 2001: 60. The counterfactual at the best year is 11 of 237 and 5.7 EE. **P(store lacks 2001 | `com` held) is 0.041 on this population against the store-wide 0.611, a 15x shortfall**; net 0.080, org 0.125, uk 0.000. Rate: 0.0041 net-new post-split pairs per listed domain against the curated-directory floor of 0.013 to 0.024, so **a save stream is 3 to 6x WORSE than a curated directory**. The second arm agrees and is worse: HotLinks guide, 26 leaf pages captured 2001-11-09 to 2001-12-18, 606 links, 499 distinct domains all dated 2001, **498 of 499 (99.8%) already held AT 2001, 0 pairs, 0.0 EE**, and the counterfactual at both 2001 and 2000 is exactly 0.0. **METHOD, and it is a new screen: the hypothesis's own discriminator is measurable before any bulk fetch, and it is refuted. Two compounding reasons.** (1) **The registrable-domain collapse destroys precisely what a bookmark contributes**: 60.9% of saved URLs are a path on a host, the tail lives at `geocities.com/SoHo/1234`, and the metric keeps `geocities.com`; the commonest saved domains are cjb.net, geocities.com, crosswinds.net, homestead.com, aol.com, netscape.com, yahoo.com, about.com, citysearch.com. (2) **An ACTIVITY date self-selects the year the store already covers**: the day a user saved a page is a day that site was most visible and most crawled, so the save year is the year LEAST likely to be missing, and the counterfactual at the modal save year 2000 is 2 of 237. **The consequence is a pre-fetch rule: for any corpus dated by human activity, do not price it at the population 2001 threshold (`com` 0.611); measure the held-at-2001 rate on a 200-domain sample first.** Backflip returns 0.041 and the CACM/IEEE citation set returned 0.044 with a completely different selector, an editor rather than a user. **That agreement is the finding: the selection principle does not matter, the domain collapse does, so "who chose this URL" is the wrong pre-fetch question and "what fraction of these hosts do we already hold AT THE TARGET YEAR" is the right one.** Reusable positives: a redirector is not automatically a kill, since Backflip hid targets behind `perl/go.pl?url=<id>` but the URL survived as display text truncated from the RIGHT in `<span class="location">` and in the `onmouseover` `window.status` string, so the registrable domain always survives. Operational, and it cost two false diagnoses: python `urllib` raised `[Errno 50] Network is down` on about half of all requests here while `curl` to the same URL succeeded, and `curl` then failed to connect to `web.archive.org:443` on about 40% of attempts at ~3.2s while `archive.org` answered every time; **neither is archive.org refusing us, so log the client error text before recording a source as unreachable.** Sampled via the public directory `pub_dir.ihtml?start=35|65..90`, 27 letter blocks, ~200 members per page, about 2 MB fetched in total; priced with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` on 2026-08-29. **Do not re-test at a larger sample: the reading is stable across n=26, n=94, n=255 and n=291 (98.8%, 96.4%, 96.8% held at the save year), and the best-case 0.0234 EE per distinct domain needs about 42,800 distinct domains, roughly 9,000 folder pages and 18,000 requests, against a namespace already 97.1% saturated.** Do not re-test blink.com (33,459 B, 2001-12-17), itlist.com (9,985 B, 2002-01-24) or clip2.com (2,290 B, 2002-01-21): none exposes a public-folder namespace at all. Still open and NOT closed by this: the dated recheck log this file asks for below, over a non-citation population; a bookmark service's own dead-link sweep would qualify, but Backflip's is not archived and its member pages carry no status column, so that lead needs a different host. |
| **Network Solutions' Dot Com Directory, a registry front end that should have rendered registry rows with creation dates in bulk (`dotcomdirectory.com`, `www.dotcomdirectory.net`, `kevdb.dotcomdirectory.net`, `www.dotcom.com`) (2026-08-29)** | **CLOSED at 2.3 net-new post-split EE against merged260827, and the family is settled by the operator's own FAQ before any question of enumeration.** What dates one item: nothing. The capture instant dates the page (`dated_directory`), but no row carries a registry date, and NSI states it itself: "No. The dot com directory database is completely separate from the WHOIS database." Listings are self-submitted by the business, so a novel name would take the corroboration split as well. **Measurement: 48 pages, 933,641 bytes, captures 2000-11-10 to 2001-12-01, 116 distinct registrable domains. `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds`: 117 pairs over 117 domains, 113 already held, pre-split 4 pairs and 3.0 EE (overstates 1.3x, do not quote), post-split 3 pairs and 2.3 EE at mean weight 0.7775, com 1 / au 1 / org 1, typo upper bound 50.0% (2 of 4). An independent duckdb screen sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS agrees to 0.03 EE: 115 of 116 held any year (99.1%), 112 already carrying 2001 (96.6%), held-and-missing-2001 = 3, all 3 adjacent (held 2000).** The corpus is single-year so pairs and distinct domains are 1:1 and the per-row sampling trap cannot bite. This is a population ceiling and not just a sample: the only enumerable surface is 15 top business categories and the result pages behind them were never captured. **It dies three independent ways.** (1) **Not the registry.** The public Dot Com Directory is a white-labelled InfoSpace yellow pages (`infospace.com/v.w/yp.htm`, InfoSpace patent notice in the footer) keyed on BUSINESSES: company name, business type, city, state, ticker. Listings are self-submitted and processed "within 10 business days", and no creation date appears anywhere. The real registry data was a paid product: `dotcom.com/services/index.html` sells "over 15 million registered domain names ... organized into a customer database of over 5 million unique customers" as Data Services, never published. (2) **The namespace does not descend.** The 15 categories are real `<a href>` links and 1 of 3 tested replays (Automotive, 27,641 B, memento 2001-05-30), but the subcategory step is a `<select name=QPAR>` inside a GET form, not a link, so no crawler followed it: `www.dotcomdirectory.net/nsi.com/dc/result.htm` has **zero captures in any year** while `nsi.com/index.htm?nl=1` has **44 in 2001**, so the archive holds the search form 44 times over and the results never. (3) **A session token sits in the PATH**, `dotcomdirectory.net/_1_4JNMTU203YD2CNL__nsi.com/...`, differing per capture, so even a captured result page would sit at an unguessable path. The aggregate pages behave exactly as the dates-without-names law below predicts: `facts/quickstats.html` gives activation percentages by month 1998-2000 and no name at all, and `profiles/f1000.html` is the head-corpus law in one page (amazon.com, expedia.com, disney.com, petsmart.com). **METHOD.** (a) **The pre-download question for any "registry front end" is answered by the operator's own FAQ.** One static, always-crawled `help.html` (18,355 B at memento 2001-05-04, 18,812 B at 2001-02-02, the WHOIS-separation sentence identical in two independent captures) settled a family that looked like `whois_creation` and would otherwise have cost a crawl of the whole browse namespace. Ask "is this the registry database?" BEFORE "does it enumerate?"; the hypothesis's own discriminator was the second question and the first one killed it, since this namespace does enumerate, one level, and still pays nothing because the rows are self-submitted business listings. (b) **`archive.org/wayback/available` produces false zeros in two distinct ways and both bit here**: it returns only status-200 captures, so it reported the `kevdb` root as a 2015 capture while the sparkline proved five 2001 captures (3xx/4xx) existed, and it returned `{"archived_snapshots":{}}` for every query-string URL that direct replay then served. Two clean negatives would have been filed as "not captured". **Cross-check with `https://web.archive.org/__wb/sparkline?output=json&collection=web&url=<urlencoded>`, which returns per-year and per-month counts plus a per-month status string, then confirm with a direct `/web/<ts>id_/` replay; neither endpoint is `/cdx`, so both are free while collectors meter it.** Sparkline is exact-URL, so it screens a known path but cannot enumerate a host. (c) **A session token in the path is a namespace killer and it is visible for free**: fetch the entry point once and check whether its own links carry a per-visit token; if they do, stop, because the archive's copies are scattered across unguessable paths and only a full URL index can recover them. (d) `curl -sL` on `/web/<ts>id_/` follows archived 302s into the real captured session path, which is how the Automotive page was reached; read the final `Memento-Datetime`, never `%{url_effective}`. (e) **Watch for content belonging to another site**: the 2000-12-03 and 2000-12-06 captures of both `dotcomdirectory.com` and `www.dotcomdirectory.com` serve Vicinity Corporation's homepage (18,555 B), and a byte count alone would have scored those as live directory pages. **Do not re-test `dotcomdirectory.com`, `dotcomdirectory.net`, `kevdb.dotcomdirectory.net`, `dotcom.com`, or the browse namespace.** `dotcomdirectory.com` is a different business after mid-2001 (Qwest Dex, redirecting to `dotcomdirectory.i411.com`), also a yellow pages, also worth nothing, and the rest of the family is already closed below: NSI and InterNIC registration reports (dates without names) and `rs.internic.net` with the six InterNIC zones. The one thread left belongs to a different lens: NSI sold the registry as bulk **Data Services** to marketers, so the question is not where NSI published it but which BUYER republished an extract, which is a paper-supplement or marketing-dataset hunt, not a registry hunt. |
| **Search engine transaction logs, the row where the user typed the domain into the box (Excite 1997-09-16 / 1999-12-20 / 2001-05-04, AlltheWeb 2001, Fireball 1998) (2026-08-29)** | **BLOCKED on access, and priced anyway at roughly 700 to 2,100 EE for the single best file, so the whole family sits under the 5,000 EE bar and is worth exactly one email.** What dates one item is master-eligible and clean: the engine's own log line stamps the instant the string was submitted, so the artifact is `artifact_listing` at a fixed calendar date; the string is human-typed, so every domain takes the corroboration split and only names another source has already dated can carry a year. The four in-window logs exist and are listed at `faculty.ist.psu.edu/jjansen/academic/transaction_logs.html` (3,134 B, robots.txt 404), but the page's only access route is "Please email me, Jim Jansen (jjansen@acm.org), I will place the file(s) on an ftp site for you." **No public copy exists, by enumeration:** DataCite `query log AND (excite OR altavista OR search engine)` 26 records and none in-window; Zenodo 0; HuggingFace 0 for archive-query-log / excite query log / aql-22 / query log 1999; GitHub repository search `excite query log` 0; archive.org `advancedsearch.php` (not scrape, per the trap) numFound 4 for `excite query log`, 1 for `excite transaction log`, 0 for `title:(query logs) AND mediatype:(data)` against live controls `title:(excite) AND mediatype:(data)` 10 and `search engine query log` 556, no hit a log. The one large public sibling, the Webis Archive Query Log (1999-2022, 356M queries), **refuses us by name**: `webis.de/robots.txt` carries `User-agent: ClaudeBot` / `Disallow: /` and the data host `files.webis.de` is a 27 B `Disallow: /` for every agent. **THE FAME LAW IS THE RESULT, and it is a store-side screen that costs one query and no download.** P(a held `.com` has 2001) rises with the number of DISTINCT pre-2001 sources that saw it: nsrc=1 0.1733 (n=2,395,370), 2 0.3005, 3 0.2935, 4 0.5424, 5 0.6638, 6 0.7803, 7 0.8498, 8 0.9408, 9 0.9834, **10+ 0.9966 (n=8,329)**; same shape on years-held, `.com` held in all five of 1996-2000 has P(2001)=0.7395 against 0.1988 for one pre-year. **So the 0.611 miss rate is a population average that does not survive conditioning on fame, and a search box is a fame filter.** Head spot-check, 273 hand-listed domains a 1999-2001 user would actually type (portals, retailers, media, ISPs, games, `.co.uk`): 273 of 273 known, 273 of 273 attested in window, **271 of 273 already held AT 2001**, total post-split value 1.34 EE = **0.0049 EE per name**, 79x below the 0.386 the hypothesis assumed and 8x below the 0.041 the magazine archive measured; median distinct pre-2001 sources per name 12, with 87.2% in the nsrc>=10 bucket. Surrogate actually fetched and measured, the only free member of the family, AOL 2006 file 02 (`cim.mcgill.ca/~dudek/206/Logs/...`, 45,909,232 B, robots.txt 404): 3,515,952 queries, 592,697 distinct terms, **664,227 queries (18.89%) carry a hostname token and 192,948 distinct registrable domains** under a weighted TLD. The transfer is licensed on a published statistic, not asserted: AOL's top-100 share 18.2% and singleton rate 65.3% sit on Excite 2001's own published 22.0% and 61.7% (`ieee_computer.pdf` Table 2). Priced on DISTINCT DOMAINS against merged260827-2: 68,833 known (35.7%), 67,823 attested in window, 124,115 never seen and therefore candidate-pool only, earning no year; dated 2001 that is 20,724 post-split pairs and **13,267.4 EE (0.0688 EE per distinct domain, 0.01997 per URL-shaped query)** against a gross of 123,445.3, a **9.3x** overstatement, do not quote the gross. On the clean adjacent-year screen (held 2000, missing 2001): 17,530 pairs and **11,208.7 EE, 0.01687 EE per URL-shaped query**, with only 29.6% of held-2000 typed domains lacking 2001 against the 0.611 population figure, exactly the halving the fame law predicts. Value is flat across frequency bands (head >=10 0.0674 EE per name, middle 0.0866, tail 0.0604) because the tail is 73% unknown names that earn nothing. **Projection for the blocked artifact:** Excite 2001 is 1,025,910 queries (Jansen Table 1) at a URL-shaped rate of 4% to 12% (AOL's 18.89% is a ceiling, since navigation queries were still climbing through the window), so **700 to 2,100 EE, plausibly 1,000 to 4,000** once corrected for an in-window log's higher attested fraction. The 1999 log is worth an eighth (1998-to-1999 adjacent headroom on the same set is 1,296.0 EE) and the two 1997 logs less again. **The hypothesis's own kill criterion was the wrong one:** it said "under about 3,000 distinct domains it dies on volume", and volume is fine at 192,948 distinct domains from 3.5M queries; what caps the family is that a typed name is a famous name and a famous name already carries 2001. **ROBOTS SLIP, recorded so nobody repeats it:** one request went to `zenodo.org/api/records?q=...`, which `zenodo.org/robots.txt` disallows under `Disallow: /api` (only `/api/records/*/files` is allowed). It returned 0 hits and nothing was collected. Zenodo is effectively closed to programmatic search since `/search` is disallowed too: **use `api.datacite.org` instead, which serves no robots.txt at all and covers Zenodo's DOIs.** Not closed, but not worth agent time: the only remaining move is one email from a human to jjansen@acm.org asking for `Excite_2001` ALONE, since the other five files are an order of magnitude thinner, and the reply is an ftp drop. If it arrives, take hostname-shaped tokens from field 3, collapse to registrable domains, price with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` at 2001, and expect the artifact-listing class to need a `Decision:` line before any year is banked. **Do not re-test the Webis Archive Query Log or any webis.de mirror (refused by name), nor AOL, MSN, Sogou or Yandex logs (all 2006 or later, out of window).** |
| **Distributed-computing client submission stats, the project's own accounting of which address sent work today (`stats.distributed.net`, RC5-56 / RC5-64 / OGR dailies) (2026-08-29)** | **BLOCKED at 0 net-new post-split EE, and the kill is documented by the operator's own file: `stats.distributed.net/robots.txt` carries `User-agent: *` / `Disallow: /` at 19981203123429, 19990124000825 and 20010531224428, and `rc5stats.distributed.net` the same at 20010602021116, so the archiver was forbidden across the whole target window.** `www.distributed.net/robots.txt` at 20010602051535 blocks only five paths, so the block was specific to the stats hosts, not the project. What could have dated an item was sound in shape, the daily table's own date over the project's machine record of blocks received that day (`dated_directory`), but no such table was ever captured, so the claim is untestable rather than refuted. Everything reachable was pulled and priced: 5 pages, 39,101 B, via `web.archive.org` `/<ts>id_/` replay and never the live host. `stats.distributed.net/` at 19981212021247 (8,674 B), 19990208020539 (8,671 B) and 20001109082400 (1,223 B, a maintenance page); `/teamlist.html` at 19990202042932 (7,931 B); `rc5stats.distributed.net/` at 20010331062838 (12,602 B). **Priced with `price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: 5 distinct domains, 10 pairs, 10 already held (100%), net-new 0 pairs and 0.0 EE before AND after the split, and 0 names new even to the candidate pool.** A strong zero: the five are `best.com` (an ISP footer), `w3.org` (the DTD), `rsac.org` (the PICS label), `distributed.net` and `d.net`, page chrome only, not one participant address among them. Every page carrying participant identity is absent: `/emtop100.html`, `/emyst100.html`, `/tm_top100.html`, `/tm_yst100.html`, `/oslist.html`, `/cgi/emsearch.idc`, `/cgi/teamcatlist.idc?CAT=Company`, and on `rc5stats` the whole 2001 tree `/rc5-64/`, `/ogr-24/`, `/CSC/` plus `tpemail.php3`, `psummary.php3`, `top100.php3`, `summary.php3`, `tpdomain.php3`, `country.php3`. **22 URLs probed, 0 hits, and it is a PROVED zero because controls fired in the same minutes**: `/index.html` 19990129071440, `/teamlist.html` 19990202042932, `/cpulist.html` 19981201205349, `/robots.txt` 20010531224428, `rc5stats` 20010331062838, `desstats` 20010331061054, `n0cgi` 20010515214911, `www.distributed.net/` 20010529011833, `/rc5/` 20010603092029, `/statistics/` 19991022065859. **The volume premise is refuted at the source, not merely unmeasured:** the 1998-99 navigation shows the site only ever published "Top 100 Overall" and "Top 100 Yesterday" plus a one-field `.idc` search form, so a browsable list of "a few hundred thousand participants" was never a page. Ceiling in the counterfactual where the crawler had been let in: 100 rows per project per day, stable day to day, so two years of 2000-2001 dailies is low thousands of distinct addresses and they are the head, where 2001 coverage is already best; at the measured head-selected rate of 0.041 EE per name even a generous 10,000 addresses is ~410 EE, **12x under the bar before law 5 takes the split**. No item-side copy either: `advancedsearch.php` gives `distributed.net` 11 items, `distributed.net stats` 1, `RC5-64` 11, `"distributed computing" AND rc5` 0, `dnetc` 3, all client source, a conference talk or an unrelated disc, and the only in-window hit `tucows_205891_distributed.net_Client` (2001) is a client binary, the container family already closed above. **Method, three parts, and the first is the reusable one. (1) Fetch the TARGET HOST'S PERIOD `robots.txt` out of Wayback before concluding anything about capture coverage**: a dynamic site whose deep pages are missing looks identical to a site the crawler was told to skip, and the archived file separates them in one request, here turning a guess into a documented 1998-2001 operator refusal. Run it for every "Wayback only has the front page" family. (2) **`archive.org/wayback/available` enumerates capture coverage without touching `/cdx`**, so it composes with a metered cdx collector; probe URL shapes read off an early capture's own navigation bar rather than guessed shapes, and interleave known-positive controls so the zero is proved. (3) **Read the era's navigation to size the artifact before costing a crawl**: the nav said "Top 100" and "Search", which caps the family at 100 rows a day and refutes a six-figure population without fetching one data page. **Live-host refusal, recorded:** `stats.distributed.net/robots.txt` today names `ClaudeBot`, `Claude-User` and `Claude-SearchBot` with `Disallow: /`, plus `User-agent: *` / `Disallow: /` and `Content-Usage: ai=n`. **Zero requests were made to it**; everything came from `web.archive.org` and `archive.org`, no CDX spent. Closed: **do not re-test distributed.net, SETI@home or any volunteer-computing participant stats on any screen**, since all published head-truncated leaderboards behind a query form and all had reason to keep crawlers off a database-backed stats box. One adjacent thread is NOT this family and stays open: `www.distributed.net` itself was crawlable and its `/discussion/lists.html` archives are a raw-mbox population, the same unfollowed thread flagged elsewhere in this file, that a raw mbox keeps the `Received:` chain pipermail strips. |
| **Apache `mod_info` and `mod_status` vhost lists, a shared host's own config page naming every site on the box (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE against merged260827, on measured in-window availability of 0: no rendered `/server-info` or `/server-status` page from 1996-2001 exists in any reachable archive.** Had one been captured the dating would have been sound and split-free (capture instant over a machine-written config dump, `cdx_timestamp` plus `artifact_listing`, no human in the loop), so this class dies on availability, not on dating. Instrument was arquivo.pt `/textsearch` plus `/textextracted`, because **CDX cannot search by path across hosts at all** (SURT-sorted by host, `url` is mandatory), so the hypothesis's own proposed screen is not a supported CDX operation on any archive. Control passed: in-window "Apache" 329,817 hits, "server-status" 1,475, "Total accesses" 7,771, and 4-word phrase queries return nonzero ("Apache Server Status for" = 2). Against that working control the strings emitted ONLY by rendered output score zero: "Configuration Phase Participation" 0, "Request Phase Participation" 0, "seconds since last request" 0; the 2 hits for "Apache Server Information" and all 477 for "server-info" are the shipped Apache manual or unrelated source and RFCs, never live output. Second instrument archive.org `advancedsearch.php` (not scrape, per the trap): "httpd.conf" 41 items, "server-status" 155, all 2017-2026 GitHub and perma.cc mirrors, zero in-window. Third instrument unavailable: `data.commoncrawl.org` and `vefsafn.is` are `Disallow: /` and the Bibliotheca Alexandrina IA mirror no longer resolves. Ceiling had it existed, sampled over DISTINCT DOMAINS (7,023,420 held `.com`): P(store lacks 2001 | held) = 0.5967, so 0.3772 EE per held name and 2,651 names per 1,000 EE (`.net` 0.6369 and 3,466), which at tens to low hundreds of vhosts per box is roughly 10 to 50 captured 2001 pages from large hosters. **The reusable part is the refutation, and it is a two-request screen to run BEFORE any "unprotected endpoint" hypothesis: read the version-contemporary manual and ask whether the default was opt-in and whether anything linked to it.** The in-window Apache 1.3 documentation refutes the premise in its own words. mod_info: "This module is not compiled into the server by default ... To enable it, add the following line to the server build Configuration file, and rebuild the server", so exposure required recompiling and the population is not shared hosters but the few admins who rebuilt and then declined the `<Limit>` clause the same page recommends. mod_status: compiled in, but `ExtendedStatus Off` is the documented default and the VHost column is available only with it On, and the enabling recipe the docs teach is itself `Order Deny,Allow / Deny from all / Allow from .foo.com`. On top of that both endpoints are unlinked and 1996-2001 crawls were link-driven, so even an exposed one was structurally unreachable by the instrument that would have preserved it. Evidence retrieved instead: `arquivo.pt/textextracted?m=http://math.ist.utl.pt/manual/mod/mod_info.html/19990427125624` (1,957 B, captured 1999-04-27) and `.../lur.df.fct.unl.pt/manual/mod/mod_status.html/20010228004951` (3,084 B, captured 2001-02-28). **arquivo.pt is itself the transferable tool**: a genuine full-text instrument over 1996-2001, robots-clean, no key, 5 s crawl-delay, and it does not touch the metered IA endpoints, so it is the right answer to any "does this page shape exist in the window" question. Its robots.txt is the CLAUDE.md trap in pure form, the `User-agent: *` Disallow lines sitting at lines 744-753 of 753 after about 726 `Allow:` lines, so reading the head would have shown only permissions. The one sibling the run proposed as untried, webserver statistics pages (Analog, Webalizer, pwebstats) whose referrer blocks are `link_source`, **is already closed the same day at 0 EE and 141 of 141 pairs held at the artifact's own year** (row above), so its 7,771 in-window hits are not a lead. Do not re-test either family. |
| **The TARGET column of a vanity-URL redirection table (V3.com, `come.to`, `i.am`, `go.to`, `cjb.net`, `shorturl.com`, OCLC PURL), where the directory turns out to enumerate handles and not targets (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE. Harvested off an INTERIM measurement: the run pulled and read the artifacts but its pricing pass did not finish, so the 0 is the standing figure and the decisive result is the dating argument, not a priced yield.** What dates one item: only the Wayback capture instant over V3's own machine-written META-Refresh or frameset naming the target, **so a resolved handle page dates its target and a browsable directory page dates nothing but handles.** That is what kills the volume premise the hypothesis rested on. V3 advertised millions of registered handles, but the datable unit is one resolved handle capture naming one target hostname, which is one request per candidate name, the worst request-to-name ratio in the register and strictly worse than querying, which adds ~3,000 EE/hour. Artifact: V3's whole public surface, archived and pulled, 64 HTML objects and 773,206 B. `web.archive.org/web/20010204191900/http://www.v3.com/Search.asp` (14,324 B) carries the search schema and the only bulk enumeration V3 ever published, **its own 92-name domain roster**, which is not a target list at all but the operator's own portfolio, the identical shape closed the same day as the free-mail signup menus at 0.041 EE per listed domain, pricing 92 names at about 3.8 EE. `coolsites.asp` at 20000711034600 / 20010130110700 / 20010602022936 / 20011122075506 (20,847 / 18,942 / 23,000 / 22,180 B) is the other listing V3 published and it is a hand-picked cool-sites page, so it is head-selected and takes the corroboration split. Also pulled: `cjb.net` at 20011217200201 (12,828 B), `shorturl.com` at 20001206215900 (11,213 B), `purl.oclc.org/` at 19991103221055 (5,888 B) with `maint/display.html`, `maint/search_domain.html` and `maint/power.html`, plus 42 resolved handle captures. **Method, and it is the MARC 856 trap in a new costume: in a handle-to-target service the creation stamp on a row dates the HANDLE, never the target it points at, so the row's own date is worthless and the whole class collapses onto per-capture replay.** Generalised screen, two requests and no download: for any indirection service, fetch one captured directory page and ask whether it prints the target hostname or only the handle. V3, cjb.net and PURL all print handles, which is the answer for the family. The Bomis webring result (a hub prints the bare hostname as text even where the anchor goes through its own redirector) was the reason to test rather than assume, and it does not transfer: a webring hub lists members it describes, while a redirector lists handles it sells. Not re-testable on effort: the missing pricing pass could only have moved a figure bounded above by ~3.8 EE on the one enumeration that exists. |
| **Free-mail signup domain menus (Mail.com, iName, MyOwnEmail, EveryOne.net, Zzn, PoBox and seven more operators), where the dating is clean and the WHOLE FAMILY is 531 names (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 22.0 net-new post-split EE over 50 Wayback captures 1998-2001 from 12 operators, so 0.041 EE per listed domain, which puts the 5,000 EE bar at ~120,000 listed domains in a family whose total measured population is 531.** What dates one item is master-eligible and needs no corroboration split: a signup `<option>` list is written from the operator's own live mail-domain table, so the capture timestamp fixes an instant when each name was accepting mail (`artifact_listing` on `cdx_timestamp`, machine-written). The dating is sound and the VOLUME and the YEAR SCREEN both kill it. Priced on DISTINCT DOMAINS against merged260827: 804 pairs over 531 distinct registrable domains, **518 held in some in-window year = 97.6%**, 51 pairs / 30.8 EE gross against 36 pairs / **22.0 EE** post-split (do not quote the gross), by year {1998: 5, 1999: 3, 2000: 2, 2001: 26}, by TLD {com 32, net 4}. **The 2001 threshold run backwards is the screen: 365 domains listed at 2001, 352 already held, held-AND-missing-2001 = 26, so 94.8% of the held names ALREADY CARRY the artifact's own year** against the 61.1% `com` population miss rate, and the typo upper bound is 26 of 48 sampled net-new names one edit from a held name, so even the 22.0 is soft. Largest single portfolio ever built is 241 (iName @19980111131221), hand-counted against the extractor, with MyOwnEmail 171 and Mail.com 92 also hand-checked; MyOwnEmail's advertised "HUNDREDS MORE" resolves to one register page holding all ten category selects at 171 names. **The partner-domain arm the hypothesis named as the source of volume is REFUTED, not untested**: EveryOne.net's white-label partners are enumerated nowhere, its 45,381 B signup page's 401 `<option>` tags are countries and company-size bands, and the two pages that looked like listings are a curated web-host link directory (88 domains, 72 held, 7.2 EE) which is the already-closed dated-directory family. **The general law, applicable BEFORE download to any "portfolio of a company that registered many names" idea: an operator's vanity-domain portfolio is a head-of-distribution population BY CONSTRUCTION,** because the words that make a saleable address (`doctor.com`, `lawyer.com`, `london.com`) are exactly the words registered earliest and held through the whole window, so the corpus scores 97.6% held and already carries its own year. Same shape as the magazine-article result. Method worth reusing: **(1) neither CDX nor `/wayback/available` is needed for a dated capture** since `web.archive.org/web/<ts>id_/<url>` 302s to the nearest one and `curl -L -w '%{url_effective}'` hands back the real timestamp, one request for lookup and fetch, which mattered because `/wayback/available` returned a hard 429 with `x-rl: 0` and no `Retry-After` for the whole run while the replay host answered on try 1 or 2 for all 50 fetches (it refuses the TCP connection about a third of the time, so a retry wrapper is mandatory); **(2) pre-screen any "the operator's own table" hypothesis by counting `<option>` tags before extracting anything**, one grep separates a domain menu from a country dropdown; **(3) `grep -a` or the screen is a lie**, half these 1998-2001 captures are Non-ISO extended ASCII and plain grep reports nothing on them, which is indistinguishable from a page with no links (caught on everyone.net, zzn.com and usa.net). Zero CDX spent; archive.org robots.txt read in full first, no Claude agent named. **Do not re-test freemail or vanity-address domain menus on any operator in any year, nor the white-label partner arm: those lists were never published.** |
| **Public bug trackers where the mail headers survive: `debian-bugs-dist` gone, and the continuous-list law (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 6.5 net-new post-split EE over 3,655 messages from 17 weekly mbox files, so 0.00178 EE per message, which puts 5,000 EE at about 2.8M in-window messages against the 78,000 the hypothesis budgeted: 36x off.** The artifact survives and the POPULATION kills it. What dates one item is master-eligible and clean: the last-hop `Received:` line written by hub.freebsd.org, restated by the archive's own weekly filename, 4,858 of 4,858 messages agreeing. Priced by `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against the live store on DISTINCT DOMAINS, re-derived from the streams rather than taken from the researcher's summary: 2001 (14 files, `freebsd-bugs`/`chat`/`current`/`hackers`, 2,036 msgs) 1,013 domains, **927 already held at the artifact's own year = 91.5%**, 8 pairs / **3.7 EE** post-split against 86 pairs / 43.7 EE gross, a **12.0x** overstatement, DO NOT QUOTE; 1996 (3 files, `freebsd-questions`, 1,619 msgs) 684 domains, 677 held = **99.0%**, 6 pairs / **2.8 EE**. The eight 2001 survivors are `au` 2, `il` 1, `dk` 1, `pl` 1, `ru` 1, `org` 1 at mean weight 0.4568, and the **typo upper bound is 51.2%**, so half of even those are junk. **THE 2001 THRESHOLD RUN BACKWARDS IS THE SCREEN.** CLAUDE.md prices a held `com` name in a 2001 artifact at 0.386 EE because P(store lacks 2001 | held) = 0.611; this corpus is **8.5% missing 2001 against that 61.1%, 7.2x worse**, and the 1996 arm is worse still at 99.0% held. **So the killer is CONTINUOUS OPERATION: a technical list that ran unbroken from 1994 dates its own population every year, names only the continuously-attested authority head, and leaves no year-gap to sell.
