The longer the list ran, the more completely its names are already dated.** Ask how long a mail corpus ran before pricing it; prefer one that STOPPED, or one whose senders are not its subscribers. The hypothesis's flagship is a **proved zero, not a wrong path**: `lists.debian.org/debian-bugs-dist/` answers HTTP 200 with "This mailing list has no archive" and no `.txt.gz`, while the positive control `/debian-devel/2001/01/` is a 75,727 B MHonArc index, so the scheme works and the archive is genuinely absent; `bugs.debian.org/robots.txt` (239 B whole) gives named crawlers `bugreport.cgi` then `User-agent: *` / `Disallow: /`, a blanket refusal; the only bulk capture anywhere, `warc_home-grab-grabs-_debian-bugs_20171114`, was settled by reading its **4.3 MB CDX instead of the 859 MB WARC** (99,908 captures of bugs 781,992-881,900, every one stamped 2017, out of window by sixteen years); KDE and GNOME Bugzilla store parsed comments, so no `Received:` chain and zero addresses to an anonymous reader. **Reusable and worth keeping although it paid nothing here:** `docs.freebsd.org/cgi/getmsg.cgi?fetch=0+0+archive/<year>/<list>/<YYYYMMDD>.<list>+archive` returns a whole weekly mbox as `text/plain` with headers intact, ~60 lists 1994-2026, one request per week-file and no crawling, and its robots.txt is 52 B read whole disallowing only `/statistic` and `/doc/`. Two corrections to the researcher's own log, both verified against the data on disk: the density is **0.498 domains per message at 2001 and 0.422 at 1996, not the 0.275 reported** (2.6x worse than the spam corpus's 1.30, not 4.7x), and these mboxes carry **two `^From ` lines per message**, so a naive `grep -c` doubles the message count. |
| **The hostnames a 2001 program was compiled to contact: 130 Tucows payloads opened, and the container law (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0.6 net-new post-split EE over 130 archives and 137 MB, so 0.0046 EE per archive; the whole in-window Tucows corpus of 11,499 items, ~12.1 GB and ~23,000 requests, projects to 53 EE on the admissible reading and 241 EE if you wrongly credit every build-written string, 94x and 20x under the 5,000 EE bar.** Every prior catalogue closure in this file measured the INDEX (Winsite's `INDEX.TXT`, SimTel's CD indexes, Download.com, all zero vendor domains); nobody had opened a payload, so the read was genuinely new. What dates one item: the archive's own catalogue release `date`, already master-eligible and banked as `tucows_catalogue`, which can date a string the BUILD wrote and cannot date the bundled documentation where 94% of the hostnames actually live. Four arms sampled as DISTINCT ITEMS then priced on DISTINCT DOMAINS against merged260827: random stratified 1998-2001 (20 items, 22 domains, 0.6 EE), title-matched net/web/ftp/mail/irc/server 2000-2001 (40 items, 554 domains, 7.3 EE), random all-2001 (40 items, 219 domains, 1.5 EE), titles that should SHIP a server list (30 items, 125 domains, 1.47 EE); union 130 items, **804 distinct domains, 791 held in some in-window year = 98.4%**, held-and-missing the artifact's own year 20 pairs / 10.90 EE gross, 12 of the first 15 with Y-1 held, only 8 domains absent from the store entirely. That reads as a small clean find and the attribution pass destroys it. **Bucketing every hostname by the MEMBER FILE it came from: binary (.exe/.dll/.prc/.scr) 47 distinct hostnames, installer and config (.ini/.conf/.inf) 22, bundled documentation (readme/changelog/credits/FAQ/configure) 824, other bundled text 1,016. Build-written 68 against 1,069 in text the build did not write, a factor of 15.7, and only 40 of 130 archives hold a single build-written hostname, i.e. 0.52 per archive, below the hypothesis's own stated kill line of one per archive.** And the bundled text cannot take the release date: 11 of the 15 held-missing pairs in the first 60 archives come from two files, `dpsftp-0.6.1/doc/X-FAQ` (a copy of the comp.windows.x FAQ shipped inside a 2000-dated Java FTP client, whose icomsim.com and sunbim.be the store's own record contradicts, both last held 1998) and PTlink 6.3.7's `doc/example.conf`, `src/version.c` and `src/test/data/match.data`, a shipped EXAMPLE config and a wildcard-matching UNIT TEST FIXTURE, so those hostnames were never contacted by anything. Killer 2 in its purest form. Priced on the admissible arm alone (binary plus installer and config strings, the 40 archives that have any): 66 distinct domains, 95.5% held, 3 net-new pairs / 2.1 EE, and after removing PTlink's example-config names and the `example.es` placeholder **exactly ONE admissible pair survives 130 archives**, `awesomescreens.com` 2001 at weight 0.6321, verified independently in the store as held 2000 and missing 2001, and it is the hyphen-free sibling of `awesome-screens.com` which the already-banked `creator` field carries at 2001. The ceiling is structural, not sampling: the 24 build-only hostnames over the first 100 archives are the vendor's own site, the four shareware payment processors (kagi, regsoft, shareit, handango), the platform giants, two freemail hosts, an InstallShield template placeholder (`www.mywebsite.com`, `www.server.com`, `www.different.com`) and one binary-corruption token (`www.anaja.comaxzb`); kagi.com, shareit.com, hotmail.com, yahoo.com, bigfoot.com, aladdinsys.com, xs4all.nl all carry the full 1996-2001 span. Two closed head-of-distribution populations: the vendor's own domain, which `tucows_catalogue` already banked at that very year, and a dozen processors shared across thousands of items. The designed best case returned the same rate, 20 build-written hostnames over 30 server-tool archives, and no FTP or IRC client in the sample shipped a site list. **Method, four parts, and the third is the reusable one.** (1) The payload of a dated archive is two requests (`/metadata/<id>` then `/download/<id>/<file>`) and a 7z pass gets inside self-extracting exes, cabs and nested tars, taking readable file count from 1 to 30 on an InstallShield exe and 3 to 45 on a Palm zip. (2) **Anchor the hostname regex on `http://`, `www.` or `@`, never on name shape, when the input is binary**: `price_items --all-tlds` over raw binary strings reported 178 "domains" from 20 archives of which 158 were byte noise (`2.fj`, `zy7.bh`) or filenames (`.zip`, `normal.dot`); anchored extraction gave 22. (3) **For any corpus made of CONTAINERS rather than RECORDS, the container's date dates only what the container's own build wrote, so bucket by member file BEFORE pricing and price the buckets apart.** Bundled documentation, example configs, autoconf boilerplate and test fixtures ride along at a 15.7x volume advantage and read as a find until you ask which file they came from. This applies unchanged to ISOs, distro packages, installer bundles and document attachments. (4) **When the corpus's index is already ingested, the payload's dominant yield is zero by construction**: check what an already-banked field covers before reading a second field off the same items. archive.org robots.txt read in full first (12 lines, `Disallow: /control/` and `/report/` only); the `/download/` redirect target `dn710805.ca.archive.org` serves no robots.txt (404); web.archive.org untouched; no CDX spent. Closed: do not re-test payload-embedded endpoints on another software archive, another collection or another decade. Both halves are dead for opposite reasons, commercial and shareware payloads carrying ~0.5 build-written head-of-distribution hostnames each while open-source payloads carry volume the release date cannot date. Scaling elsewhere is already shut anyway: `cdromsoftware`'s 12,845 in-window items are ISOs closed on fetch cost at line 704 of this file, and `open_source_software`'s 19,920 items are the bundled-documentation failure mode by definition. |
| **Abuse-contact registries (`abuse.net`, its `whois.abuse.net` and DNS TXT lookups, `network-abuse.org`, period anti-spam FAQ contact tables), where the only interface is a one-field form and the operator forbids the export (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE over 5 distinct registrable domains, 5 of 5 already held and 5 of 5 already carrying 2001, so held-and-missing-2001 = 0; four requests, priced against merged260827.** The dating route is fine and is not why it dies: a browsable listing replayed at a Wayback instant would be clean `dated_directory`. There is no listing. `web/20010401id_/http://www.abuse.net/lookup.phtml` (1,001 B) is a single `<FORM ACTION="/lookup.phtml" METHOD=GET>` with one text input; `howwork.html` (5,168 B) says "You can check our current list" and the href on that phrase points at the form; the whole 2001 site is ten static pages (index, addnew, books, commercial, contact, howwork, lookup, register, spamtools, tools) and none is an index of names. The operator forecloses bulk access in writing on `using.phtml` (7,763 B, live): "We don't make copies of the abuse.net database available to build into tools, so don't ask... The database is not available via BIND zone transfer... there is no zone file", with a local MySQL mirror by invitation only above 100,000 queries/month. The population is the painful part and is stated on the form page itself: "Database updated February 5, 2002 09:41, current size 92725 entries", i.e. 92,725 rows of exactly the machine-maintained, non-promotional, really-registered names the hypothesis wanted, with no retrieval path to any of them. Everything actually retrievable (three in-window captures, 26,674 B concatenated, plus the form) yields **7 hostnames and 5 distinct registrable domains**: `abuse.net`, `gpo.gov`, `doshelp.com`, `iecc.com`, `spamfree.org`. Sampled as DISTINCT DOMAINS, not `domain_year` rows. `network-abuse.org` is NXDOMAIN. robots.txt read in full first (7 lines, 98 B; GPTBot disallowed, `Disallow: /cgi-bin` for all); nothing under /cgi-bin fetched. **Method, and it generalises: ask the OPERATOR, not the crawler, whether a bulk artifact exists.** The developer or usage page states the export policy in plain words, and one fetch settles what a CDX sweep would spend an hour guessing at. Second, **the phrase "our current list" is not evidence of a list; resolve the href** (here it went to a one-field form, and the anchor text alone would have justified a download with nothing to download). Third, a lookup form is often the cheapest place on a site to learn the DATABASE POPULATION, because such pages advertise their own size and update stamp: 92,725 entries for 1,001 bytes. This is the mailing-list-catalogue closure in a new costume, and the family now reads as one law: **a service endpoint is the worst possible target, and a per-domain query interface does not become a corpus however good the population behind it is.** Do not re-open. The residual (captured `lookup.phtml?DOMAIN=x` RESULT pages, which would be genuine per-item `dated_directory`) fails on volume before anything else: nothing on the site links to a populated query, so captures are single or double digits against the ~2,600 held `com` names 1,000 EE requires, and enumerating them needs the metered CDX endpoint. |
| **Guestbook homepage URL fields on the hosted services (Dreambook, Lpage, GuestWorld, Bravenet, HTMLGear), refuted on the family constant before the first fetch (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 EE for zero requests, on the closure already recorded in this table as "IRC logs, web forums and signature URLs, closed on density at a scale that cannot be exceeded (2026-08-27)".** The hypothesis argued that a guestbook is a form with a dedicated homepage field rather than a conversation, so the fill rate is a property of the script; that is true and it moves the wrong ratio. The IRC, web forum and signature-URL closure of 2026-08-27 ends with the sentence "do not re-test IRC logs, web forums, BBS echomail, signature URLs or guestbooks on any screen", and it is measured, not asserted: four artifacts, 20,761 distinct domains, 26,360 of 28,348 pairs held (93.0%), mean weight 0.6388, **511.1 net-new post-split EE**. Its transferable constant is net-new post-split pairs per LISTED domain: 0.0480 (Something Awful), 0.0391 (CloneCD), 0.0203 (Table Talk), 0.0155 (FidoNet), against 0.386 for a random held `.com` missing 2001. At that constant a 2001-dated artifact needs about **21,000 distinct domains** for 1,000 EE. A replayed book carries 20 to 50 entries, and at any fill rate below 1.0 with signers repeating across books that is order **10 to 25 distinct domains per Wayback fetch**, so 1,000 EE needs roughly 1,000 to 2,000 separately replayed pages. Two further reasons the premise cannot rescue it: the URL field is HUMAN-TYPED, so law 5 applies unchanged and novel names take the split and earn no year, meaning a higher fill rate buys novelty, which is a COST; and signers are personal-site owners on the low-traffic tail, which is head-selection inverted but lands in the same place, since the closure measured 93.0% already held on a comparable population and the unheld remainder is exactly what the split refuses to date. **Method: the screen order itself, which is the trap CLAUDE.md names.** One grep of `docs/sources.md` for the family, before the first fetch, refuted a carefully argued per-fetch economics case (4.5 domains per fetch on AVS gateways against 20 to 50 entries per book) that was beside the point. **When a hypothesis distinguishes itself from a closed family by mechanism, check whether the mechanism moves the constant that closed the family or a different one.** Here it improved the numerator of a ratio nobody was measuring. Do not re-open on a fill-rate argument. Only a BULK guestbook artifact would reopen the shape: a hosted service's own entry database, or a mirrored book archive of order 21,000 distinct 2001-dated signer domains in one downloadable file. Nothing of that kind is named in the record, and per-page Wayback replay cannot assemble it at a sane request budget. |
| **Link-persistence and URL-decay study data (Spinellis's CACM and IEEE Computer set measured in full; Koehler, Markwell and Brooks, Bar-Ilan and Peritz, Rumsey closed with it), Ding's paper-supplements lens aimed at its one machine-written artifact (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0.7 net-new post-split EE over 1,437 distinct registrable domains, 1,436 of 1,437 already held at 2000; the counterfactual at the artifact's best possible year is 27.8 EE.** Not previously in `docs/sources.md` under link-rot, link persistence, decay, Koehler, Spinellis, Markwell, Bar-Ilan, Rumsey, citation, supplement or replication, so genuinely new. Fetched all of `https://www.spinellis.gr/sw/url-decay/`: `log.rl.20000530.gz` (185,773 B, 37,671 lines, 4,367 check blocks, the ONLY in-window pass), `log.ist.20020828.gz` (2002, out of window), `cacm.urls`, `computer.urls`. robots.txt read in full first (35 B, `Disallow: /cgi-bin`). The evidence class is as good as the hypothesis claimed: a machine-written `getting <url> <status>` line paired with the block's own `date Tue, 30 May 2000` stamp is a self-dating observation that the host resolved and served, no human typing and no corroboration split. Status mix: 3,031x 200, 686x 404, 662x 301, 567x 302, 267x 901 (webcopy's own failure code, no evidence), 83x 504, 52x 503, 31x 403, 15x 400, 5x 500, 2x 401, 1x 303. Live hosts 1,801 of 1,990, collapsing to 1,437 distinct domains (com 664, org 220, edu 156, uk 44, gov 34, net 33, ca 27, de 27, au 23, jp 22). Priced with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against the live store, sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS: **1 net-new pair, 0.7 EE** (one `.org`, weight 0.7101). Re-priced at 2001 the same 1,437 domains give 46 net-new pairs and **27.8 EE**, so the whole family at its ~2,500 distinct-domain ceiling cannot reach 50 EE. **Method, and it is a new pre-fetch discriminator for the whole supplements lens: the 2001 threshold does not transfer to citation-derived populations, and this measures the shortfall.** P(store lacks 2001 | `com` held) is 0.611 across the store; on hosts cited by CACM and IEEE Computer it is **4.4%** (29 of 664), a **14x** shortfall, and 3.2% across all TLDs, a 19x shortfall, worse even than the 2001 magazine archive's 9x, because a refereed CS citation selects a narrower head than a magazine does: standards bodies, universities and vendors, all carried in every year already. **So a corpus whose URLs were harvested from FORMAL CITATIONS is head-selected twice, once by the author and once by the venue, and must be priced at the adjacent-year held rate of an authority corpus (95 to 99%), never at the population average.** Second: **self-dating buys nothing when the population is one the store already holds in that very year.** Datability and novelty were not in tension here as law 5 usually forces; there is simply no headroom, so screen the POPULATION before valuing the evidence class. Third: check the pass DATES before downloading, since a recheck study is worth only the years of its passes, and Spinellis ran exactly two, one in and one out of window. Do not re-test the link-rot family on any screen: every member harvests from formal citations and so shares the 4.4% figure. The one live variant is a dated recheck log over a NON-citation population (a directory's own dead-link sweep, a bookmark service's link checker, an ISP hosting index's availability run), where no editor or referee filtered the URL list; that is a different lens and should be briefed as one. |
| **Non-web service ping directories (newzbot's NNTP scanner measured; The MUD Connector, SHOUTcast and Icecast YP, Hotline trackers, DC hublists, Z39.50 target tests closed with it), where escaping law 2 by CONNECTING is what destroys the population (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE over 9 distinct registrable domains, 9 of 9 already held and held-and-missing-2001 = 0, 0 names new to the candidate pool; priced against merged260827.** The premise is sound and is not why it dies: a scanner row carries its own "last checked" stamp, so the row is self-dating `artifact_listing` and the liveness inference law 2 kills is genuinely never made. Artifact: `web/20011214022943id_/http://www.newzbot.com/serverlist.php` (7,089 B), machine-scanned, with its idle-scanner status page (20020202014558). The `since=ALL` full-database variants are the four sort links on that page and every one 404s in Wayback, since query-string URLs were never captured, so 13-row weekly deltas are the retrievable ceiling. The MUD Connector is worse: `mudlist.html` captures (19991127104923, 29,136 B) are "72 New Listings Found" pages carrying names and added-dates and **no telnet host at all**, the host living on `mud-bin/adv_search.cgi?Mode=MUD&mud=` pages that 404 in Wayback, so the cheapest screen cannot be run there without ~1,700 per-entry fetches. The screen the hypothesis named, run on the one retrievable edition: 13 rows all stamped 2001-12-06 or later; **4 of 13 (30.8%) are bare IPv4** and can never be a domain; the 9 remaining hostnames collapse to 9 distinct domains, all held, all at 2001. The raw rows show why: `ip-66-51-100-134.tera-byte.com`, `host213-123-247-107.in-addr.btopenworld.com`, `ilm25-40-080.ec.rr.com` and `aux-209-217-58-114.oklahoma.net` are **4 of the 9 (44%) ISP reverse-DNS names of a customer machine**, registering as `tera-byte.com`, `btopenworld.com`, `rr.com`, `oklahoma.net`; the other five are institution and ISP heads (`yorku.ca`, `chello.at`, `uni-kiel.de`, `linuxfr.org`, `infinity.net.pl`). Small sample, stated as such, but the hypothesis itself said this one ratio decides the family. **Method: a directory written by a machine that CONTACTED the host records the host's PTR name, and for the low-traffic tail that name belongs to the ISP.** This is the mechanism that put visitor logs at 98.4% and 99.6% held, and it is structural rather than incidental: escaping law 2 by connecting is exactly what forces the name to come from reverse DNS or from an IP literal instead of from the operator's own registration, so the family buys self-dating at the price of the only population that pays. **One-number screen for the whole family: bare-IP fraction plus ISP-reverse-DNS fraction of the non-IP rows** (here 30.8% plus 44% of the rest, 8 of 13 rows unusable before the store is asked). Applies unchanged to SHOUTcast and Icecast YP, Hotline trackers and DC hublists, which are keyed on IP and port and so are strictly worse, not even carrying a PTR lookup. |
| **B2B vertical marketplace self-registered supplier profiles (VerticalNet's 59 communities, priced on `solidwaste.com` and `plasticsnet.com`; Industry.net, `manufacturing.net`, Ariba and Commerce One killed by the same mechanism), where the fill rate is exactly 0 and the causation runs backwards (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 0 net-new post-split EE, 0 distinct domains, for five in-window fetches; nothing was priced against merged260827 because there is nothing to price, held fraction undefined and held-and-missing-2001 = 0.** What would have dated one item is sound and irrelevant: a Buyer's Guide listing served from VerticalNet's live supplier database and replayed at a Wayback instant inside 2001 is clean `dated_directory` shape, but **no item carries a domain to date**. The self-registered profile has a postal address, a phone, a fax, a product list and keywords, and no URL and no email field at all. Artifacts, all reachable and in window: `Sponsors.asp?SType=Storefront` on `solidwaste.com` (65,470 B, capture 20010620113239, the page's own "Total=342, Pages=14") and on `plasticsnet.com` (63,643 B, 20010602032732, Total=142, Pages=6); `/BuyersGuide/Companies.asp?CID=4276&NR=48` (44,962 B, 20010620134931); storefront mini-sites `/storefronts/oneplus.html` (15,307 B, 20010603052022) and `/storefronts/agr.html` (15,745 B, 20010616212131). The whole 59-community roster is free off one page, `web/20010531142856id_/http://www.verticalnet.com/` (31,397 B), which prints all 59 community hostnames plus 14 `search_onlines.asp?group=` sector indexes. **The zero was measured three ways on two independent communities.** (1) Listings: 3 pages, 52 supplier rows each carrying a complete street address, phone and fax block, **0 supplier hostnames**, in the anchors or in the rendered text; the only external hosts are `akamaitech.net` image CDNs, `adsdirect.vertical.net`, `athoc.com`, `convertit.com` and sister communities. (2) Profiles: `CompanyProfile.asp?CoID=<n>`, one request per company, 24 linked off a single category page; 2 of 4 sampled returned 200 and 2 returned 404, and **the two with content have no 2001 capture at all**, the archive 302-ing `20010620000000id_/` to 20060312152417, out of window and footed "Copyright 1996-2006, VertMarkets". The full rendered text of CoID=10212 runs name, address, phone, fax, products, keywords and stops: **no web-site field exists in the template**. (3) Storefronts, the paid self-authored page the hypothesis actually predicted would carry a URL: 3 fetched across both communities, **0 external supplier hosts**, including `agr.html` at a genuine 20010616 capture. Extractor control on the same pipeline in the same session: the VerticalNet homepage yields **63 distinct external hosts**, so the zero is the artifact's and not the reader's. Method, two parts and the second is the general one. (1) **The fill-rate screen costs ONE page and must run before anything else**: strip tags on a single listing page and count external hostnames against contact rows; 52 fully-addressed rows at 0 hosts settled a 59-community, ~20,000-supplier corpus for five fetches. (2) **The hypothesis inverted the causation, and the inversion is the transferable law: a marketplace suppresses the supplier's URL precisely BECAUSE the supplier self-registered.** The argument was that a telco's yellow-pages row has an empty URL field from neglect while a self-registered profile carries a URL because the supplier wanted the traffic. The opposite holds. VerticalNet's business was renting the supplier a storefront ON `solidwaste.com` and selling the lead, so linking out would give away the only thing it sold, and the storefront slug is the company shortname (`accurateplastics`, `akrontool`) rather than the company domain. **The stronger the self-registration incentive, the stronger the operator's incentive to keep the visitor inside**, so B2B marketplaces, hosted-storefront exchanges and lead-generation directories are all WORSE bets than the neglected telco row, not better. That is the same sign the ad-network and comparison-engine routing results reached from a third direction. `web.archive.org` serves no robots.txt (404), so nothing there restricted the fetch; no CDX was spent. Closed: do not re-test VerticalNet, VertMarkets or its 59 communities, and do not fetch Industry.net, `manufacturing.net` or the Ariba and Commerce One supplier networks, which are the same hosted-profile business model and die on the same reasoning. The two unfetched variants are named only so nobody re-derives them: `/content/vertpages/ecommcenters.asp` (`SType=E-Commerce+Center`, the tier above storefronts) and `/buyersguide/onlinecompanyreg/` (the self-registration form) are the same template and the same business model, and the storefront reading already tests the hypothesis at its strongest point. The one data point worth having for the LAW rather than for EE is a marketplace that charged a LISTING fee but hosted nothing, where the operator has no click to protect. |
| **Adult verification service gateway member directories (Adult Check, CyberAge, MANcheck, NetVerifier, AVS Empire), closed on the field the row carries and on a Wayback host exclusion (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 10.0 net-new post-split EE over 28 pages and 1,004,000 B, 0.36 EE per page and 0.021 EE per listed member row, so 5,000 EE needs ~240,000 listed members, 2.4x the largest AVS that ever existed.** What dates one item: the Wayback capture instant of a gateway directory page served from the live member database; no page in the family self-dates. `price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: 28 items all in window, 125 distinct domains, 92 already held, pre-split 33 pairs / 19.3 EE (overstates 1.9x, do not quote), **post-split 17 pairs / 10.0 EE**, all 17 at 2001, mean weight 0.5904, typo upper bound 63.6%. An independent duckdb screen sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS agrees to 0.7%: 119 domains, 89.9% held, 91 already carrying 2001, held-and-missing-2001 = 16, 15 adjacent. **The family dies on the row, not on volume or dating.** All 376 counted CyberAge member rows are `aux.avsofchoice.com/lnkwarn?refws=<memberID>`, an ID and no domain; the only hostnames leak from a JavaScript thumbnail popup on ~40% of rows and usually name a shared content farm, so 517,000 B and 376 rows gave 49 distinct hosts, 0.13 per row. NetVerifier is the same shape (`reportSiteLite.asp?siteID=`), AVS Empire routes all 13 entries through `maximumcash.com/cgi-bin/maxcash.cgi`, and Adult Check Gold's own `inactive_links.htm` prints 100 of 3,251 Gold sites with name and description in 27,877 B and **zero hostnames and zero anchors**. MANcheck is the one arm printing bare member domains and fails instead on volume and window: its five in-window `/cats/` pages are hand-written weekly picks carrying 5 to 15 links against a claimed 9,503 to 10,342 sites, and its machine-generated roster was **first crawled in 2002**. **Adult Check itself, the 100,000-site target the hypothesis rests on, is EXCLUDED from the Wayback Machine**: 403 with an empty body on every path, `wayback/available` NONE at 2000 and 2001, against a control answering 200 in the same second. Method, and the first part generalises: **a roster prints the member's domain when the reader must go to the member AND the lister has no interest in the trip; when the lister's revenue IS the interception, the row degrades to an ID.** That is one mechanism covering AVS gateways, affiliate catalogues and provider member indexes, and it is checkable in ONE request by reading the anchor template. Second: **`wayback/available` returning NONE plus a replay 403 with an empty body, against a live control, is a host-scoped EXCLUSION rather than an absent capture**, so the sibling brand `adultcheckgold.com` was measured instead. Third: on a JavaScript-heavy 2001 directory the member hostname may exist only inside an `onclick` string, so scan for `https?://` anywhere in the document; a quoted-href regex read 0 hosts where a permissive scan read 12. No CDX used. `web.archive.org` refused about 5 of 6 TCP connections at a flat 3.4 s with no 429, and succeeded on retry every time, so use a flat 2 s retry and many attempts, not exponential backoff. Closed: do not re-test adult verification services, age verification gateways or paid-access member directories on any screen, measured on four independent networks. The only untested increment is MANcheck's browsable `/search/cat/` namespace, ~40 pages, every capture 2002 and therefore out of window. One thread belongs elsewhere: the CyberAge popups name shared CONTENT-FARM hosts (`hrescontent.com`, `teeniepornvideos.com`, `sexycyberwebsites.com`), a different population from a member roster. |
| **Broadcast and press licensee registers (NAA newspaperlinks.com, E&P MediaINFO Links, NewsLink, radio-locator, 100000watts), where the fill rate PASSES at 100% and the family dies on the year (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 52.8 net-new post-split EE over 28 priced state pages and 394,001 B, 0.0648 EE per listed newspaper, so all 51 states are worth ~97 EE and E&P's 3,217 US records ~208 EE for 161 fetches; the whole press family, US, Canada, world and magazines, does not reach 1,000 EE and the bar needs 77,000 listed papers.** What dates one item: the register page's Wayback capture instant (2001-04-18) over a server-generated state query, corroborated on NewsLink by a per-page `META HTTP-EQUIV=Last-Modified` regeneration stamp that varies page to page. `price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: 815 distinct domains, 729 already held at 2001, pre-split 86 pairs / 52.9 EE (do not quote), **post-split 85 pairs / 52.8 EE**, mean weight 0.6216, com 80 / net 5, 1 name new to the candidate pool, typo upper bound 27.9%. An independent duckdb screen on DISTINCT DOMAINS agrees: 811 domains, **811 held any year = 100.000%**, 726 already carrying 2001, **held-and-missing-2001 = 85, 10.481% of held**, all 85 adjacent. California, the head state, is worse still: 125 domains, 100% held, 96.8% at 2001, 2.5 EE. The hypothesis's own close criterion fires, 10.5% under its 15% line and six times under the 61.1% `.com` population average. The broadcast arm never gets priced for two separate reasons: (1) NewsLink's leaves are **redirect-obfuscated**, every entry `<A HREF=cgi/goto.cgi?2=002252>`, so canews.html carries 253 links and zero domain names and one key costs one Wayback request to resolve (tested; the archived 302 came back 20021010, out of window), ~12,000 requests for a population already shown 100% held; (2) radio-locator and 100000watts are **not archived in window at leaf level**, the availability API returning 2005 for every state finder whether asked 19990101 or 20011215. Method, three reusable parts. (1) **A media register dies on the YEAR, not the fill rate, and the two deaths need different screens.** RSGB-style registers were closed on `href="http` fill; these are 100% filled and still pay nothing, because a newspaper is head enough to already carry 2001. Measure `already carrying 2001 / held`, not fill, on any sector census whose members are businesses that advertise; 100.000% held over 811 domains is the sharpest no-discovery reading in the file. (2) **An opaque numeric redirect key is a harder death than an empty URL field and it is one request to detect**: fetch one leaf, grep for `://` inside hrefs, and `cgi/goto.cgi?2=NNNNNN` means the register never prints its own subject. (3) **The availability API is the cheap in-window test for a CGI namespace and it costs no CDX**, but URL-encode the query string (the raw `?` form returns NONE while the encoded form returns a capture) and reject any timestamp not starting 2001, since the API returns the CLOSEST. Also: the E&P template writes `HREF='http://...'` in single quotes inside latin-1 bytes, so a shell grep reported 0 URLs on a page holding 20; decode as latin-1 before counting. Closed: do not re-test US or Canadian newspaper, radio or TV registers on any screen. The two loose ends are bounded by the measured rate and not worth the requests: 21 remaining NAA state pages (~45 EE) and E&P's 161 US result pages plus world, Canada and magazines (~600 EE for ~400 fetches). Reopen only on a NON-US register of a sector whose members are not businesses. |
| **Price-comparison engine merchant registries (BizRate, and mySimon, DealTime, PriceSCAN, Shopper.com unreached), where the routing discriminator predicts SHAPE and not payout for the third time (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 7.0 net-new post-split EE over 22 listing pages and 969,667 B, 0.318 EE per page and 0.0129 EE per listed store slot; the full roster is ~6,730 store slots over ~270 pages, worth ~86 EE, so 5,000 EE would need ~393,000 listed stores and the family's best member is short by 30x to 58x.** What dates one item: the listing page prints its own generation date in the header ("November 9, 2001") and the capture instant fixes when BizRate's live merchant database asserted that store set, so `dated_directory` and nothing a human typed. Artifact: `bizrate.com/ratings_guide/listing.xpml?cat_id=<C>[&start=<N>]`, captures 20010617 to 20011127, reached from `/ratings_guide/guide.xpml` which enumerates all 20 departments; there is no all-departments roster. 542 store links, 456 raw host strings, **357 distinct registrable domains**. Sampled as DISTINCT DOMAINS: **356 of 357 held (99.7%)**, 345 already carrying 2001 (96.9% of held), **held-and-missing-2001 = 11, 3.1% of held**, all adjacent. `price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: pre-split 12 pairs / 7.6 EE (overstates 1.1x), **post-split 11 pairs / 7.0 EE**, all at 2001, all `com`, typo upper bound 50.0%, exactly 1 name new to the candidate pool; an independent duckdb screen agrees at 11 pairs / 6.95 EE. The premise is confirmed on shape and destroyed on population: 3.1% against the 61.1% `.com` average is a **20x head-selection penalty and the worst yet measured in this roster family** (COMDEX booths 9.7%, BURST! members 9.0%, Bomis rings 7.9%). **Head/tail gradient INSIDE the artifact, 4.7x**: the 14 first pages (default sort "Top-Rated Stores") give 229 domains, 100.0% held, 1.3% missing 2001, 0.136 EE per page; the 8 deep-pagination pages give 147 domains, 5.5% missing 2001, **0.633 EE per page**, and the two sets are near-disjoint. **URL fill, the screen the hypothesis did not name**: on the Apparel first page only 6 of 25 store anchors carry the store's own host, the other 18 being `linksynergy`, `cc-dt.com`, `bfast.com` and `qksrv.net` tracking URLs with no merchant hostname anywhere, with four more recoverable only from description prose, so union fill is ~40% on a head page and ~66% over 22 pages. Cost compounds it: 6 of 32 replay attempts refused or timed out and 22 pages cost ~25 minutes against the ~3,000 EE/hour querying alone pays. Method, three reusable parts. (1) **The routing discriminator is now measured on a THIRD population and it predicts shape, not payout**: a price engine does print a route to the store, but it prints a CPC tracking URL, so ask what fraction of anchors carry a host, on one page, before pricing. (2) **`wayback/available` canonicalises `www.` away and the replay path must use the host it RETURNS**: `web/<ts>id_/http://www.bizrate.com/marketplace/index.xpml` failed on six serial attempts while the API's own `http://bizrate.com:80/...` at the same timestamp returned 35,810 B first try, and six false zeros were produced before this was noticed. Also `curl -L` on an `id_` replay can silently deliver the archived `robots.txt` instead of the page, so never follow redirects on a replay fetch. (3) **A paginated roster's `start=` bar is a free denominator AND a free tail sample**: one first-page fetch per department gives the store count and the deep-page URLs, and pricing head and tail separately turned 0.136 into 0.633 EE per page on the same artifact. Closed: do not re-test BizRate or any shopping-bot merchant roster, because a rated store is a store customers bought from in 2001, exactly the population the store already covers at 2001. The one untested variant, not worth it at the 20x penalty, is a MERCHANT-SIDE artifact (`merchant.bizrate.com/oa/merchant_services/`) listing applicants rather than rated stores. |
| **Statutory tourism and accommodation registers (smoothhound.co.uk, Bord Failte, AA, NITB), where the fill rate PASSES at 86.4% and the artifact still cannot add its own year (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 1.61 net-new post-split EE over 44 in-window premises pages, 0.0366 EE per fetched page and 0.0168 per LISTED premises; at a generous 15,000 premises the whole directory is 250 to 550 EE for ~17,000 Wayback requests, ~0.03 EE per request, below the curated-directory floor.** What dates one item: the Wayback capture instant of the register page, and that is exactly what kills it. Structure enumerates cleanly: town-letter index, town page, one page per premises; 16 of 26 letter indexes gave 829 distinct town pages (full alphabet ~1,400-1,800) and 6 town pages listed 303 distinct premises at 21 to 106 each. A domain-bearing field is present on 38 of 44 fetched premises pages = **86.4%, far above the 10% kill screen**: proprietor email in a hidden `mto.pl` form field on 37 of 45, an external website URL on 11 of 45. Pricing against merged260827, sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS: 34 domains, 33 already held (97.1%), 1 novel (earns nothing), **held AND missing 2001 = 2, i.e. 6.1% of held**. CONTROL measured the same day on 40,000-row store samples: P(missing 2001 | held) is 0.352 for `co.uk` and 0.637 for `com`, so this population reads **5.8x worse than its own TLD baseline**, which makes it a pointed-wrong reading and not a nothing-found one; 17 of the first 18 per-domain year lists already carry 2001. The statutory boards are worse on retrievability, not better: Bord Failte's register is ASP query-string driven with no in-window capture of `/reservations/`, `/seeireland/` or `gettingaround/search.asp`, and the AA hotel guide index is an empty 78-byte capture. **THE LAW, and it generalises past this family: a directory whose date comes from its own Wayback capture cannot add that year to the domains it links with direct hrefs, because the crawl that dated the artifact also dated its targets.** That is law 1 sharpened from the NAME to the YEAR: the names are not merely held, they are held at precisely the one year the artifact could ever evidence. So the pre-download screen for any Wayback-dated listing is neither the fill rate nor the held fraction but "were these links reachable by the crawler that captured this page". The only escaping channel here was the crawler-invisible one, emails inside hidden form fields, and public-suffix collapse destroys it: those fields reduce to `freeserve.co.uk`, `fsnet.co.uk`, `demon.co.uk`, `btinternet.com`, `aol.com`, `clara.co.uk`, every one held in all six years. Reusable screen, three requests: fetch one listing page, take 20 linked entries, ask the store what years those 20 carry, and stop if they carry the artifact's own year. Also reusable: `archive.org/wayback/available` plus `web/<ts>id_/` costs one request per page and needs no CDX, so a family can be priced without touching the metered index. Closed: do not re-test the accommodation sector. The residual worth one hour, and only one, is a listing whose outbound links are NOT crawler-reachable (POST forms, redirector scripts, plain-text URLs in print-derived pages), since that is the single condition under which a Wayback-dated directory can still add its own year. |
| **Free-for-all (FFA) link pages, ffanet.com and the networks around it, where the self-dating premise is REFUTED and the family pays anyway on the 2001 year screen (2026-08-28)** | **FIND at 25.2 net-new post-split EE over 41 pairs from 9 pages and 178,235 B, 0.0726 EE per listed distinct domain, effectively all of it from ONE page.** What dates one item: the Wayback capture instant of a member FFA page, which displays the posted link as live text at that instant. Class `dated_directory`, master-eligible; approval request filed, not ingested. **The hypothesis's own premise dies first.** It says an FFA page stamps every entry with its own date, so no capture is needed. Counting date literals against link count across all 9 pages: `iwv2000.htm` 506 links / 0 stamps, `bds.htm` 307 / 0, `list.pl` 123 / 0, `linkstoyou.com` 38 / 0. The only page with per-entry dates is `freeforall.net`, 90 links / 63 stamps, and it is a hand-curated "free stuff" table (Title / Info / Date, MMDDYY) and not an FFA page at all. So this is an ordinary `dated_directory` and the date is in the capture. **The 4.56% question the brief said decides the family is answered 20x the other way**: 347 distinct 2001 domains, 317 held (91.35%), against the remailer corpus's 4.56%, because an FFA submitter is advertising and must post a URL that resolves. **And the whole yield is the year, exactly as squidGuard.** 2001 captures: 347 distinct, 91.35% held, 41 held-and-missing-2001, **25.1997 EE**, all 41 `com` 37 / `net` 4, mean weight 0.6146, and all 41 are held at 2000 so the adjacent-year figure equals the raw one. 2000 captures: 175 distinct, **175 of 175 held and all 175 already carrying 2000, 0.0000 EE**. Gross pre-split is 71 pairs / 43.4 EE and overstates 1.7x, do not quote. Typo upper bound 40.8%; 21 of 347 novel to the store. The redirector killer that closed WebRing and `free_host_member_indexes` does NOT fire: FFA entries are plain `href`s to the submitter's own URL. What erodes the count instead is submitters without a domain posting on a free host, so the registrable name collapses onto one certainly held that year (`roibot.com` x8, `hotyellow98.com` x7, `angelfire.com` x6, `homestead.com` x5, `geocities.com` x4), which is why held-at-2001 is 87.1% where the `.com` population predicts 38.9%. Artifacts: `web.archive.org/web/<ts>id_/http://pages.ffanet.com:80/links/<member>.htm`, 47,810 B (`bds.htm` 20000706234213) and 71,305 B (`iwv2000.htm` 20010307072648); the 110-member roster is `ffanet.com:80/links/list.pl?` at 20000304021731, 14,590 B; plus `freeforall.net` 20010722003910, `linkstoyou.com` 20010630123832, `ffanet.com` 20010709121131, `ffanetwork.com` 20010721153909, `freeffa.com` 20010720133803, `ffapages.com` 20010302105939, `1-2-free.com` 20010622230517. Not archived: `ffanet.com/ffalist.htm`, `ffa.net`, `ffaking.com`, `1st-in-links.com`. Out of window and NOT to be fetched: `addfreelinks.com` 2010, `ffagold.com` 2003, `ffamaster.com` 2002, `ffa-links.com` 2002. Method, three parts. (1) **Probe availability before replay**: `archive.org/wayback/available?url=&timestamp=` answers in ~1.5 s where `web/<ts>id_/` took 25 to 120 s under contention, so one cheap probe per URL turns every miss into a 1.5 s miss and keeps off `/cdx` entirely. (2) **Audit the date claim before pricing the corpus**: date literals against link count is one pass and it refuted the premise outright; a hypothesis resting on "the artifact carries a per-entry date" should be tested by that ratio on page one. (3) **A curated page inside a family is not an instance of it**: `freeforall.net` looks like the hypothesis confirmed and is the one page that is not an FFA page; pricing it alone would have reported a self-dating family that does not exist. Tools at `scratchpad/ffa/`. Continuation, ONLY at 2001: ~25 EE per 2001-captured member page means ~40 pages for 1,000 EE and ~200 for 5,000; pull `list.pl` at several captures since the roster rotates, availability-probe every member URL at a 2001 pin, discard 2000 captures unmeasured, replay the survivors. Page size predicts yield in this sample but is confounded with year and wants separating on the next 20 pages. Rights: `list.pl` asserts sole intellectual property over the ROSTER, member pages carry a normal copyright line, measurement unaffected. `web.archive.org` serves no robots.txt (404, verified); `archive.org` disallows only `/control/` and `/report/`. |
| **Online yellow pages 2001, nine publishers (Yell, Switchboard, SuperPages, Telstra, Canada), where the artifact is in the archive and the DATA IS NOT IN THE ARTIFACT (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 4.9 EE, which should be read as zero.** Nothing dates an item, because no item exists: across nine publishers and twelve archived files, 261,588 B, business rows captured is **0**. The hypothesis's first kill condition fires but its reason is wrong, and the correction is the reusable part. It predicted "a result surface living in a query string Wayback never captured". Query strings ARE captured: `cgidir.dll?LNK=3:2&MEM=1` has 20010615143522, `cgidir.dll?func=catlist&mem=1` 20021030004718, `cgiqa.dll?LNK=3:1&MEM=1` 20010714010256, `cgidir.dll?PR=105&mem=1&...&N=1` 20020613132717. **What is never captured is a URL that was never an `href`.** The result URL is assembled client-side: `sp_listings.phtml` carries eleven occurrences of a JavaScript `buildURL(stype)` concatenating `?SRC=&STYPE=&C=&N=&K=` from form fields and `switchboard.com` two more, so a 2001 crawler had nothing to follow and the result space has zero captures by construction. Availability probed on 81 URLs through `archive.org/wayback/available`, never `/cdx`: publisher HOMEPAGES 29 of 29 have in-window captures, so the family is not missing from the archive; parameterised RESULT URLs with real values **0 of 11**. The controlled six are the strongest number: they are the exact `results_categories.jhtml;$sessionid$...&_DAV=browse` anchors printed on the yellowpages.com.au page the crawler itself captured at 20010627194118, and **0 of 6 were followed**, with the parameterless `/yp/search/results_categories.jhtml` (20010208093117) and `/yp/search/results.jhtml` (20010608181603) both answering 200 as controls. Fill rate, the second kill condition, cannot be measured because the denominator is zero. Priced with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827 on DISTINCT DOMAINS: 59 distinct, 45 held (76.3%), pre-split 14 pairs / 10.9 EE (do not quote), **post-split 6 pairs / 4.9 EE**, and the net-new TLDs are `ng` 4, `select` 1, `com` 1, where the `.ng` names are `click.ng`, `html.ng`, `js.ng`, `image.ng` off an ad server path and `this.select` is CSS. Every genuine name of the 59 is the publisher, a sister property, an ad network or a homepage banner advertiser; **not one is a directory listing**. Per flagship, measured not assumed: **Yell** POSTs to `search.yell.com/search/DoSearch` in May 2001, GETs it by August, and `yell.co.uk` and `yellowpages.co.uk` are 266-byte meta-refresh stubs; **Telstra** POSTs to `results.jhtml` and puts `;$sessionid$` in the path, making every URL single-use; **Switchboard** is GET-native but its captured pages are the empty form and an A-Z jump list with `C=` empty; **SuperPages** `listings.phtml` renders "Try Again"; **canadayellowpages.com** is a 1,798 B splash; **ukyellowpages.com** is an SEO doorway with a 70-term keyword meta tag. The volume argument in the hypothesis (11M Switchboard businesses, 2M Yell) is real and irrelevant, because none of it was ever crawlable. Adjacent to the printed-directory row below, which killed Thomas Register, Kompass and D&B on digitisation reach; the online editions are a different artifact and fail on different and more complete grounds. Method, three pieces. (1) **The pre-download screen for any database-backed 1996-2001 site is `grep -c buildURL` on its archived homepage**, or any JS concatenating a query string from form fields: if the search URL is assembled in script it was never an `href`, no crawler emitted it, and the result space has zero captures no matter how large the database was. This is sharper than "it has a query string", which is wrong. One fetch, before pricing anything. (2) **The controlled follow test**: take the anchors off a page Wayback itself captured and ask the availability API whether each was followed, so zeros are about the crawler and not about your URL guessing, paired with a parameterless control on the same path. (3) **A session ID in the path (`;$sessionid$`, `;jsessionid=`) is a hard zero**, since every capture is a distinct URL nothing else links to. Also confirmed: `archive.org/wayback/available` echoes your requested `timestamp` at the top level, so a `grep -oE '"timestamp"'` taking the last match reads your own input as the snapshot date; parse `archived_snapshots.closest.timestamp` or every probe reports a perfect hit. Do not re-test Yell, Yell.co.uk, yellowpages.co.uk, Scoot, Switchboard, SuperPages, BigYellow, InfoSpace, SmartPages, AnyWho, Canada411, canadayellowpages.com, ukyellowpages.com, yellowpages.ca or Telstra Yellow Pages Online on any screen. Left open and belonging elsewhere: Switchboard sold hosted business pages at `ypc.switchboard.com/bin/cgicontent.dll?BUS=<id>` with sequential IDs, but the one specimen has no capture at any date; and a yellow-pages publisher that printed a STATIC "new web sites this month" page would be an href and therefore crawlable, though none of the nine had one. |
| **Dated job postings, 49 Usenet job groups already on disk, where the ad names the RE-POSTER and not the employer (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 158.2 net-new post-split EE counterfactual (8.6 EE against the live store), 0.000797 EE per in-window posting and 0.00932 EE per distinct advertiser domain, so 1,000 EE needs ~1,254,000 postings and 5,000 EE needs ~6,270,000.** What dates one item: the `Date:` header of the posting, machine-stamped by the posting agent, in the same record as the advertiser's address, master-eligible as `usenet_announce`; but the body domains are human-typed and take the corroboration split, which is where 98.0% of the gross goes. **All three groups the hypothesis names were already ingested**, so the live-store figure is 0 by construction and the honest number is the counterfactual: dropping source_ids 22, 23, 31, 32, 39, 40 and asking whether any of the 39 remaining timestamped sources dated the pair gives 21,727 of 23,370 held (93.0%), pre-split 1,643 pairs / 993.2 EE, **post-split 270 pairs / 158.2 EE**, by year {1996:7, 1997:21, 1998:50, 1999:71, 2000:13, 2001:108}. Against the live store it reads 94.1% held and 8.6 EE; the 1.1-point gap IS the whole contribution of the usenet lineage. Corpus: 198,397 in-window postings parsed out of 49 job-group mboxes already at `data/raw/usenet_bulk/*.mbox.zip`, 552,123,385 B, apply-to channel only. **Zero network requests; `web.archive.org` was never touched.** Positive control in the same code path, because nothing-found and pointed-wrong look identical: 200 `.com` names the counterfactual store holds at 2000 and lacks at 2001, written as synthetic "Contact: jobs@X / Apply: http://www.X/careers" ads through the SAME extractor and the SAME pricer, returned **200/200 and 126.4 EE**, so 198,397 real ads are worth 1.25x what 200 hand-picked names are, and the pipeline is sound. The 2001 threshold on distinct domains: of 15,215 advertiser domains held at 2000, only **2,153 are missing 2001 (14.2%)** against the `.com` population average of 61.1%; the 2000 adjacent figure is 1.1%. Head/tail gradient is 1.5x, not BURST!'s 17x: 100+ mentions 507 domains 98.7% held 6.4 EE, 2 to 99 mentions 8,969 domains 95.3% held 82.4 EE, exactly once 7,504 domains 87.4% held 69.4 EE, so even the singleton tail is 4x worse than the population. **The hypothesis's own discriminating variable, firm size, is refuted directly**: `alt.bestjobsusa.denver.jobs` 1,138 postings 0.000 EE, `.la.jobs` 1,669 0.000, `.sandiego.jobs` 1,001 0.000, `.texas.jobs` 334 0.000, `.illinois.jobs` 117 0.000, against `comp.jobs.contract` 67,497 postings at 0.265 EE per 1,000. **The mechanism**: 2.71 distinct domains per posting and the top 25 domains carry 40.6% of all mentions, being dice.com 51,014, selectjobs.com 38,607, gate.net 20,884, aol.com 13,817, usenetserver.com 7,286, netcom.com 7,079, recruitersonline.com 5,426. Those are job boards, news providers and ISPs, and "Company Name: Technology Services, LLC" is printed as plain text with no domain at all. The employer's domain is not in the ad. **The web-board arm is closed with ZERO archive requests**, because the boards syndicated to Usenet so their own output is on disk: selectjobs.com 22,314 postings / 782 domains / 97.1% held / **5.3 EE** (0.239 per 1,000), medzilla.com 3,878 / 825 / 96.5% / 4.4 EE, gojobs.com 3,785 / 235 / 91.2% / 2.4 EE, careerspan.com 2,328 / 510 / 97.4% / 2.6 EE, dice.com 251 / 60 / 96.7% / 0.6 EE. Recovering a whole 2001 board archive out of Wayback would need roughly 1M to 4M per-posting captures to reach 1,000 EE. Robots read whole before any request: `www.jobserve.com` 1,265 B / 70 lines blocks eleven bots, none ours; `www.jobs.ac.uk` 120 B; `www.dice.com` 1,167 B / 50 lines disallows `/job` and `/jobsearch/`, exactly the per-posting path. Method, three reusable parts. (1) **When a corpus is already ingested, do not report the 0 it returns; rebuild the pre-ingest store by dropping its lineage.** `SELECT DISTINCT domain, evidence_year FROM evidence WHERE source_id IN (<timestamped sources MINUS this lineage>)` restores what the store knew before the ingest for exactly the pairs in question, and it moved the answer from 8.6 EE to 158.2 EE. Script at `scratchpad/counterfactual.py`, takes any `price_items.py` item stream. (2) **A syndicating aggregator can be priced without touching its host**: dice, selectjobs, medzilla and gojobs all gatewayed to Usenet, so grouping on-disk postings by the `From:` domain prices each named web lead offline, at full corpus size, with no crawl and no false zero. Group by poster before proposing to fetch a board. (3) **The screen for any advertisement corpus is POSTING CONCENTRATION, not firm size**: postings per distinct domain predicts the rate directly, `alt.jobs.overseas` at 3.3:1 paying 2.142 EE per 1,000 against `comp.jobs.contract` at 32.1:1 paying 0.265, an 8x spread on the same shape. Count distinct posters against distinct ads on a sample before parsing; a corpus where a few hundred agencies file everything is a people population and dies to the ChangeLog inversion regardless of how small the advertised firms are. Do not re-test Usenet job, resume, employment or careers hierarchies, and do not fetch jobserve.com, jobs.ac.uk, monster.com or dice.com per-posting pages. General result: any classified-advertising population, jobs, for-sale, personals or property, names the venue and the agency rather than the subject. Not disproved: a classified corpus where the ADVERTISER is the domain holder by definition, such as a web-hosting or domain-for-sale group, but that is a different family. |
| **Ad network publisher rosters (BURST! Media and eleven rivals), where the shape prediction is CONFIRMED and the family dies on MEMBERSHIP SIZE, plus the head/tail gradient measured inside a single directory (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 19.4 net-new post-split EE over 32 pairs from 13 pages and 178,349 B, 0.0533 EE per listed member and 1.49 EE per page, with the entire BURST! gallery capped at ~280 EE against a 5,000 EE bar.** What dates one item: the Wayback capture instant of a BURST! gallery page, which is served from BURST!'s live membership database and so asserts the named site is a paying, ad-carrying member at that instant; the page's own "(c) 2001 BURST! Media" gives a year but no instant, so nothing dates an item without the capture. Machine-generated from a database, so no corroboration split on the listing itself. Artifacts: `web.archive.org/web/<ts>id_/http://www.burstmedia.com/release/gallery/cat_list.asp` with `?q=<cat>&p=<n>&s=trafficup|trafficdown&d=0`, captures 20010610 to 20010701, 13,070 to 16,208 B per page, 13 pages; category index at 20010618120800 is 8,855 B; contrast artifact `www.phase2media.com/advertising_opps/byalpha.html` at 20010610081925, 15,735 B. **The hypothesis's own discriminator is confirmed exactly as written**: BURST! anchors each member's title straight at the member's own server (`http://www.coolquiz.com`, `http://www.eeggs.com`) with no redirector, ~37 bare hostnames per page, 40 members per page, sorted by traffic, over 20 top categories and ~200 subcategories. Priced with `scripts/price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827: 13 items, all in window, 364 distinct registrable domains, 364 pairs, 325 already held, pre-split 39 pairs / 23.7 EE (overstates 1.2x, do not quote), **post-split 32 pairs / 19.4 EE**, all 32 at 2001, mean weight 0.6065 (com 29, net 2, uz 1), 7 novel names to the candidate pool only, typo upper bound 64.1%. Year screen on DISTINCT DOMAINS, not rows: 364 listed, 356 held in any year (97.8%), 324 already carrying 2001, **held-and-missing-2001 = 32, i.e. 9.0% of held** against the 61.1% `.com` population average. **The head/tail gradient INSIDE the one artifact is the real result and it is 17x.** Sorted `trafficup` (largest members): 3 pages, 108 domains, 98.1% held, 105 of 106 held names already carry 2001, held-and-missing-2001 = 1 (0.9%), 1 pair / 0.6 EE, **0.2 EE per page**. Sorted `trafficdown` (smallest members): 2 pages, 61 domains, 95.1% held, held-and-missing-2001 = 9 (15.5%), 9 pairs / 5.5 EE, **2.75 EE per page**. The two sets are disjoint (overlap 1 of 38 against 31). So the network's head is already covered at 2001 and only its tail pays, which is the magazine-archive law reproduced inside one directory rather than across artifacts. **Volume ceiling, measured not assumed, off the "Page 1 of N" counter every response carries**: Arts & Entertainment 34 pages, Computers & Internet 9, and 4, 4, 4, 6, 5 for five more, so 66 pages over 7 of 20 categories, mean 9.43, hence ~189 pages and **~7,200 member entries for the whole gallery, capping it at ~280 EE** (~380 EE by the per-listed-domain route, before the ~15% cross-category duplication). 1,000 EE would need 18,760 listed members and 5,000 EE would need 93,800, so the family's best member is **short by 13x**. Throughput compounds it: web.archive.org refused roughly half of all connections and 13 pages cost ~45 minutes wall clock with retries, so ~189 pages is several hours for under 400 EE against the ~3,000 EE/hour querying alone pays. **The rest of the family is thinner, not thicker.** Phase2Media's `byalpha.html` is the head-selected variant the affiliate closure predicted: 28 distinct domains for the whole artifact, 26 already carrying 2001, 2 post-split pairs / 1.3 EE, and it prints brand names as plain text with ZERO anchors ("BabyZone", "Boating Online", "Maxim Online" carry no domain at all) while naming AmericanExpress.com, Barbie.com, Britannica.com and FOXSports.com. Eleven other networks checked at their 2001 homepages expose no member roster to follow: valueclick.com (8,963 B, publisher recruitment and press only), engage.com (17,458 B, solutions pages), fastclick.com (12,167 B, media kit), burstnet.com (1,453 B redirect stub), flycast.com (66 B), l90.com (255 B), targetnet.com (4,244 B), plus adsmart.net, doubleclick.net, smartclicks.com and adorigin.com, whose 2001 captures exist but whose fetches failed on transport rather than absence. **Two false-zero controls, both honoured**: `archive.org/wayback/available` returned NONE for `goldenwebawards.com`, a 2001 capture this file already records, so every NONE here is a lower bound and not an absence, invalidating the zeros for 247media.com and for deep pagination `q=2&p=17` and `q=2&p=30`; the ~280 EE ceiling deliberately does not depend on those, being computed from the membership TOTAL rather than from reachable pages, which is the generous direction. Positive control that the fetch path works: `q=10&p=2` found and fetched at 20010618183013, 14,346 B, so shallow pagination is archived and reachable. **Method, three reusable parts.** (1) **When a directory offers a sort order, sort by SMALLEST and sample there, then price head and tail separately**: page 1 of the traffic-sorted roster reads 0.9% held-and-missing-2001 and would have closed this family at 0.2 EE per page, while the same artifact's reverse sort reads 15.5% and 2.75 EE per page. One extra request per category converts a head sample into a tail sample, and the head of any 2001-dated directory is worthless by construction. (2) **A paginated directory prints "Page 1 of N", so the volume ceiling costs ZERO extra requests** if you read the counter off the page you were fetching anyway; combined with the reverse sort, one request per category yields the tail sample, the category size and the subcategory list at once. (3) **The roster discriminator predicts SHAPE reliably and PAYOUT not at all.** A roster prints the member's bare domain exactly when the reader must GO to the member, and BURST! is the positive instance the affiliate catalogues lacked, so the rule is now measured on both sides; what kills a roster is that one ad network has 10^3 to 10^4 members while 10^4 to 10^5 listed names are needed. **Price the next roster on membership size first, never on whether it prints domains.** Do not re-test ad network, banner network or ad rep firm member rosters: the shape works, the population does not. The one thing not disproved is a MULTI-YEAR walk of this same gallery, since the members lacking 2001 are a different set from those lacking 2000 or 1999 and the gallery has earlier captures, but at 1.49 EE per page that is a rate argument the family already loses, so revisit only if a bulk copy is ever found that does not cost one request per 38 names. |
| **Federation club registers (RSGB, linux.org), closed on the URL FILL RATE, a one-request screen the family had never been given (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 27.6 net-new post-split EE over two arms, 0.0553 EE per listed club entry, and closed for every sport, hobby and user-group register on either screen.** What dates one item: the register page's own regeneration stamp ("This page was last updated on 16 March 2001", varying across 66 of 68 RSGB county files, so a real monthly regeneration), with the Wayback capture instant as the second leg. On linux.org the same footer is a template constant, 44 of 45 pages identical to the minute, so only the capture instant dates it. Artifacts, 1,283,000 B: RSGB affiliated-societies register, 68 of 75 county files under `web.archive.org/web/20010418144102id_/http://www.rsgb.org/memb/clubs/LCLB*.HTM` (538,733 B) plus two index pages; positive control linux.org user-group register, 45 country and US-state pages under `.../20011115235923id_/http://www.linux.org/groups/` (708,157 B) plus its index. **RSGB, the hypothesis's own named target, is killed by the hypothesis's own stated discriminator**: 68 county files, 493 club entries, exactly ONE club web URL in the register (`www.sadars.org`), a **fill rate of 0.20%**; every other entry gives a callsign, a meeting night, a named secretary and a phone number. Priced with `price_items.py --all-tlds`: 68 items, 57 distinct registrable domains, 51 held, pre-split 6 pairs / 5.2 EE (do not quote), **post-split 4 pairs / 3.6 EE**, mean weight 0.8940, uk 3 / com 1. Those 57 are not club sites but the mailto hosts of secretaries, so the population is ISPs (freeserve.co.uk, ntlworld.com, compuserve.com, aol.com, talk21.com) with about twenty club-owned names among them. Independent duckdb screen on DISTINCT DOMAINS agrees: 49 of 51 held (96.078%), 46 already carrying 2001, **held-and-missing-2001 = 3**, all adjacent, 2.94 EE gross. **The positive control proves the zero belongs to the register and not to the instrument**: linux.org is the same shape over a web-native population, 434 of 1,346 groups read, 379 carrying a URL, **fill rate 87.3% against RSGB's 0.20%**; 492 distinct domains, 415 held, pre-split 77 pairs / 32.9 EE (do not quote), **post-split 58 pairs / 24.0 EE**, mean weight 0.4135, de 18 / org 12 / com 11 / net 6 / ar 4 / br 3, typo bound 66.2%, duckdb agreeing at 473 of 492 held (96.138%) and 57 of 58 adjacent. Projected over the full 1,346-group register: linear 74, saturation 74, power law 69, so **quote 69 EE for the whole artifact**. **So the family fails both independent screens, one target each**: RSGB fails DENSITY at 0.20% fill over a population the federation itself puts at "more than 700" clubs, and linux.org passes density and fails AUTHORITY and volume at 96.138% held, 83.5% already carrying 2001, 0.0553 EE per listed group. The 5,000 EE bar then needs about **90,000 club entries in one register**, and no 1996-2001 federation register is within two orders of magnitude. The premise, that a reader has to GO to the club so the register must print its site, is simply false for 2001: the register prints a person and a phone number, because the club's contact was a volunteer secretary and not a server. Method, two reusable parts. (1) **THE URL FILL RATE IS THE WHOLE SCREEN FOR A CLUB REGISTER AND IT COSTS ONE REQUEST**: a 2001 federation register was compiled from paper affiliation forms, so its URL field is optional and empty, while a register compiled by a web portal from web submissions is 87% full. Ask who typed the entry, not how many clubs the body has; fetch one county index and count `href="http` against club entries before pricing anything. (2) **A COUNT PRINTED NEXT TO EVERY LINK IS A FREE DENOMINATOR AND A FREE EXTRACTOR TEST**: linux.org's index prints "Australia (17)", summing to 1,346 over 102 countries, which gave the projection base without fetching the register and caught an off-by-one in the block splitter, since the "more information" anchor sits at the END of a row so the text BEFORE anchor i belongs to group i. Also: a portal footer stamp can be a template constant, so vary-check it across pages before letting it date anything. Navigation used no `cdx` at all: `archive.org/wayback/available?url=...&timestamp=...` to find a capture, then `web.archive.org/web/<ts>id_/<url>` and the page's own hrefs to walk down, with a six-attempt retry wrapper because `web.archive.org` refuses the connection intermittently and succeeds on retry. Unreachable and why: ARRL's `FandES/field/club/biglist.html` is a GET form and the availability API returns NONE for its `?section=AL` form, `clubsearch.phtml` being a search endpoint; ScoutBase UK had moved to `sid.sbuk.org.uk/ukemail/` PHP forms by 2001 and its static Jumpstation A page carries 7 external links; the-fa.org at 2001 is a frameset over a JavaScript preloader with no reachable club directory. Two loose ends left deliberately, bounded by the 0.0553 EE per entry rate at under 1 EE and 45 EE: RSGB's 7 remaining county files plus `memb/nat-club.htm`, and the 912 linux.org groups not yet read. Do not re-test sport, hobby or user-group club registers on any screen. |
| **GNU-style ChangeLog author lines, and the saturation screen that replaces address density (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 6.0 net-new post-split EE over 13 pairs from 141.4 MB, 0.0069 EE per registrable author domain, and closed for every per-contributor author population in any project or distribution.** What dates one item is real: the ISO date and the address are the same field (`2001-03-14  Name  <user@domain>`), so it escapes the mutable-column trap that killed MARC 856 and the NSF `piEmail` column, but it is human-typed and takes the corroboration split. Artifacts, 148,234,136 B pulled: 46 `download.gnome.org/sources/` module tarballs (99,742,459 B), 13 `ftp.gwdg.de/pub/linux/kde/Attic/2.2.2/src/` modules (47,115,650 B), 9 `archive.debian.org` `Sources.gz` over slink/potato/woody (1,376,027 B), plus `squidguard-1.2.0/CHANGELOG` already on disk. 837,232 ChangeLog lines yield 54,620 in-window author lines which collapse to **1,040 distinct email hosts and 871 registrable domains, a 52:1 collapse**. Priced with `price_items.py --all-tlds` against merged260827 sampling DISTINCT DOMAINS: 1,360 pairs, **1,310 already held (96.3%)**, pre-split 50 pairs / 25.1 EE (do not quote), **post-split 13 pairs / 6.0 EE**, mean weight 0.4589, typo bound 62.5%, 19 names new to the candidate pool. Per-arm: GNOME 49 modules 51,355 lines / 466 domains / 703 of 730 pairs held / 4.2 EE; GNOME restricted to 2001 20,215 lines / 205 domains / **193 held AT 2001 (94.1%)** / 2.2 EE; KDE 2.2.2 2,592 lines / 65 domains / 80 of 82 held / 0.2 EE at mean weight 0.1711, under the 0.4 floor; Debian maintainer and uploader fields over 10,431 source packages (slink 1,882, potato 2,964, woody 5,585) / 321 pairs / **320 held (99.7%)** / 0.7 EE. **The 2001 threshold runs BACKWARDS on this population**: P(store lacks Y | domain held) is 1996 0.394, 1997 0.298, 1998 0.217, 1999 0.120, 2000 0.037, **2001 0.031**, and `.com` at 2001 is **0.043 against the store-wide 0.611, 14x worse**. Positive control in the same code path, because nothing-found and pointed-wrong look identical: 200 `.com` names held at 2000 and missing 2001, written as synthetic author lines, ran through the SAME parser and pricer and returned 200/200 and **126.4 EE post-split**, so the whole 54,620-line corpus is worth less than ten such names and the pipeline was sound. **Method, and it is the result: the hypothesis's own cheap screen is the wrong screen, and it passes.** Address density is 7.8 email hosts per 1,000 lines on gnome-core, 8x the proposed floor, and the family still dies; the binding constraint is distinct-domain saturation, fitting **hosts = 6.87 x lines^0.406** (exponent measured twice independently at 0.414 and 0.399 over 3,565 / 12,183 / 51,355 lines giving 190 / 316 / 561 hosts). At 0.0069 EE per registrable domain, 1,000 EE needs ~145,000 author domains, and the fit puts 5,000 hosts alone at 11.3M author lines, 219x this corpus, while the entire Debian woody source archive has 310. **So screen an author corpus on distinct-domain saturation, never on address density.** The mechanism is the mirror image of the blocklist law: a blocklist pays because its held names LACK the year, while a contributor writes from a long-lived employer, university or ISP domain the store already carries in EVERY year. **Any corpus whose population is PEOPLE rather than SITES shows this inversion**, which reaches the mailing-list closure from a second independent direction. Three reusable operational notes: (1) **`download.gnome.org` mtimes are not release dates** (gtk+ re-stamped 2013-Feb-22), so pick tarballs by version series, and since ChangeLogs are cumulative one late tarball carries the whole 1996-2001 history at one fetch per module; (2) **one `Sources.gz` bounds a whole distribution's maintainer-domain universe in a single request**, the `ls-lR` trick applied to an author population; (3) `download.kde.org` is Mirrorbits and 302s with `link: rel=duplicate` headers naming every mirror, so read those headers to pick an allowed mirror rather than following the redirect blind. Robots read whole before the first request: **`www.winehq.org` disallows ClaudeBot** in its Cloudflare-managed block so Wine was never fetched; `mirrors.dotsrc.org` names Claude and was avoided; `download.gnome.org`, `download.kde.org` and `archive.debian.org` 404 on robots.txt; `ftp.gwdg.de` is empty `Disallow:`; `ftp.icm.edu.pl` disallows two paths, neither ours. web.archive.org was not touched. **Do not re-test GNU-style ChangeLogs, AUTHORS or CREDITS files, Debian maintainer or uploader fields, or any other per-contributor author population.** KDE also shows the convention is narrow: 13 KDE modules hold 23,447 ChangeLog lines against 813,785 for 49 GNOME modules, so outside the GNU orbit there is not even a corpus to price. |
| **Mailing-list catalogues (PAML, Liszt, tile.net, CataList), and a fourth screen: a SERVICE endpoint is the worst possible target under the 2001 threshold (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 11.2 net-new post-split EE over 16 pairs from 66,245,691 B of Usenet mbox; counterfactual re-dating of the same 2,151 hosts to the only year that pays gives 61.5 EE, 0.0286 EE per distinct host, and the largest artifact in the family (Liszt, ~90,000 lists) tops out at 228 EE against a 5,000 EE bar.** The hypothesis is right that a catalogue of LISTS enumerates mail HOSTS in bulk and wrong about what a mail host is worth. What dates one item is the `Date:` header of a PAML Usenet part, identical to `usenet_announce`, but **11,381 of 18,258 entries in the 1996 parts carry a per-entry `Last change: <month> 95`, so the posting date dates the CATALOGUE and not the entry.** Artifacts pulled and read offline: `archive.org/download/usenet-news/news.lists.mbox.zip` 29,483,704 B and `news.lists.misc.mbox.zip` 36,761,987 B. The 2001 web editions (`paml.net` 7,363 lists built 2001-05-02, `tile.net/lists` about 52,000, CataList 48,424 lists / 2,104 sites stamped `4 Jun 2001 04:00 -0400`) are all archived and all sit behind web.archive.org, which refused TCP for most of the session after about 15 replay fetches; `paml.net` is now a GoDaddy lander, `tile.net` redirects to ChrisKnight.com and `www.neosoft.com` 403s to riseinternet.com, so there is no live route. Priced 495 in-window PAML parts (1996: 235, 1997: 240; the 20 dated 2001 are 115-byte stubs, not editions) with `price_items.py --all-tlds`: 3,342 distinct pairs over **2,151 distinct domains, 3,233 already held (96.7%)**, pre-split 109 pairs / 73.9 EE, **post-split 16 pairs / 11.2 EE**, and the surviving TLDs are `dance`, `tv`, `baseball`, `recipes`, so the true figure is lower. Whole message bodies were fed, so owner addresses and `http://` homepage URLs are already inside that number and it is generous. The decisive test re-dated the same 2,151 hosts to 2001 and re-priced: **100 net-new post-split pairs, 61.5 EE**. One number explains it: of the 2,079 hosts the store holds, **1,979 already carry 2001, so only 4.81% are missing it** (`.com` alone 864 held, 59 missing, 6.83%; adjacent held-2000-missing-2001 5.86%). Proved against a known positive in the same code path and session: 2,058 random `.com` domains drawn from the store read **59.48% missing 2001** (adjacent 66.69%), reproducing the register's 0.611, so the query is pointed right and the negative is real. **Mailing-list mail hosts are 8.7x LESS likely to be missing 2001 than a random held `.com`.** Cohort check against the objection that 1996-97 hosts are unusually established: the 1997-new cohort reads 6.08% missing 2001 and 0.0368 EE per host against the 1996 cohort's 4.26% and 0.0252, same order, so a later edition changes nothing. Sizing the whole family from the concentration ratio: PAML prints one `Contact:` line per list and 19,175 entries in 1996 collapse to 1,328 distinct hosts, 22,269 in 1997 to 1,515, so **14.4 to 14.7 lists per host**, while CataList self-reports 48,424 lists across 2,104 sites, 23.0 to 1. Top hosts `netcom.com` 730, `world.std.com` 424, `xmission.com`, `fatcity.com`, `lists.best.com`. At the most favourable measured rate Liszt's 90,000 lists give ~6,200 hosts and **228 EE**, tile.net ~3,600 hosts and 132 EE, CataList's 2,104 sites 77 EE; even the impossible bound of one host per list puts Liszt at 3,312 EE, still under the bar, so **the family cannot reach it arithmetically**. tile.net separately fails on retrievability: browse pages print a bare list name and the host lives only behind `showlists.php?list_id=N` over ~52,000 ids (known positive `?domain=A` returns 200 and 485,867 B; the one `list_id` probed returned 404, which is one probe and not a rate, but 52,000 replay fetches is unaffordable regardless). **Method: counterfactual re-dating is the cheap way to price a corpus whose good edition is unreachable.** Take the host population from any edition you CAN get, rewrite every item's year to the target year and re-price; that isolates what the population is worth in the year that pays from what the particular artifact asserts, and it costs no network. Pair it with a control drawn from the store by the same code path, because a held-fraction that extreme is otherwise indistinguishable from a broken query. Second reusable rule: **ask for the artifact's own lists-per-host ratio before pricing any directory of SERVICES rather than SITES** (CataList prints its ratio on the front page and PAML's is recoverable from one edition; either would have killed this in one fetch). Third: when web.archive.org is refusing connections, check whether the artifact was ALSO posted to Usenet, since `archive.org/download` stayed up throughout while `web.archive.org` was dead. **The register gains a fourth screen alongside density and authority: a mail host, a name server or any other SERVICE endpoint is the worst possible target under the 2001 threshold, because the very durability that let it host other people's lists is what makes the store already hold it at 2001.** One unspent thread, not for this family: `news.answers.mbox.zip` (3,370,920,579 B) carries the 1998-2000 PAML editions missing from `news.lists`, and `rtfm.mit.edu`'s mirror does NOT contain PAML (only `mail/mailing-lists/providers`), so the two are not duplicates. |
| **Pay-per-click bid tables (GoTo/Overture and ten rivals), closed on what the crawler LINKED rather than on what the engine sold (2026-08-28)** | **CLOSED at 22.2 net-new post-split EE over 36 pairs from 22 captures, 0.083 EE per listed domain; GoTo itself has 0 of 70 crawler-seen query URLs in window, and everything still reachable in the family caps under 50 EE.** What dates one item is clean: the Wayback capture instant of a pay-per-click result page asserts the advertiser is buying that keyword at that moment, so it is a live-site observation and machine-generated with no human-typed split; the page carries no date of its own, so nothing dates an item without the capture. **The named artifact does not exist in window** - no GoTo or Overture bid page has a 1996-2001 capture, and `inventory.overture.com` is 2002. The surviving sibling is `partner.ah-ha.com/1000.asp?query=<kw>`, 19 captures at 2000-12-12, 2001-04-13 and 2001-06-03 (~33 KB each; `web/20010603133655id_/` for `query=tickets` is 32,986 B), plus 3 `www.bay9.com/?page=<country>` captures at 2001-04-04. Rank and bid price sit in each row's redirector (`tools/redirect_nk.asp?...&bid=0%2E05&...&r=18`) and the destination domain is plain text, so it is a genuine bid table, exactly the shape proposed. 70 query-string URLs were lifted verbatim from two captured goto.com pages (20001210082400, 20010830140743), so they are URLs the crawler itself saw: 67 have no capture and 3 are captured only at 2004-2005. 8 guessed peek and bidtool URLs return nothing. Pointed-wrong is excluded by four positive controls answering in the same batches (`www.isi.edu/in-notes/us-domain-delegated.txt` 20010606153725, `www.goto.com/d/about/advertisers/` 19990221101215, `www.goto.com/d/search/` 20001210082400, `www.goto.com/` 20010830140743), and the oracle is exact-match not fuzzy: the same URL with two fabricated `;$sessionid$` tokens returns nothing while the real token returns 20040528190918. **The family is alive on the second-tier engines and dead only on the market leader**: 10 of 11 PPC engines have 2001 homepage captures (7search.com none), and of 45 crawler-seen query URLs ah-ha is 19/20, kanoodle 8/15, bay9 4.
